# Changelog

## 2026-07-20

- Repo created at `~/Desktop/P-Corp`, separate from the trading robot repo, per Joshua's decision.
- Founder Brief received and stored as `FOUNDER_BRIEF.md` (authoritative, v1.0).
- Lean documentation skeleton created: `README.md`, `MASTER_SPEC.md`, `ENGINEERING_MANUAL.md`, `ARCHITECTURE.md`, `ROADMAP.md`, `TECH_STACK.md`, `UI_GUIDELINES.md`, `PERSONALITY_SPEC.md`, `MEMORY_SYSTEM.md`, `WAR_ROOM.md`, `ALPHA_MODE.md`, `TRADING_DIVISION.md`, `SECURITY.md`.
- Scope deliberately limited to what the Founder Brief actually decided, plus explicit open questions — no speculative depth, no tech stack chosen, no production code. Rationale: trading robot remains the active priority (Phase 0, see `ROADMAP.md`).
- **Decided (`TECH_STACK.md`): platform scope is Apple-only (macOS desktop, iOS companion); desktop UI shell is native SwiftUI**, chosen over Electron and Tauri for native feel and direct code/pattern reuse with the iOS companion. Frank's intelligence layer will run as a separate local service (language still open) talking to the SwiftUI shell over local IPC — the UI framework choice does not lock in Frank's backend language.
- **Decided (`TECH_STACK.md`): Frank's backend runs in Python**, via the Claude Agent SDK, communicating with the SwiftUI shell over local IPC. Chosen primarily because Trading Division is the flagged likely first specialized agent and the existing trading-robot repo's analysis tooling is already Python (135 `.py` files vs. 19 `.mq5` files, confirmed today) — direct reuse over a second implementation.
- **Decided (`TECH_STACK.md`): IPC mechanism is local HTTP + WebSocket (FastAPI), bound to `127.0.0.1` with a local auth token** — REST for simple calls, WebSocket for streaming responses and unprompted push notifications. Chosen over XPC/Unix sockets (both local-only, would need rebuilding for mobile/remote sync) and gRPC (protobuf schema overhead across Swift/Python not justified at this scale), specifically because the same interface can later be exposed for remote/mobile access behind real auth and TLS rather than requiring a separate integration.
- **Decided (`TECH_STACK.md`): process lifecycle target is a persistent background service managed via `SMAppService`**, not a subprocess tied to the app's lifetime — because Frank's "second brain" framing and Layer 1's Notifications responsibility require Frank to exist and act even when the UI is closed. Early prototyping may still use a simple spawned subprocess for dev-iteration speed; graduating to `SMAppService` is a sequencing detail, not a change of target.
- **Decided (`TECH_STACK.md`): packaging is an embedded, relocatable Python runtime + locked dependencies as bundled resources, launched via a thin native shim executable** — not a PyInstaller/py2app frozen single-binary. Chosen because per-dylib notarization signing gets increasingly fragile under frozen-binary tooling as dependencies grow (numpy/pandas for Trading Division, etc.), and owning the embedding directly avoids inheriting that fragility over a decade-long horizon.
- **Decided (`TECH_STACK.md`): database is SQLite, embedded locally in the Python backend**, for tasks, projects, conversations, and memory records, with a semantic/vector search layer on top for contextual recall rather than a dedicated vector DB as the primary store. Chosen over Postgres specifically to avoid pre-deciding the still-open self-hosted-vs-cloud sync question — SQLite works local-first today and doesn't foreclose a sync layer (e.g. libSQL/Turso) being added later without a storage migration.
- **Decided (`TECH_STACK.md`): sync is libSQL/`sqld`, starting on Turso's managed offering, with self-hosting `sqld` kept as an explicit reversible future option** — not a hard self-hosted-vs-managed fork today. Chosen over a Mac-as-server/Tailscale-tunnel model (Frank would go dark whenever the Mac is closed or offline) and over committing to full self-hosting now (real ops/security burden competing with the trading robot and Alpha Mode for founder time). Because `sqld` is open source and Turso speaks the same protocol as a self-hosted instance, "who hosts it" can be revisited later without a data-model or protocol rewrite. Flags an encryption-at-rest requirement for the most sensitive memory categories (marriage, family, health) as a `SECURITY.md` follow-up.
- **Decided (`TECH_STACK.md`): auth is self-issued per-device keypairs in Keychain/Secure Enclave, with QR-code device pairing** — no third-party identity provider. Chosen because P Corp OS has exactly one user ever, so the real problem is device trust for a handful of Joshua's own devices, not multi-tenant user management; a managed auth provider would put an external company in the critical path of accessing his own second brain. Reuses the Apple platform bet already made and subsumes the existing local loopback auth token into one mechanism. **This closes out the core `TECH_STACK.md` decision set** — only iOS companion specifics remain open, and can wait until real iOS work begins.
- **`ENGINEERING_MANUAL.md` written for real** (status: Active), now that the stack is decided. Repo layout is a monorepo (SwiftUI app + Python backend + docs, one repo), with the option to split out individual agents later if warranted. Establishes a code review policy that applies identically to human-written, Claude-assisted, and future agent-authored code — no exceptions — as an enforced process, not just a stated intention, extending FOUNDER_BRIEF.md's own Working Rules. Also sets: trunk-based git workflow, per-language tooling (XCTest/swift-format for Swift; pytest/ruff/uv for Python), integration tests across the IPC boundary, and documentation-stays-in-sync discipline matching what this repo has done from the start.
- **Phase 0 (Foundations) marked complete; Phase 1 (the interview) begins** — `ROADMAP.md` and `README.md` updated accordingly. Noted an open thread for this phase: interview content touching sensitive categories (marriage, health, finances) won't be committed to this repo verbatim until Joshua and Frank have explicitly agreed on how that gets recorded, given `MEMORY_SYSTEM.md`/`SECURITY.md` already flagged that this category needs privacy handling (e.g. encryption-at-rest) that doesn't exist yet.

## 2026-07-21

- **`PERSONALITY_SPEC.md` written for real** (status: Active), consolidated from two Phase 1 interview sessions (2026-07-20, 2026-07-21). Covers: interaction style (direct, plus an explicit mandate to proactively name Joshua's own patterns even unprompted and unwelcome), the stakes/reversibility/who-else-affected delegation test, strengths (operator, thinker, hands-on learner), behavioral patterns to watch for (holds onto control longer than justified, copes with difficulty by enduring it alone, may not self-report approaching capacity limits, money decisions run without a system), a two-dial risk-tolerance model (high for calculated risk, low for relational/control risk), values (legacy, freedom, family, proof of building something real), business philosophy, and leadership style. Interview itself continues — this spec is the current best model, not a final version, per FOUNDER_BRIEF.md's own instruction that it never really finishes.
- **`PERSONALITY_SPEC.md` updated: feedback calibration resolved.** When Frank surfaces something Joshua doesn't want to hear, be solution-driven — pair the observation with an actual next step, not bare criticism or praise alone. Confirmed he takes feedback well "as long as it's helpful too," with real precedent (Nick/Raoof) of this landing before. Moved from open question to confirmed guidance; the only remaining open item in this area is frequency/threshold for proactively surfacing something, which the doc now notes should be learned through use rather than decided up front.

## 2026-07-23

- **First production code written: the desktop UI shell.** Joshua explicitly requested starting Phase 2 (`ROADMAP.md`), specifically the desktop visual shell, following his own phased UI build-out plan (shell first, no intelligence; interactivity, Frank, voice, memory, and live data layered in over later phases). Built as a Swift Package Manager executable at `desktop/` (`Package.swift`, `Sources/PCorpOS/`) rather than a full Xcode project, since only command-line developer tools are installed, not Xcode itself — SPM + SwiftUI's native `App` lifecycle builds and runs via `swift run` without needing Xcode. Scope matches his stated Phase 1 exactly: static sidebar nav, War Room center (greeting, a restrained non-mascot stand-in for Frank's presence, an unwired input bar), and a right rail (Mission Status, Today's Agenda, Frank's Insights, Quick Actions) — all hardcoded placeholder content, no backend calls, no wired-up button actions. Verified it builds (`swift build`) and runs (`swift run`) without crashing; visual verification is Joshua's to do, since Claude can compile-check SwiftUI but can't render or screenshot a native macOS window the way it can a web page.
- `ENGINEERING_MANUAL.md` and `ROADMAP.md` updated to reflect the actual repo structure now that a first prototype exists — `desktop/` for the SwiftUI shell, no `backend/` yet.
- **Fixed a real bug from first look:** sidebar text was rendering near-invisible because semantic colors (`.primary`/`.secondary`) were adapting to the system's dark mode, while the design itself is a fixed light aesthetic. Forced `.preferredColorScheme(.light)` and moved to explicit colors in the sidebar. Also removed the hard divider lines between layout columns per Joshua's direct feedback, relying on background-tone contrast instead (matches how the reference mockup actually separates regions).
- **Frank's orb redesigned twice more from direct feedback:** first pass added an organic `BlobShape` (not a circle) with a floating animation; second pass smoothed it further (16 points, gentler variance) for a liquid rather than faceted look, added a radial gradient with a tight specular highlight and rim light for real volume, and made the floating motion and contact shadow more pronounced.
- Refined sidebar icons for clearer matches to their labels and gave them explicit muted tinting instead of relying on system foreground colors.
- **`UI_GUIDELINES.md` written for real** (status: Active) from decisions made while building the shell: the visual language (light-only for now, no visible seams, blob/liquid presence for Frank), a confirmed copy-tone principle (military-style language — "Missions" not "Tasks," "War Room," "Mission Status" — kept easy to understand, independent of the clean visual design), and the on-screen naming convention ("Joshx" in text). `PERSONALITY_SPEC.md` updated with the corresponding spoken-address convention ("Josh," once Frank actually talks).
- Copy changes in the shell: "Good morning, Joshua" → "Good morning, Joshx"; "+ New Task" → "+ Mission"; the matching Quick Actions entry → "New Mission."

## 2026-07-24

A long, iterative design-and-build session on the desktop shell, plus the first backend code. Full rationale for each decision lives in `UI_GUIDELINES.md` and `ENGINEERING_MANUAL.md`; this entry is the summary.

- **Real dark mode** — `Theme.swift`'s `AppTheme` (light/dark palettes), toggled from Settings, backed by `@AppStorage`, driving `.preferredColorScheme` explicitly. Every view themed, not hardcoded colors.
- **Frank's presence rebuilt twice more:** a liquid `BlobShape` → per direct feedback, a real particle cluster (`TimelineView` + `Canvas`, deterministic seeded positions, 120 → 260 → 450 particles as feedback asked for "more" and "bolder"), sized up, made visible in dark mode via theme-aware color (a deliberate exception to "brand elements don't get themed").
- **The P logo mark resolved** after three failed hand-built vector-path attempts — extracted from Joshua's actual reference image via real pixel processing (luminance thresholding, not guessed coordinates). Same technique used for the Alpha Mode Media sidebar icon (found in his existing brand assets) and a hand-particle effect (`HandParticles.swift`, built but parked, not in the current layout).
- **Real interactivity throughout:** hover states, a sliding sidebar-selection indicator, Quick Actions and Frank's Insights wired to honest navigation (not no-ops), a real input focus state, glass/material finishes (`.ultraThinMaterial`/`.regularMaterial`), a launch fade-in, a redesigned "not built yet" placeholder for the 9 unbuilt sections (still honest, not bare).
- **Keyboard shortcuts (Cmd+1-9, Cmd+,)** — first attempt (SwiftUI `.keyboardShortcut` buttons) silently didn't work; root-caused to a real platform quirk (raw `swift build` executables don't get normal key-window status automatically) and fixed properly — documented in `ENGINEERING_MANUAL.md` since it'll resurface elsewhere until this ships as a real `.app`.
- **App icon** — white P mark on a black rounded-square, full macOS icon size set compiled via `iconutil` into `AppIcon.icns`, set at runtime (same activation-policy fix required before it actually appeared in the Dock).
- **First Python backend code** (`backend/`) — a `uv`-managed FastAPI service proving the IPC contract from `TECH_STACK.md`: `/health` and a `/ws` WebSocket that streams echoed responses back. Deliberately echo-first, not real Claude reasoning yet (explicit scoping decision). The SwiftUI "Talk to Frank" input is now genuinely wired to it — the first real end-to-end round trip in the project, verified independently on both sides before being connected.
- **Backend upgraded from echo to real Claude reasoning, same day.** Attempted the Claude Agent SDK first, per `TECH_STACK.md`'s original plan; discovered it requires installing the actual Claude Code CLI as a subprocess dependency and brings full tool-use (file access, bash) with it — confirmed via `CLINotFoundError` and the missing CLI binary, not assumed. `SECURITY.md`'s agent-sandboxing/permission model doesn't exist yet, so asked Joshua directly rather than picking a workaround; he chose the plain `anthropic` SDK, deferring the Agent SDK until that permission model is real. `TECH_STACK.md` updated to record this as a sequencing decision, not a reversal. Implemented: `AsyncAnthropic().messages.stream()` for real token streaming, and a first real system prompt (`backend/app/personality.py`) distilled directly from `PERSONALITY_SPEC.md`. API key handling via `backend/.env` (gitignored) copied from a committed `backend/.env.example` — deliberately never asked Joshua to paste a key into chat. End-to-end testing is deferred: Joshua asked to add a real key and verify next week; the code is ready and waiting.
- **Conversation persistence added** (`backend/app/db.py`) — SQLite via `aiosqlite` (async, non-blocking). Confirmed decision, asked directly rather than assumed: one continuous conversation, not multiple threads, matching "there is only ever one Frank" — so just a `messages` table, no `conversations` table at all. On WebSocket connect, history loads from disk and continues rather than starting fresh. Verified persistence survives a completely separate process invocation, not just in-memory state within one run. Deliberately not here yet: typed memory records (facts/preferences, per `MEMORY_SYSTEM.md`'s user/feedback/project/reference scheme), semantic/vector search, and context-window management for a very long-running conversation.
- **Real Claude integration verified end-to-end** — Joshua added a real Anthropic API key to `backend/.env` sooner than the "next week" originally planned. Backend tested standalone first (health check, a real WebSocket message, confirmed persistence across a fresh connection recalling prior context), then the full Swift round trip: launched the actual desktop app, sent a message through the real "Talk to Frank" input, got a genuine streamed Claude response back in Frank's voice. **First real conversation with Frank in the project's history.**
- **Typed memory records** (`backend/app/db.py`, new `backend/app/memory.py`) — the first real layer of `MEMORY_SYSTEM.md`, separate from raw conversation history. Reuses the existing Claude Code memory scheme's four types (user/feedback/project/reference), asked Joshua directly rather than assumed. **Frank's first tool call**: a single hardcoded `save_memory` tool via the plain `anthropic` SDK's tool-use — explicitly not the deferred Agent SDK (no file/shell access, one fixed action), confirmed with Joshua as a distinct, smaller-risk decision. Every record loads into the system prompt each turn (no ranking/filtering yet — correct-enough at current scale). Verified end-to-end: asked Frank to remember a project fact, confirmed it actually wrote to `memory_records` (not just claimed to in text), then opened a fresh connection and confirmed Frank recalled it unprompted. Deliberately deferred: semantic search, forgetting/versioning, cross-device sync. `MEMORY_SYSTEM.md` updated from stub to reflect all of the above as decided, not open.
- **Memory made visible in the shell** — a new `GET /memory` read-only endpoint (`backend/app/main.py`) and a real "Frank" nav section (`desktop/Sources/PCorpOS/FrankView.swift`, `MemoryClient.swift`), replacing that section's "not built yet" placeholder. Before this, a saved memory only existed as a row Frank wrote to SQLite — no way to actually see it without a raw DB query. Records display grouped by type with a colored badge, newest first. Read-only by design: creation still happens exclusively through Frank's own `save_memory` tool, not a manual form. Verified with real data: seeded two memories through an actual conversation, confirmed both appeared correctly in the running app, cleared before committing.
- **Priority check-in, then `SECURITY.md` written for real.** Flagged directly that several sessions of P Corp OS work had accumulated despite `FOUNDER_BRIEF.md`'s own "Current Priority" naming the trading robot as the real focus — Joshua confirmed the trading robot stays priority, P Corp OS work paused (not abandoned). Later resumed explicitly at Joshua's request; asked which Layer 1 gap to build next (auth, cloud sync, or `SECURITY.md`), he chose `SECURITY.md`, since it's the gate already blocking general tool-use and the eventual Trading Division integration. Threat model scoped to what genuinely exists today (one machine, no cloud sync, a loopback-only backend with zero auth) rather than the full future vision. Permission model: a three-tier tool classification (regular / needs-confirmation / prohibited) deliberately borrowed from the same framework that's governed every action Claude has taken across this whole project — `save_memory` classified as "regular" (`backend/app/memory.py` updated to note this). Document-only this pass: the local-auth-token gap it surfaced (no auth at all on the backend today) is real but deliberately left as a separate, explicitly flagged next step.

## 2026-07-25

- **Local-auth-token check implemented** (`backend/app/auth.py`) — `SECURITY.md`'s flagged next step, closing the "any local process can connect" gap. A machine-generated shared secret (`backend/data/auth_token`, gitignored, not something Joshua configures), checked via a FastAPI dependency on every REST route and a query-param check before the WebSocket accepts. Desktop side (`AuthToken.swift`) reads the same file, appends it to every request. Verified both failure paths (no token, wrong token) are rejected before touching anything else, then confirmed the real app still worked correctly with it in place.
- **A real chat thread in War Room, finally.** Before this, the UI only ever showed the single most recent reply as one line, even though the backend has persisted full conversation history since day one — genuinely invisible until this. `BackendClient.swift` rearchitected to own a real `[ChatMessage]` list instead of one repeatedly-overwritten string; a new `GET /history` feeds it on connect. Also fixed a real rough edge surfaced while rebuilding this: the backend's own `"\n[done]"` end-of-turn sentinel had been silently getting appended into the visible reply text this whole time — now recognized and suppressed, exposed instead as a proper `isStreaming` flag.
- **Conversation sessions — reopening the earlier "one continuous conversation, no conversations table" decision, based on real usage.** Once Joshua actually used the chat thread, he wanted to start fresh ones ("let's also be able to start new chats"), then asked where to find old ones once he had one. Not a reversal: durable memory (`memory_records`), not the raw transcript, is what actually makes "there is only ever one Frank" true — so the transcript itself can now start, list, and switch like in any normal chat app. New `conversations` + `app_state` tables (the latter because "active = newest" breaks the moment you want to reopen an older conversation without it needing to be the newest row), a new-chat button, and a conversation-switcher popover (`GET /conversations` with a preview snippet + message count, `POST /conversations/{id}/activate`). Migration handled directly rather than by wiping: existing messages backfilled to conversation #1, verified the real prior conversation survived the schema change intact.
- **Two real bugs fixed from actually using the above, same day.** The top bar's "+ Mission" button started visually compressing once two more icon buttons (new chat, conversation history) crowded the row — it has no fixed frame unlike its neighbors, so it was the thing that gave way instead of the `Spacer()`s; fixed with `.fixedSize()`. The conversation switcher listed every conversation newest-first, including empty ones from clicking "new chat" without sending anything — those buried the one real conversation, reported directly ("i couldnt find the previous conversation too"); `list_conversations()` now excludes conversations with zero messages.
- **The time-of-day greeting going stale, fixed.** `timeOfDayGreeting` read `.now` as a plain computed property, so it only recomputed when something else caused SwiftUI to re-render the view — an idle window kept showing "Good morning" into the afternoon, reported directly. `WarRoomView`'s body now runs inside a `TimelineView(.periodic(from: .now, by: 60))`, so it ticks on its own regardless of any other app activity.
- **Real macOS notifications** — closes Layer 1's "Notifications" responsibility from `FOUNDER_BRIEF.md`; `TECH_STACK.md` had already decided the mechanism (the WebSocket carries unprompted pushes from Frank), this implements it. `UNUserNotificationCenter` hard-crashed on this still-unbundled app (`bundleProxyForCurrentProcess is nil`, confirmed with a standalone test) — fell back to shelling out to `osascript` (`SystemNotification.swift`), which doesn't need the calling process to have a real bundle identity. Wire format: a `"\n[notify]{json}"` sentinel over the existing WebSocket, same pattern as the existing `"\n[done]"` marker. Triggered by `save_memory` firing, not a new "when should Frank notify" policy. Verified fully live: Frank correctly asked for confirmation before saving a name that didn't match what was on file (an unprompted, genuine "never assume" moment, not staged), confirmed it, a real notification appeared. Also surfaced a real process gap: running test scripts directly against the live backend had silently shifted which conversation was active — nothing was lost (verified directly), but it's a reminder that testing against the same database real usage lives in carries real risk.
- **Forgetting/versioning for memory records** — closes `MEMORY_SYSTEM.md`'s flagged gap from the start; `memory_records` only ever accumulated, no update or delete existed at all. Soft-delete (`deleted_at`), not a real DELETE — reversible in principle, matching `SECURITY.md`'s "regular" tier reasoning for both memory tools, and gives a rudimentary audit trail for free (a forgotten row's content still exists, just excluded from what Frank sees and what the UI shows). "Versioning" is deliberately forget-then-resave, not a version-history schema. Two paths, one mechanism: Frank's new `forget_memory` tool (matches by title, since row IDs are never surfaced to him) and a manual hover-revealed trash icon in `FrankView`, both sending the existing `[notify]` sentinel so forgetting is as visible as saving. Verified end-to-end: saved and forgot a disposable test memory through a real conversation, confirmed via the raw DB that the row survives with `deleted_at` set rather than being destroyed, tested the REST delete endpoint directly, then confirmed live — Joshua used the actual trash icon to forget a real memory himself.

## 2026-07-26

- **The Calendar section built, with real macOS Calendar data.** First of the 8 remaining placeholder nav sections to get built out — picked deliberately over the other 7 (the rest need external data sources, touch unresolved sensitivity questions, or are UI for Phase 3 capabilities that don't exist yet). Tried `EventKit` first; it silently denied access with no permission dialog ever appearing — same root cause as the earlier `UNUserNotificationCenter` crash (still no real app bundle), confirmed with a standalone test before building anything on it. Fell back to AppleScript via `osascript` (`SystemCalendar.swift`), same pattern already used for notifications. Real gotcha hit and fixed along the way: AppleScript's default number-to-string coercion for Unix epoch seconds uses the system locale's decimal separator and scientific notation (produced `"1,7851464E+9"`, confirmed directly, which silently fails to parse as a Swift `Double`) — switched to passing small year/month/day/hour/minute components instead, sidestepping the formatting problem entirely. `CalendarView.swift` replaces that section's placeholder with real upcoming events grouped by day; the War Room right rail's "Today's Agenda" card is real now too (the old hardcoded `PlaceholderData.agenda` and its `AgendaItem` struct removed entirely, not left as dead code). Verified against real calendar data throughout, including live in the running app.
- **The Knowledge section built, as a browser for this project's own docs.** Second placeholder section built out. Deliberately not a generic file browser — a real doc viewer for `FOUNDER_BRIEF.md`, `ROADMAP.md`, `TECH_STACK.md`, and the rest of this project's own foundational markdown files (`KnowledgeDocs.swift`), since Frank's own knowledge starts with the documents that define him, and they're already real content requiring no external system. Read directly off disk, no backend round trip. `SimpleMarkdownView` is a small hand-rolled renderer, not a third-party markdown package — scoped to exactly what these docs use (headings, bullet/numbered lists, horizontal rules, inline bold/italic/links), which is enough to make them genuinely readable without a dependency this project doesn't otherwise need.
- **`SMAppService` packaging, stage 1: a real `.app` bundle.** None of the remaining 6 placeholder sections were as ready as Calendar/Knowledge had been, so switched to this instead, per `TECH_STACK.md`'s already-scoped 3-stage plan. `desktop/build_app.sh` assembles and ad-hoc signs a real bundle from the SPM executable — no Xcode installed, so this is a from-scratch shell script, not an Xcode target. Three real problems hit and fixed, each confirmed directly rather than guessed around: (1) `codesign` refuses to sign the bundle while SPM's own generated resource bundle sits anywhere under `Contents/` — traced to its generated accessor resolving via `Bundle.main.bundleURL`, which for a real `.app` is the bundle's own root, and `codesign` separately rejects anything there besides `Contents/`; fixed by copying real resource files into `Contents/Resources/` instead (the standard location) and adding `AppResources.swift`, which checks there first and falls back to `Bundle.module` only for `swift run` dev mode — updated at all 4 call sites (P logo, Alpha Mode logo, both app-icon locations). (2) stray resource-fork/Finder-info extended attributes on source artwork, stripped via `xattr -cr` before signing. (3) the more consequential one, found by actually launching the bundle the normal way (`open`, not `swift run`): its working directory is `/`, confirmed via `lsof`, not `desktop/` — every cwd-relative path (the auth token, the Knowledge docs) silently broke. Fixed at the root with `ProjectPaths.swift`, deriving the repo location from `#filePath` (fixed at compile time) instead of runtime cwd. Also confirmed directly: a real signed bundle gets normal Dock/activation behavior automatically from macOS — the manual activation-policy workaround built for the raw executable is provably unnecessary for this launch path now, though left in place since `swift run` remains the everyday dev workflow and still needs it. Verified live: Joshua confirmed the Dock icon appeared immediately and every section still works through the real bundle. Stages 2 (embed Python + a native shim) and 3 (`SMAppService` registration) are separate, not started.

## 2026-07-28

- **`SMAppService` packaging, stage 2: the Python runtime is embedded.** `desktop/build_app.sh` now downloads a relocatable CPython 3.12.13 build (python-build-standalone, cached locally after first run), installs the exact locked backend dependencies into it via `uv export` + pip (only re-installed when `backend/uv.lock`'s hash changes), compiles a thin native C shim (`backend_shim.c`, per `TECH_STACK.md`'s own "Swift/C shim" framing), and embeds both into the real bundle. The actual point: the backend no longer depends on this dev machine's own `uv`-managed venv or system Python at all. Deliberate scope decision: the shim's `PYTHONPATH` still points at the real repo's `backend/` source, not a bundled copy — `db.py`'s SQLite path is relative to its own file location, so a bundled copy would silently fork Frank's memory into a second database depending on which way he's launched, breaking "there is only ever one Frank"; full self-contained source is a real, later decision for actual distribution, not assumed here. Real bug found and fixed the same way stage 1's cwd bug was: `load_dotenv()` searches from the process's working directory by default, not the file's own location — since the shim can launch from anywhere, this silently left the API key unset the first time a real conversation was attempted through it; fixed by deriving the `.env` path from `__file__` instead. Verified with escalating real tests: the extracted runtime survives being moved to a different path (genuinely relocatable), the real backend module imports and serves `/health` standalone, the compiled shim resolves paths correctly in an isolated test bundle, then — running for real from inside the actual signed `.app` — a genuine live Claude conversation end-to-end through the whole embedded stack. Test conversation cleaned up afterward, same discipline as always; the real conversation the shim's test had displaced back to active. Stage 3 (registering the shim via `SMAppService` as a persistent LaunchAgent) is separate, not started.
- **`SMAppService` packaging, stage 3: Frank is a real persistent background service. This closes out `TECH_STACK.md`'s full 3-stage packaging plan.** `BackendService.swift` wraps `SMAppService.agent(plistName:)` (register/unregister/status), wired to the existing "Launch at Login" toggle in Settings — previously a stubbed `@State` that did nothing, now real, reading actual `SMAppService.status` on appear rather than a separately persisted preference (since the user could disable this directly from System Settings' own Login Items pane, outside the app entirely, and the toggle should reflect real state, not drift from it). `build_app.sh` writes the `Contents/Library/LaunchAgents/<label>.plist` the API requires, with `RunAtLoad` + `KeepAlive`. Real bug found via the actual unified system log — `/usr/bin/log`, not the shell-builtin `log` that was silently shadowing it and swallowing predicate arguments: the LaunchAgent registered and was approved cleanly (`sfltool dumpbtm` confirmed `disposition: enabled, allowed, notified` — approval was never the issue), but never actually started; `launchd`'s `posix_spawn` was failing with ENOENT because `ProgramArguments[0]` was just the bare executable name, and `SMAppService`/BTM does not rewrite that to be bundle-relative before handing it to `launchd`, contrary to what the API's own framing implied — plain POSIX exec semantics apply, and a bare name with no slash means a PATH search that fails since it's not on `PATH`. A real runaway respawn loop (retrying every ~10s against the broken config) was caught and stopped via `launchctl bootout` before it caused any problem, rather than left grinding in the background. Fixed with the executable's real absolute path, computed at build time. Verified thoroughly, not just "it registered": killed the backend process directly and confirmed `launchd`/`KeepAlive` restarted it automatically within seconds; quit the GUI app entirely and confirmed the backend kept serving real requests regardless — genuine independence, the actual point of the whole 3-stage effort.
- **Push-to-talk voice input, with a real audio-reactive blob.** The original ask, finally scoped and built properly, per the earlier deferral. Confirmed decision: push-to-talk, not continuous/always-listening — mic only active while deliberately triggered. `VoiceInput.swift` uses `Speech` (`SFSpeechRecognizer`) + `AVAudioEngine` directly — privacy-gated frameworks in the same category as `EventKit`, which failed outright on the old unbundled executable; worth trying now specifically because `SMAppService` packaging gives the app a real signed bundle, the actual prerequisite. `FrankOrb` now takes a real, optional live audio level (mic RMS, blended with the existing synthetic shimmer so the cluster still reads as organic motion, not a flat pulse) and reappears specifically while listening, regardless of whether a text thread is active — directly closing the original ask ("particle must disappear when texting, must be there and voice reactive when talking"). Real bug found through actual live testing: the first version's `stop()` called `task?.cancel()` immediately, killing the recognition task before it delivered its final, most-accurate transcript — confirmed directly: a real full sentence collapsed to "And it's so." Fixed by separating "stop capturing new audio" from "deliver the transcript" (the task's own `isFinal` callback, asynchronous), with a safety timeout for the case it never arrives. Retested with a complete sentence and confirmed it now comes through clean. Also fixed in passing: `VoiceInput.errorMessage` was tracked but never actually shown anywhere — a real failure would have looked identical to silence; now surfaced as its own state in `WarRoomView`. Text-to-speech (Frank's replies spoken aloud) is a separate, later stage — this is voice input only.
- **Full searchable, date-grouped conversation history.** Direct response to Joshua wanting Frank to remember every chat indefinitely and be able to reopen any of them. Retention was already indefinite (confirmed by inspecting `db.py` for expiry logic — there is none), so the real gap was findability, not persistence. `list_conversations` (`backend/app/db.py`) now computes `last_message_at` per conversation via `MAX(messages.created_at)` and sorts by that instead of creation order, and takes an optional `q` param doing a real SQL `LIKE` search across every message's actual content, not just the first-message preview — exposed as `GET /conversations?q=`. `ConversationListPopover` (`WarRoomView.swift`) rewritten in place with a debounced (300ms) search field and results grouped into day sections ("Today", "Yesterday", "Sunday, Jul 26", ...) via `Calendar.isDateInToday`/`isDateInYesterday`. Real bug hit along the way: the persistent `SMAppService`-managed backend doesn't hot-reload Python source edits, so it kept serving the old `list_conversations` (no `last_message_at`, `q` ignored) until forced to restart via `launchctl kickstart -k` — a new operational wrinkle now that Frank runs as a real background service rather than something manually relaunched on every code change. Verified live: searching "Joshx" correctly isolated the one matching conversation among several, with correct day-grouped labels shown.
- **Text-to-speech for Frank's replies.** The other half of "talking to Frank," direct feedback after trying voice input. `VoiceOutput.swift` uses on-device `AVSpeechSynthesizer`, deliberately via `write(_:toBufferCallback:)` rather than the simpler auto-playing `speak(_:)` API — `write` hands back real PCM buffers instead of playing them itself, so a small `AVAudioEngine` graph (`AVAudioPlayerNode` scheduling each buffer) does the actual playback, letting the same buffers get RMS-analyzed for `FrankOrb`'s shimmer. This is exactly the "shimmer driven by real audio once Frank has an actual voice" requirement `UI_GUIDELINES.md` had flagged as tracked-but-unbuildable since the very first version of the orb. Only replies to voice-triggered turns are spoken (typed replies stay silent); starting a new push-to-talk recording interrupts playback immediately (a `generation` counter discards stale in-flight buffers from an interrupted utterance rather than letting them resurrect audio after the fact). Real bug found immediately on first live use: voice selection ranked by quality first, gender never checked at all, so it silently picked Samantha (female) — checked what's actually installed on this Mac directly rather than guessing (41 English voices, all default quality, none Enhanced/Premium) and fixed the fallback to filter `gender == .male` first, then rank by quality within that. Confirmed live after Joshua downloaded a real Enhanced/Premium male voice through System Settings' Read & Speak pane (renamed from "Spoken Content" in recent macOS) — the actual fix for "too robotic," since no default-quality voice was ever going to sound natural regardless of which one got picked.
- **Push-to-talk auto-stops on silence — no second click needed.** Direct feedback the same day voice output shipped: requiring a click to end every turn felt like friction once the click-to-start privacy boundary already existed. `VoiceInput.swift` now tracks real mic RMS per audio buffer (reusing the meter already built for the orb) and auto-stops once actual speech has been detected and then gone quiet for ~1.2 seconds — `hasDetectedSpeech` gates this so it can never fire during the silence between clicking the mic and actually starting to talk. A 60-second safety cap covers sustained background noise never cleanly read as silence. Manual click-to-stop still works as an early exit. Thresholds are a best-effort starting point (no way to capture real mic recordings outside a live session to tune against) — confirmed working on first live try, no retuning needed yet.
- **A brand-new conversation starts automatically once per app launch.** Direct feedback: "I don't want chats to just stay there" — old conversations no longer resume automatically; they're reached exclusively through the search/date-grouped history browser shipped earlier the same day. Implemented in `BackendClient.swift` as a `static`, not instance, flag (`hasStartedFreshThisLaunch`) specifically because `ContentView`'s `.id(selectedItem.id)` recreates `WarRoomView` — and the `BackendClient` inside it — every time you navigate to a different sidebar section and back; an instance-level flag would have wiped an in-progress conversation just from clicking to Settings and back, which isn't what "always start fresh" meant once actually scoped out loud. Confirmed via `AskUserQuestion` before implementing, given how easy it would have been to get this scope wrong silently.

Tried ElevenLabs first; its free tier turned out to block API access to Voice Library voices entirely ("Free users cannot use library voices via the API"), which didn't fit the "forever free, no payment" ask — built `app/piper_tts.py` instead, a fully local, MIT-licensed, no-account TTS engine running on the same embedded backend. Real bug found via direct timing: a fresh `httpx.AsyncClient` was opened per request, paying a full DNS+TLS handshake every call (confirmed: the identical request took 4.9s cold vs 1.1s warm) — fixed with one client opened in the app's `lifespan` and reused for its whole run.
- **Auto-stop threshold fixed from real logged data, not another guess.** The original 0.015 threshold was never actually measured — real speech on Joshua's mic peaked at only ~0.005 RMS, so `hasDetectedSpeech` never became true and auto-stop silently never fired, matching his report ("it never stopped on its own"). Real debugging detour: `print()` debug logging never once showed up, because this GUI app's stdout goes to `/dev/null` once fully activated (confirmed via `lsof` on the running process) — not a buffering issue as first assumed, output was being discarded outright. Switched to `os_log`/`Logger` (the same unified-log technique this project already used for the keyboard-shortcut bug) to actually capture real values, then set the threshold to 0.002 from that real data. Debug logging removed once the real value was set.
- **Root-caused a hard crash: push-to-talk only works through the real signed `.app` bundle, never `swift run`.** The crash report was unambiguous: `EXC_CRASH`/`SIGABRT`, TCC namespace, "app crashed because it attempted to access privacy-sensitive data without a usage description... NSSpeechRecognitionUsageDescription." `swift run`'s raw executable has no Info.plist at all, so it can never satisfy this regardless of anything in code — voice input needs `build_app.sh`'s real bundle, which already has the required keys. This had been silently invalidating several earlier test rounds in this same debugging session before being caught.
- **Microphone capture pinned to the built-in mic, regardless of AirPods.** Real complaint: "Frank hears me incorrectly all the time especially through my airpods." Confirmed root cause, not assumed: using a Bluetooth headset for simultaneous input+output forces macOS into HFP, an old low-bandwidth call-quality codec — a real hardware/protocol limitation hitting every app, not this one specifically. New `BuiltInMicSelector.swift` enumerates Core Audio devices by transport type (not by name, which varies by Mac model) and forces `AVAudioEngine`'s input unit to the built-in mic via `kAudioOutputUnitProperty_CurrentDevice`, regardless of the system's current default input. Output (`VoiceOutput`'s TTS) is completely untouched — still plays through AirPods or whatever's connected, since only capture quality was ever the problem. Self-verified device detection directly before shipping: confirmed "MacBook Pro Microphone" is correctly identified as built-in even with AirPods actively connected.
- **Frank's replies are shorter.** `personality.py` said "concise" as one descriptor among many but never actually constrained length, and replies were unnecessarily long — worse for voice specifically, since a long reply means a long wait before any speech starts at all. Added an explicit line: default to 1-3 sentences, only go longer for a genuine trade-off. Confirmed via real conversation data before/after: replies visibly shortened ("Confirmed working now. What do you need, Josh?").

## 2026-08-05

- **Two real bugs hit from actually using the rebuilt app, both root-caused via real evidence, not guessed at.** (1) A `build_app.sh` run failed partway through copying the embedded Python runtime (a permissions error mid-copy), leaving `asyncio` and other stdlib modules corrupted while the already-running, `SMAppService`-managed backend kept auto-restarting into that broken state — confirmed via `/tmp/pcorpos-backend.log` showing a `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'asyncio'` crash loop. A second, clean rebuild fixed it; the same persistent process recovered on its own via `KeepAlive`. (2) Push-to-talk crashed every time, with the mic permission prompt reappearing on every launch. Root-caused via the actual crash report (`~/Library/Logs/DiagnosticReports`), not the first plausible-looking theory (a stale TCC cache from repeated ad-hoc re-signing, which a `tccutil reset` did NOT fix): the crash report's `responsiblePid`/`coalitionName` showed the app's TCC responsibility was being attributed to **this Claude Code session itself** (`com.anthropic.claudefordesktop`), not to PCorpOS — because Claude launched the app directly via Bash. macOS holds the *responsible* process (whoever launched the child) accountable for privacy-sensitive API usage descriptions, and this session's own app naturally has no `NSSpeechRecognitionUsageDescription` declared, so it hard-crashed regardless of PCorpOS's own already-correct `Info.plist`. Fixed by having Joshua launch the app himself (double-click in Finder) instead of Claude launching it via Bash — confirmed no crash. **Structural takeaway for future sessions:** Claude can rebuild the app, but Joshua must be the one to launch it whenever a feature touches a TCC-gated API (microphone, speech recognition, calendar, etc.) — Claude launching it via Bash will always misattribute responsibility and crash those specific code paths, independent of how correct the app's own `Info.plist` is.
