## 2026-08-10

- **Real bug from live usage: attaching an image made the chat appear completely frozen, even though the backend was working correctly.** Reported as "no response from Frank" — root-caused by checking the actual database directly rather than guessing from logs (uvicorn doesn't log WebSocket message contents, so the backend log alone couldn't prove or disprove anything): `messages` showed the full round trip had genuinely succeeded every time — a real image received, a real Claude vision response generated and correctly describing screen contents, both persisted — yet nothing appeared on screen. The actual cause: `ChatBubble`'s view body called `NSImage(data: imageData)` directly, decoding the full-resolution screenshot from raw bytes on *every* SwiftUI re-render — which happens repeatedly while a reply streams in — freezing the whole chat view on the main thread even though the backend had already finished normally. Fixed by decoding the image exactly once, at send time (`BackendClient.send`), and storing the already-decoded `NSImage` on `ChatMessage` instead of raw `Data` for display. A real, useful lesson distinct from the image-upload feature itself: an expensive computation inside a SwiftUI view body isn't a one-time cost, it's a per-render cost, and "streaming causes frequent re-renders" makes that especially easy to hit without it looking like a bug in isolated testing.
- **Design Critic — Design Agent now actually looks at images, not just Frank's description of them.** With every unblocked agent built, asked what's next; Design Agent's own scope note had explicitly named Design Critic as deferred specifically because image upload didn't exist — that blocker was gone the moment the image-upload feature above landed, same day. `run_claude_turn` (`main.py`) now forwards the current turn's raw image straight through to `execute_design_agent_tool_call` when Frank delegates to Design Agent — a real multi-modal call in that agent's own isolated Claude request, not a description relayed secondhand through Frank's own turn. Only the current turn's image is forwarded, never an older one from earlier in the same conversation, same reasoning as the placeholder-not-refetch decision for reopened conversations. Verified live on a real screenshot: correctly identified it as a build log rather than a finished screen and gave a specific, grounded critique referencing real visible elements (diff stats, sidebar, status pills, a toast notification) tied to Joshua's own stated taste — not generic design platitudes.
- **Full-codebase audit against a new additive feature spec, then the two easiest, lowest-risk items from it.** Joshua supplied a large future-direction spec (Memory Graph, Decision Journal, Design Agent, Shadow Mode, Opportunity Radar, AI Debate, Focus Lock, Situation Room, Legacy Vault, and more) with an explicit "audit first, don't rebuild, don't guess" instruction. Read every relevant doc and grepped the whole repo for all eight genuinely-new concepts by name — zero matches anywhere, confirmed net-new rather than assumed. Found three docs (`SECURITY.md`, `ALPHA_MODE.md`, `WAR_ROOM.md`) had drifted badly from reality — `SECURITY.md` still described the local-auth-token gap as unfixed (fixed 2026-07-25); `ALPHA_MODE.md` called itself a "skeleton" while a full Supabase integration has been live in production since 2026-08-02; `WAR_ROOM.md` called itself a "stub" while it's the fully-working primary interface. Also surfaced a real, previously-undocumented security gap: Alpha Mode Agent's project/invoice writes auto-execute with no confirmation or audit log, despite `SECURITY.md`'s own tier definitions naming exactly this kind of action as needing confirmation. Delivered as a prioritized table (published as an artifact, not just chat text) rather than starting to build.
- **Real audit logging, the first concrete item from that audit's own "easiest first" ranking.** New `app/audit_db.py` (its own SQLite file, same "distinct concern gets its own file" reasoning as `operations.db`/`automations.db`) plus exactly one line added at the single point in `main.py`'s tool-dispatch loop where every branch's `result` already gets finalized — no individual agent module touched, matching the explicit "don't rewrite existing code" constraint from Joshua's own spec. Every tool call, from any agent, is now recorded with tool name, full input, a truncated result, and a timestamp. No "who" column — deliberately, since P Corp OS has exactly one user and every call is always Frank acting on Joshua's behalf; noted as the first thing that would need to change if that's ever no longer true. Backend-only this pass, no UI view yet — a natural, separate follow-on once there's real data to look at. Verified live through a real Frank conversation turn (not just the isolated function): a real `save_memory` call was correctly captured end-to-end, then the test memory record was cleaned up before committing (the audit log entry itself was deliberately left in place — that's the log doing its job, not test pollution).
- **Focus Lock, the second easiest item, scoped deliberately small.** War Room's Mission Status card had a static, hardcoded "Focus: Build systems that scale" line since it was first built — the spec's Focus Lock section covers real behavioral scope (on/off mode, context-switch detection, automatic deprioritization) well beyond that, so before building, confirmed the smallest safe slice with Joshua directly rather than guessing which parts to include: just make that one line real, nothing else. New `app/focus.py` (mirrors `memory.py`'s shape — a tool schema plus a dispatch function, same "regular" permission tier as `save_memory`) backed by two new nullable columns on the existing `app_state` singleton table (`current_objective`, `objective_set_at` — same migration pattern used everywhere else, no objective set is a real, honest state rather than an error). `GET /focus` exposes it; `RightRail.swift`'s `MissionStatusCard` now fetches and displays the real objective (with an honest "Nothing set yet" fallback) via a new `FocusClient`, mirroring `AgentsClient`'s one-shot REST pattern — the tagline and progress bar were left untouched exactly as agreed, only the "Focus:" line changed. Verified live end-to-end through a real Frank conversation turn (`set_focus_objective` called, persisted, notification fired, correctly reflected back by `GET /focus`, captured in the audit log) before resetting the test objective back to the honest empty state and rebuilding the Swift shell clean.
- **Decision Journal, third item off the audit's ranking, scoped capture-only.** Asked Joshua to choose between capture-only, capture+UI, and capture+auto-context-injection before building; he picked capture-only, the same minimal shape as audit logging's own first pass. New `decisions` table added alongside `memory_records` in the existing `pcorp.db` (not its own SQLite file — this is core Frank knowledge, not a distinct-retention-pattern concern like `audit.db`/`automations.db`), with `decision` required and `reasoning`/`alternatives` optional so logging something without a fully articulated "why" isn't blocked. New `app/decision_journal.py` (mirrors `focus.py`'s shape) adds a `log_decision` tool, same "regular" permission tier as `save_memory`. `db.py` also gained a `list_decisions()` helper, deliberately not wired to any endpoint or tool yet — same "get real data flowing before deciding what surfaces it" reasoning as `audit_db.list_recent_calls`. No UI view, no automatic surfacing into Frank's context this pass. Verified live through a real Frank conversation turn: a real decision (to build Decision Journal this way) was logged, persisted with all three fields correct, notified, and captured in the audit log — left in place afterward since it's a genuine decision, not test data.
- **Memory Graph, fourth item, scoped linking-only.** Same three-way choice offered as Decision Journal (linking-only vs. +recall vs. +visual UI); Joshua again picked the smallest. New `memory_links` edge table (`from_type`/`from_id`/`to_type`/`to_id`/`relationship`) over the two existing capture tables, `memory_records` and `decisions` — a plain type+id column pair rather than per-combination foreign keys, since more linkable record types (tasks, projects) are a plausible future addition that shouldn't need a schema change. The real design question was how Frank identifies which two records to link, given he's never been shown a raw row ID for either (`forget_memory` already established this — it matches by title, not ID): `link_records` (`app/memory_graph.py`, mirrors `focus.py`'s shape) resolves both sides server-side by the same text Frank already has — a memory's title, or a decision's own wording — using the identical exact-then-substring, most-recent-first matching `forget_memory_by_title` already used, now factored into `db.py`'s `_resolve_memory_id`/`_resolve_decision_id`. Returns a clear "couldn't find one or both of those" result rather than silently linking the wrong thing if a match fails. No traversal/query tool, no UI this pass. Verified live through a real Frank conversation turn: a real memory was saved, then linked to the existing Decision Journal entry above ("Memory Graph build" preceded by "Build Decision Journal as capture-only, no UI yet"), both IDs resolved correctly, notified, and captured in the audit log — left in place, genuine data.
- **Shadow Mode, fifth item — the first genuinely new "observation" capability, scoped over two rounds of direct confirmation rather than one.** First confirmed what the term even meant (picked "passive activity awareness" over a dry-run-suggestions reading), then how invasive capture should be (picked continuous logged history over an on-demand-only snapshot). Deliberately scoped to the least invasive shape available for that choice: `ActivityTracker.swift` observes `NSWorkspace`'s own frontmost-app-change notification — a public API needing no permission prompt at all, chosen specifically over the AppleScript/System-Events route (which would trigger a real TCC automation-permission dialog, the same class of problem as the earlier mic crash) — and only fires when the frontmost app actually changes, not on a fixed poll interval, so the resulting `activity_log` table (new, in `pcorp.db`) is already a segmented history rather than raw samples. Captures **app name only** — never window titles, URLs, or file contents. `POST /activity/log` (`main.py`) is the write path from Swift, not a Frank tool; `get_recent_activity` (`app/shadow_mode.py`) is the read path — a genuine on-demand recall tool, included deliberately (unlike Decision Journal/Memory Graph's pure write-only first passes) because a log Frank can never reference would just be inert data with no path to ever being useful. Scoped to answer only when directly asked, never proactively volunteered. Verified live end-to-end: two real `POST /activity/log` writes, then a real Frank conversation turn correctly calling `get_recent_activity` and reporting back accurately without editorializing beyond what was asked; both test rows cleared afterward (unlike Decision Journal/Memory Graph's genuine entries, these were synthetic — never real activity) so the log starts honest once launched for real.
- **Real bug from live usage, same day: `UserNotificationCenter` (macOS's notification-banner process) got logged as if Joshua switched to it.** It briefly becomes the frontmost app whenever a notification banner appears, which `NSWorkspace`'s notification faithfully reports — technically true, but not a real activity switch, just noise. Fixed by filtering to `NSRunningApplication.activationPolicy == .regular` before logging — the same policy macOS itself uses to decide what gets a Dock icon — rather than hardcoding a blocklist of specific system-process names, so it also naturally excludes Dock, SystemUIServer, Spotlight, and similar system UI without needing to enumerate them. The one already-captured noise row was deleted from `activity_log`; the two genuine `P Corp OS`/`Claude` rows from that same test session were left in place.
- **Opportunity Radar, sixth item — scoped over two rounds, then folded into an existing system rather than becoming a new one.** First confirmed what "opportunity" meant (Alpha Mode business signals, not a broader Frank-notices-anything scope), then discovered the real Alpha Mode Supabase database already has a `leads` table with real fields to work from (`temperature`, `qualification_score`, `follow_up_sent`, `quote_status`, and more) — checked the live schema directly rather than guessing at it. Confirmed the one clear, low-noise signal to start with: warm/hot leads that haven't had a follow-up sent yet. Asked where it should surface, and where the additive spec's own "extend, don't duplicate" rule pointed directly at the existing "Frank's Insights" card (`app/insights.py`) rather than a new UI section — same deterministic, no-LLM, data-already-exists shape as its other three categories (overdue tasks, overdue invoices, stale client outreach), so a new `_leads_needing_followup()` category was added there rather than building a parallel mechanism. New `alpha_mode_supabase.leads_needing_followup()` queries the real `leads` table read-only, fails soft on error same as `summarize_block()`. Verified against real production data (currently one real lead, correctly excluded since it's `cold`) and against synthetic data via a monkeypatched dependency (not written to the real database) to confirm the formatting logic itself, including the null-`qualification_score` fallback. `RightRail.swift`'s empty-state copy updated to match ("Nothing overdue, due soon, or worth following up on") — the card's rendering was already fully generic over whatever `compute_insights()` returns, so no other Swift changes were needed.
- **Situation Room, seventh item — an escalated alert tier, deliberately separate from the routine Insights card rather than folded into it (the opposite call from Opportunity Radar, made on purpose: this one's meant to read as "interrupt," not another list row).** Scoped over two rounds: first what the term meant (an automatic severity-threshold alert, not a Joshua-triggered "we're in a crisis" mode), then the concrete thresholds. New `app/situation_room.py` reuses the same underlying data (overdue tasks, overdue invoices, stale client outreach — the identical sources `insights.py` already reads) but filters to a stricter tier: invoice **30+ days** overdue (real cash-flow risk vs. any amount overdue), task **14+ days** overdue (stale/neglected vs. daily debris), client outreach **45+ days** stale (vs. the routine 14-day nudge) — thresholds confirmed directly with Joshua rather than guessed, same spirit as `insights.py`'s own `OUTREACH_STALE_AFTER_DAYS` precedent. New `GET /situation-room` endpoint. Rendered as a new `SituationRoomBanner` at the top of War Room (`WarRoomView.swift`), styled distinctly from the calm right-rail Insights card (red accent, warning icon) and only ever shown when a real alert exists — its absence is the honest, common case. `SituationRoomClient` polls every 30s, same pattern as `InsightsCard`. Verified against real production data: the actual "Marketing SOP" task, overdue since 2025-07-31, correctly flagged at 375 days overdue — no synthetic data needed, a real gap in Alpha Mode's own task tracking the feature caught on its first real run.
- **Real gap found via that same test task: no way to delete a task, only mark it done/blocked/etc.** Joshua flagged it directly after manually deleting the bad "Marketing SOP" row via raw SQL to clear the Situation Room banner it had just triggered — a genuinely bad row (wrong due date, never real) has no honest status to move it to; marking it "done" would have been a lie. New `delete_task` tool (`app/operations_agent.py`, alongside `add_task`/`update_task_status`) soft-deletes via a new nullable `deleted_at` column on `tasks` (same migration pattern as `memory_records`), matching by title with the same exact-then-substring logic already factored out of `update_task_status` into a shared `_find_task_id` helper. Soft, not a real `DELETE`, specifically to keep this in SECURITY.md's "Regular" auto-allowed tier (reversible) rather than "needs confirmation" — same reasoning `forget_memory` already established. Fixed two related raw queries that would have kept showing a soft-deleted task forever otherwise: `situation_room.py`'s and `insights.py`'s own direct `tasks` queries (neither goes through `operations_db.py`'s functions) were both missing a `deleted_at IS NULL` filter — caught while wiring this, not left as a latent bug. Verified live end-to-end: a real task added then deleted in the same conversation turn, confirmed gone from `GET /operations/tasks`, the row itself preserved with a real `deleted_at` timestamp (not destroyed), both calls captured in the audit log.
- **AI Debate, eighth item — scoped to one specific shape, not the broader "two personas debate each other" reading.** Confirmed Joshua wanted a real decision pressure-tested (strongest case for, strongest case against, then an honest verdict) rather than a multi-turn simulated back-and-forth between two AI personas — a materially bigger, different feature, explicitly not chosen. New `app/debate.py` — same proven architecture as every other specialist (Operations/Design/Creative Director/Communications/Memory/Research Agent): a second, streamed Claude call with its own system prompt, invoked as a `debate_decision` tool from Frank's own turn. Deliberately **not** added to `agents_registry.py` as a specialist agent, unlike those others: this isn't a domain Frank delegates to, it's a structured response format applied to whatever decision Joshua names — a real architectural difference, not an oversight. The system prompt explicitly instructs steelmanning both sides and forbids a wishy-washy "it depends" non-verdict. No database — nothing to store; if a debated decision lands somewhere real, Joshua can ask Frank to `log_decision` separately, no special glue code linking the two since both tools are already available in the same turn. Verified live on a real question ("should Alpha Mode Media hire a full-time editor instead of freelancers"): both sides came back genuinely argued and grounded in real client context (Vodacom, JD Sports, The Bread/Yoco, pulled in by Frank's own self-contained framing of the request), with a concrete verdict naming exactly what data would resolve it, not a hedge.
- **Legacy Vault, ninth and final item off the audit's ranking — scoped carefully given real stakes, and one real limitation named rather than quietly built around.** Confirmed the meaning first (succession/emergency info for someone else, not a permanent-memory tier), then the architecture: a genuinely separate table/tools from `memory_records` (`app/legacy_vault.py`, `save_to_legacy_vault`/`list_legacy_vault`/`delete_from_legacy_vault`), specifically because regular memories get folded into every turn via `build_memory_block` and vault entries must never do that — they're meant to sit dormant until genuinely needed, not surface mid-conversation. Unlike Decision Journal's capture-only first pass, list and delete are real tools here too, deliberately, since content this consequential needs to be reviewable, not write-only. **Named, not solved:** this only makes Frank able to store and recall vault entries on request -- it does not solve actual access for someone else if Joshua is incapacitated, since P Corp OS has no multi-user access model at all; physically getting this information to whoever needs it (export, printing, a lawyer) remains a separate, harder, unsolved problem, confirmed as out of scope for this pass rather than silently ignored. The tool description explicitly forbids storing credentials/passwords/API keys/account numbers, same hard line as everywhere else in this project. Verified live three ways: a real entry saved and correctly listed back; a deliberate attempt to save a bank password, which Frank correctly refused *before ever calling the tool* (confirmed via the audit log — no `save_to_legacy_vault` call exists for that attempt) and redirected toward a password manager instead. The one real test entry was deleted afterward rather than left in place — unlike Decision Journal/Memory Graph's genuine session decisions, this was assistant-invented content, not something Joshua actually asked to have recorded in what's meant to be his own deliberately curated succession record.
- **Chat input multi-line attempt, tried twice, then reverted.** First attempt used `TextField(axis: .vertical)`; a real bug surfaced immediately on the actual rebuild (screenshot from live use): it grew correctly but left a stale, truncated single-line ghost render behind on screen. Second attempt replaced it with `TextEditor` plus a manual placeholder overlay and `.onKeyPress` handling for Return-to-send/Shift+Return-for-newline. Joshua asked to go back to how it was originally rather than keep iterating on it live — reverted cleanly to the original single-line `TextField` (confirmed via diff against the pre-change commit, byte-identical). Multi-line input for this field isn't pursued further for now.
- **Alpha Mode Media's sidebar section is now real** — asked what should be there before building rather than guessing; landed on a dashboard surfacing data that already existed in the backend but only ever showed up through conversation, Insights, or Situation Room: open projects (with stage), pending/overdue invoices, and leads needing follow-up (the same Opportunity Radar signal, shown in context here). `alpha_mode_supabase.py` gained `get_open_projects()`/`get_pending_invoices()` (factored out of `summarize_block`, which now reuses them — no duplicated query logic) plus a new `dashboard_snapshot()` that aggregates both with `leads_needing_followup()`, each section failing soft independently so one bad Supabase request doesn't blank the whole page. New `GET /alpha-mode/dashboard` endpoint; new `AlphaModeDashboardView.swift`/`AlphaModeDashboardClient.swift`, same GET-and-render pattern as `AutomationsView`. Verified against real production data: 12 real projects (Vodacom, JD Sports, Decorex, The Bread/Yoco, and others) and 8 real pending invoices came back correctly shaped. Also surfaced that Alpha Mode Media had actually been a 6th, previously-uncounted placeholder section in `ROADMAP.md`'s own tracking — corrected there too.
- **Opportunity Radar's second signal: quotes sent but sitting unanswered.** The real `leads` table has `quote_status`/`quote_sent_date` fields Opportunity Radar's first pass never used. New `alpha_mode_supabase.quotes_needing_followup()` flags quotes still in `sent` status (never moved to `accepted`/`declined`) after 14+ days — same 14-day default as `insights.py`'s own `OUTREACH_STALE_AFTER_DAYS`, same read-only/fail-soft shape as the existing lead signal. New `_quotes_needing_followup()` category folded into `compute_insights()`, no new UI or endpoint needed (the Insights card already renders whatever categories exist). Verified two ways: the real PostgREST filter query against production data (empty result, no error — correctly matches reality, since the one real lead is still `draft`, never `sent`) and the formatting logic against synthetic data via a monkeypatched dependency (not written to the real database), including the null-`quote_amount` fallback.
- **Sidebar and Agents tab icons now carry color** instead of the app's default monochrome black/white. New `IconColors.swift` maps each nav item and each specialist agent's icon to one deliberately restrained system color (no custom bright/neon values) — the same class of colors already used sparingly elsewhere (status dots, Situation Room's red, Opportunity Radar's orange flame), consistent with Joshua's own stated taste against loud color. The Alpha Mode Media sidebar entry was the one deliberate exception initially (theme-tinted, not recolored), then given its own real brand blue (`#000CFD`, given directly by Joshua) once he saw it live. Sidebar icons keep their existing selected/unselected distinction (full color vs. 55% opacity), now colored instead of grayscale.
- **"Show System Status" is now a real Settings toggle, not decoration.** `SettingsView.swift`'s own docstring said it plainly: nothing there was wired to a backend or persisted except dark mode. Persisted via `@AppStorage` (same key `Sidebar.swift` now reads directly, same proven pattern as dark mode) — flipping it off actually hides the sidebar's "SYSTEM STATUS" block (and its divider) rather than doing nothing. "Proactive Insights" and "Sound Effects" remain no-ops for now — the former would need to gate real polling/notifications across multiple views, the latter needs a sound asset and playback wiring that doesn't exist yet; both bigger scope, deliberately not bundled into this pass.
- **Mobile access re-enabled and verified live, real progress toward a native iOS app rather than the app itself.** Direct ask: whether an exact-match mobile version was possible. Before writing anything, checked what already existed rather than assuming a rebuild was needed — found the Tailscale dual-listener and `/mobile` web page were already built (2026-07-31) but dormant, `TAILSCALE_IP` unset in `.env`. Set it to this Mac's real Tailscale IP, restarted the backend, confirmed the second listener came up alongside the unchanged `127.0.0.1` one, verified the token-gated endpoint answered correctly over Tailscale, then Joshua confirmed a real chat round-trip with Frank from his actual iPhone 15. `SECURITY.md`'s threat model updated per its own stated trigger ("revisit when... a second device becomes real") — still no cloud sync, still single shared auth token, no app-level TLS needed since Tailscale's own WireGuard tunnel already encrypts the link. A real native SwiftUI iOS app (matching the desktop app's visual language, not its literal fixed-width 3-column layout) is the live next option — blocked only on Xcode being installed; this Mac still only has Command Line Tools, confirmed directly rather than assumed.

## 2026-08-12

- **New `PCorpKit` shared Swift package — real code reuse foundation for the native iOS app, not the app itself yet.** Joshua installed Xcode; before creating any iOS project, extracted every platform-agnostic file (models, all eight `*Client.swift` networking classes, `Theme`/`PCorpFont`/`IconColors`) out of `desktop/` into a new local package both the macOS app and the future iOS app import, matching what `TECH_STACK.md` committed to when SwiftUI was chosen. `NavItem`/`QuickAction`/`PlaceholderData` deliberately stayed desktop-only — the fixed 3-column sidebar is a desktop-specific layout, not something mobile's tab-bar/stack navigation would reuse. Two real platform differences couldn't be shared as-is and needed a pluggable-provider pattern instead: `AuthToken` (desktop reads a local file on the same Mac as the backend, impossible from an iPhone) and `SystemNotification` (desktop shells out to `osascript`, unavailable in an iOS sandbox) both became `public static var provider`/`poster` closures PCorpKit defines and each platform's app shell sets once at startup. Every `*Client.swift` file's hardcoded `127.0.0.1` became a new `BackendHost.host` (defaults to `127.0.0.1` for desktop, settable to this Mac's Tailscale IP for iOS) — the systematic fix that makes the shared networking code actually usable from a phone instead of only working on the same machine as the backend. `BackendClient`'s `NSImage` became a `#if canImport(UIKit)` `PlatformImage` typealias. Verified: `desktop/` builds clean against the new dependency on the first real attempt, and the full `.app` bundle assembles without error — a real regression check given how much internal plumbing moved, even though nothing user-facing should have changed.
- **First real iOS app screen — the whole pipeline proven end to end, not just planned.** Downloaded and installed the iOS 26.5 Simulator runtime (8.52GB), created a real iPhone 15 Simulator device (not shipped by default with this Xcode version — the newest default device templates are iPhone 17-generation). Joshua created the actual Xcode project by hand (safer than hand-writing a raw `.xcodeproj` from scratch, given how fragile that format is to get exactly right) with two real mistakes to fix directly rather than redo: wrong bundle identifier and wrong save location (`~/Desktop/P Corp OS/`, with its own accidental nested `.git` init, instead of inside this repo) — both fixed from the command line (`sed` on `PRODUCT_BUNDLE_IDENTIFIER`, `mv` into `ios/`, removed the stray nested repo), no GUI rework needed. Added `PCorpKit` as a real local Swift package dependency by hand-editing `project.pbxproj` directly (`XCLocalSwiftPackageReference`/`XCSwiftPackageProductDependency`/`PBXBuildFile`, validated via `plutil -lint` before every build attempt) — this project uses Xcode's newer `PBXFileSystemSynchronizedRootGroup` format, so source files need no individual project-file entries, only the package reference did. Wrote a real first screen (`ContentView.swift`, `P_Corp_OSApp.swift`): a plain chat thread using PCorpKit's own `BackendClient`, with a one-time manual auth-token entry screen (stored in `UserDefaults`, not Keychain — a genuine scope cut for this first pass, flagged rather than silent) and `BackendHost.host` hardcoded to this Mac's Tailscale IP. Hit and fixed a real, expected platform issue: iOS's App Transport Security blocks plain HTTP by default, so the WebSocket/REST calls were silently failing until a real `Info.plist` (switching off `GENERATE_INFOPLIST_FILE`, adding an explicit `NSAllowsArbitraryLoads` exception — a deliberate, scoped choice given this app only ever talks to Joshua's own Mac over his own private Tailscale network, not a lazy blanket disable) replaced the auto-generated one; hit and fixed the resulting "multiple commands produce Info.plist" conflict from the synchronized file group also treating it as a copyable resource, via a `PBXFileSystemSynchronizedBuildFileExceptionSet`. Verified genuinely end to end, not just "it compiled": built via `xcodebuild`, installed and launched on the real iPhone 15 Simulator, confirmed via `xcrun simctl` screenshots and Console logs that the WebSocket connection and a `POST /conversations` call both completed successfully over the actual Tailscale network interface (`utun6`) — then cross-checked from the other side, the backend's own log showing `WebSocket /ws?token=... [accepted]`, `connection open`, and `POST /conversations?token=... 200 OK` all originating from the Tailscale IP, the same listener the physical iPhone already uses. The Claude Code iOS Simulator tool's live-panel integration needs `sudo xcode-select -s /Applications/Xcode.app/Contents/Developer` (a one-time fix only Joshua can run) — not yet done, so this round's verification used direct `simctl`/log-based checks instead, stated explicitly rather than silently working around it.
- **Same app, verified on Joshua's actual physical iPhone 15, not just the Simulator.** Real code signing needed a real Apple ID: Joshua signed into Xcode with his personal Apple account, connected the iPhone via USB, trusted the Mac, and enabled Developer Mode — all steps only he could do. Found his free personal Team ID (`BNZ9L3G83L`) directly from Xcode's own stored account preferences rather than asking him to look it up, and set `DEVELOPMENT_TEAM` in the project accordingly. `xcodebuild -allowProvisioningUpdates` auto-created a real "iOS Team Provisioning Profile" and signed with his actual Apple Development identity — building for a physical device needs a real certificate/profile pair the Simulator never required. First install attempt correctly failed with "profile has not been explicitly trusted by the user" — the expected, required manual step (Settings → General → VPN & Device Management → Trust) only Joshua could complete on the phone itself; not worked around. After that, `xcrun devicectl` installed and launched the app on the real device. Verified the same way as the Simulator pass, but this time genuinely conclusive: the backend's log shows a `WebSocket [accepted]` and `POST /conversations 200 OK` originating from `100.92.85.52` — Joshua's actual iPhone's own Tailscale IP (confirmed distinct from the Mac's `100.93.170.24` via `tailscale status`), not the Mac's network stack the Simulator shares. The full loop — real device, real signing, real network, real backend — is confirmed working, not just plausible.
- **iOS auth token moved from UserDefaults to Keychain**, closing the scope cut flagged in the first iOS pass. New `KeychainTokenStore.swift` (iOS app target, not `PCorpKit` — desktop has no use for this at all, its own `AuthToken.provider` reads a local file instead) wraps the standard `SecItemAdd`/`SecItemCopyMatching`/`SecItemDelete` pattern; `ContentView`'s Connect button and `P_Corp_OSApp`'s `AuthToken.provider` both switched over. Also removed the stale plaintext copy left in `UserDefaults` from the first pass on app launch — leaving it behind would have quietly defeated the whole point of migrating. Verified on Joshua's actual iPhone: entered the token, force-quit, relaunched — went straight to chat with no re-prompt, confirming the real save/reload round trip, not just that the stale token was cleared.
- **Real War Room screen for iOS, replacing the first pass's plain chat thread.** Scoped deliberately: one properly-designed screen (desktop's own docs call War Room "the primary interface, not a stub") rather than the full 3-column layout with all 11 nav destinations — that's a much bigger undertaking, explicitly deferred to later rounds, not attempted here. New `WarRoomView.swift` reuses `PCorpFont` directly from `PCorpKit` (typography was already cross-platform) but deliberately uses iOS's own native semantic colors instead of porting `AppTheme`'s manual light/dark toggle — a first-class iOS app should follow the system appearance the normal iOS way, not carry over desktop's own in-app switch. Desktop's separate right-rail cards (Mission Status via `FocusClient`, Frank's Insights via `InsightsClient`, Situation Room via `SituationRoomClient` — all three already existed in `PCorpKit`, zero new networking code needed) become stacked sections above the chat, since there's no room for a second column on a phone. Today's Agenda deliberately excluded — desktop's version reads the macOS Calendar app via AppleScript, which has no iOS equivalent; a real mobile agenda needs iOS's own EventKit against the iPhone's own calendar, a genuinely separate feature not built yet. `ContentView.swift` simplified to just the token gate, handing off to `WarRoomView` once connected. Verified on Joshua's actual iPhone (Keychain token from the prior pass carried over the reinstall, going straight to the real screen): the Mission Status card correctly showed live backend data, including an honest "Focus: Nothing set yet" — no focus objective is currently set — and both the Insights and Situation Room sections correctly stayed hidden since there's nothing to show right now, same conditional logic as desktop.
- **Two real bugs found immediately from that same live test, both fixed same round.** (1) Sent message got no visible reply — the first WarRoomView cut put the dashboard cards and the chat in one combined `ScrollView` with no auto-scroll, so a reply could render successfully but sit off-screen below Mission Status/Insights, looking exactly like "no response" even though it wasn't. Restructured: dashboard cards are now a fixed, non-scrolling header, and the chat gets its own dedicated `ScrollViewReader`-based region — the identical proven pattern desktop's own `ChatThreadView` already used (`.onChange(of: messages.count)`/`.onChange(of: messages.last?.content)` triggering `scrollTo(anchor: .bottom)`, including mid-stream). (2) The home screen app icon was blank — the Xcode-generated `AppIcon.appiconset` had no actual image files behind its declared slots, just empty placeholders. Reused the same source artwork as desktop's own icon (`app_icon.png`, the white P mark on black) rather than commissioning anything new, but it needed real prep first: iOS app icons must have no alpha channel at all, and the source had one, so it was flattened onto its own black background and re-exported as opaque RGB before being wired into the asset catalog. Both verified live on Joshua's actual iPhone: the icon now shows correctly, and a real sent message now gets a real visible reply.
- **iOS visual language matched to desktop pixel-for-pixel, reversing the earlier iOS-native-colors call.** Direct ask: "I want the UI of mobile to match desktop as closely as possible." `WarRoomView.swift`/`ContentView.swift` switched from iOS's own semantic colors to `PCorpKit`'s real `AppTheme` via `\.appTheme`, with every card's chrome (`CardContainer`'s `regularMaterial` + `theme.background.opacity(0.35)` + `strokeBorder` + shadow), the Situation Room banner, the input bar's focus-state treatment, and chat bubble styling all copied from desktop's `RightRail.swift`/`WarRoomView.swift` rather than approximated — only the chat bubble's max width (340pt vs. desktop's 520pt) was adjusted for phone width. One real, flagged deviation from the approved scope: desktop picks `AppTheme` from a manual Settings toggle, but there's no Settings screen on iOS yet to host one, so this reads the phone's system light/dark appearance instead — the best available signal absent a real toggle, not silently swapped in.
- **Hamburger menu + real sidebar, voice-to-text, and image attach — the next round of "match desktop," direct ask.** New `RootView.swift` replaces `WarRoomView` as `ContentView`'s post-token destination: a hamburger button in a new top bar (now showing the actual P Corp logo mark, ported from desktop's `p_logo_black.png`, not text) opens `Sidebar.swift` as a slide-in drawer over the content, since a phone has no room for desktop's fixed 3-column layout. `Sidebar.swift` is a close port of desktop's own `Sidebar.swift` — same header, same nav-row icon coloring (`IconColors.forNavItem`, plus the Alpha Mode Media row's real brand logo via a newly-added `alpha_mode_logo` image asset), same System Status footer — listing the identical 11 sections as desktop's `PlaceholderData.navItems` (mirrored by hand into a new `NavItem.swift`, since desktop's own copy is explicitly desktop-only). Confirmed scope for tonight (given the hour): only War Room has a real screen so far, so every other section shows the same honest "not built yet / STANDBY" placeholder desktop itself already uses for its own not-yet-built sections — porting the other 7 real screens (Frank, Alpha Mode Media, Knowledge, Agents, Automations, Calendar, Settings) is deferred to its own future round rather than rushed tonight. Voice input: new `VoiceInput.swift`, a port of desktop's push-to-talk `VoiceInput.swift` (same Speech/AVAudioEngine flow, same auto-stop-on-silence tuning) with desktop's macOS-only `BuiltInMicSelector` step dropped and a real `AVAudioSession` activation added in its place, since iOS needs one and macOS doesn't; wired to the input bar's mic button exactly like desktop. Image attach: the paperclip button now opens a real `PhotosPicker` (iOS's own picker, replacing desktop's `NSOpenPanel`), feeding the same `BackendClient.send(_:imageData:mediaType:)` desktop already uses, with the same attached-image chip and in-bubble image rendering ported over. Also fixed a real usability bug Joshua hit live: no way to dismiss the keyboard once it was up — added a "Done" button above the keyboard and `.scrollDismissesKeyboard(.interactively)` on the chat scroll view. `Info.plist` gained the mic/speech/photo-library usage-description keys this all requires. Verified: clean `xcodebuild` for both Simulator and Joshua's physical iPhone, installed and launched on the real device.

## 2026-08-13

- **Top bar now shows the real P Corp logo mark instead of the section title as text** — same `p_logo_black` asset added the day before, just swapped into `RootView.swift`'s top bar (previously showed the selected section's name in caps).
- **Real bug: "Frank on mobile doesn't respond."** Confirmed server-side first rather than guessing client-side — `backend/data/pcorp.db` showed Frank had actually generated and stored a correct reply to Joshua's last message, and a live check of the backend's listening port showed zero active connections from the phone at all. Root cause: `BackendClient.connect()` (`PCorpKit`, shared with desktop) only ever opens its WebSocket once and guards against reopening it (`task == nil`) — correct for desktop, where a Mac app is never suspended, but wrong for iOS, where the OS silently kills network activity whenever the phone locks or the app backgrounds, leaving `task` non-nil but dead. Fixed with an iOS-only `@Environment(\.scenePhase)` watcher in `WarRoomView.swift`: returning to `.active` calls `disconnect()` then `connect()`, tearing down the stale socket and opening a fresh one (which also reloads history, so nothing sent while disconnected is lost from view). Deliberately scoped to the iOS view rather than touching `PCorpKit`'s shared `connect()` logic, since desktop doesn't have or need this problem. Verified live on Joshua's iPhone: sent a message after the phone had been locked for a while, got a real reply.
- **Explored AltStore/AltServer as a free alternative to the paid Apple Developer Program** (Joshua wants to hold off on the $99/year for now) — installed AltServer (verified signed/notarized by AltStore's real developer identity before installing), but it failed to work: the process ran without crashing but never created a menu bar icon or registered as a background item, reproduced identically both via a scripted launch and Joshua double-clicking it directly himself. Root cause traced to a version mismatch — Joshua's Mac is on macOS 26.5.2, while the downloaded AltServer build was signed back in September 2024, well before it. Given the sunk time, Joshua chose to abandon AltStore rather than chase the beta channel; AltServer was fully removed. Staying with the existing plan instead: manual rebuild-and-reinstall when the profile expires, with a scheduled reminder already set for 2026-08-18 (a day before the 2026-08-19 expiry).
- **First of the 7 deferred sidebar sections built for real: Frank.** New `FrankView.swift` is a close port of desktop's own — same header, same loading/error/empty states, same memory record cards (type badge, sensitive-record lock icon, forget button), all built on `PCorpKit`'s already-cross-platform `MemoryClient`/`MemoryRecord`, zero new networking code needed. `RootView.swift`'s switch gained a `"Frank"` case pointing at it instead of the placeholder. One real touch-vs-pointer adaptation: desktop's forget (trash) button only appears on `.onHover`, which has no touchscreen equivalent, so it's just always visible on iOS. That surfaced a real gap the same day, live: Joshua tapped it once, on a real (non-test) memory record ("Memory Graph build"), and it deleted immediately — hover was quietly acting as desktop's own "are you sure" buffer, and removing it removed that safety net too. Fixed with a `.confirmationDialog` ("Forget \"[title]\"?" / Forget / Cancel) before the delete actually fires. The already-deleted record was recovered directly in `pcorp.db` (soft-delete only sets `deleted_at`, confirmed nothing was actually lost) rather than being gone for good.
- **Second deferred section built: Settings — and it finally closes a real gap flagged two rounds ago.** New `SettingsView.swift` ports desktop's own: real, persisted Dark Mode / Show System Status toggles (same `@AppStorage` keys desktop uses), honest Proactive Insights / Sound Effects no-ops matching desktop's own current stub state. One row deliberately *not* ported — desktop's "Launch at Login" controls `SMAppService` registration for the backend process, which only ever runs on the Mac; there's no iOS equivalent of "launch the backend at login" on a phone that doesn't run the backend. More importantly, this is what let `ContentView.swift` finally switch from following the phone's system light/dark appearance to reading the same manual `darkModeEnabled` key desktop does — the originally-approved scope from the visual-matching round ("desktop's manual dark-mode toggle instead of following the system setting") had been deviated from only because there was no Settings screen yet to host the toggle; now there is.
- **Three rounds of live feedback on the new Settings/dark-mode work, all fixed same-day.** (1) Dark mode's card/input-bar surface color read as flat "dark grey" once actually seen live — `AppTheme.dark.surface` (`PCorpKit/Theme.swift`, shared with desktop) was `Color(white: 0.12)`, too close to `background`'s `0.07` to read as a distinct elevated panel under the `.regularMaterial`/`.ultraThinMaterial` blur. Bumped to a warmer `Color(red: 0.165, green: 0.155, blue: 0.175)` — both platforms share this value, so desktop's dark mode gets the same improvement. (2) The keyboard's "Done" button (added two rounds ago to fix "can't dismiss the keyboard") turned out to just be disliked on sight — removed, keeping `.scrollDismissesKeyboard(.interactively)` (drag the chat down) plus a new tap-anywhere-on-the-header-to-dismiss as the two remaining ways to close the keyboard. (3) Direct ask: swipe the sidebar open/closed, not just the hamburger button. First attempt only closed, never opened — real bug, not a misunderstanding: both directions shared one `DragGesture` on the whole screen, but the chat is a `ScrollView`, and its own pan recognizer wins iOS/SwiftUI's gesture-priority contest against a parent's plain `.gesture()` for touches starting inside it. Closing was never affected since the main content's hit-testing is already off once the drawer's open, leaving no `ScrollView` to compete with. Fixed by giving the open-swipe its own dedicated ~32pt hot zone hugging the true left bezel with no scrollable content inside it to lose to — the same edge convention Mail/Gmail/Slack use, not an arbitrary width. Verified live on Joshua's iPhone: opens and closes correctly both by hamburger tap and by swipe now.
- **Third deferred section built: Automations.** New `AutomationsView.swift` is an unmodified port of desktop's own — rule cards (trigger tool, description, ACTIVE badge) plus a recent-activity feed of real firings, entirely on `PCorpKit`'s already-cross-platform `AutomationsClient`/`AutomationRule`/`AutomationRun`. No AppKit dependencies and no hover-only affordances to adapt this time (unlike Frank's forget button), so this was a direct copy rather than a redesign. `RootView.swift`'s switch gained an `"Automations"` case.
- **Fourth deferred section built: Agents.** New `AgentsView.swift`, another direct, unmodified port — specialist cards (icon, tagline, ACTIVE/status badge) sourced from `GET /agents` via `AgentsClient`, plus the Operations Agent's open-task list via `OperationsClient`, both already cross-platform in `PCorpKit` along with `IconColors.forAgentIcon`. `RootView.swift`'s switch gained an `"Agents"` case. 4 of the 7 deferred sections now real (Frank, Settings, Automations, Agents) — Alpha Mode Media, Knowledge, and Calendar remain.
- **Fifth deferred section built: Knowledge — the first one that wasn't a direct port.** Two real differences from Frank/Settings/Automations/Agents. (1) Desktop's `KnowledgeView.swift` reads its markdown docs (`FOUNDER_BRIEF.md`, `ROADMAP.md`, etc.) straight off local disk via `ProjectPaths.repoRoot`, which only works because desktop runs on the same Mac as the repo — a phone has no filesystem access to that Mac at all. Added a real new backend surface for this: `backend/app/knowledge.py` (a filename allowlist mirroring desktop's `KnowledgeDocs.all`, validated before ever touching disk — never a free-form path from the request, which would otherwise be a real path-traversal opening) plus two new routes, `GET /knowledge` (doc list) and `GET /knowledge/{filename}` (content), both token-gated like every other route. New iOS-only `KnowledgeClient.swift` fetches from these; content loads on demand per doc selected, not all upfront, since some of these files (`CHANGELOG.md`) are large. (2) Desktop's fixed master-detail HStack (a permanent 260pt doc list beside the content pane) has no room on a phone, so this is a push-based list-then-detail flow via `NavigationStack` instead — tap a doc, get a back button, not a permanent split. `SimpleMarkdownView` (desktop's own small markdown renderer — headings, lists, rules, inline bold/italic/links) had zero platform-specific code to begin with, so it moved into `PCorpKit` outright for both platforms to share, rather than being duplicated. Verified: both `swift build` (desktop) and `xcodebuild` (iOS) clean, backend restarted and the new routes curl-tested directly before ever touching the app, then confirmed live on Joshua's iPhone.

## 2026-08-14

- **Sixth deferred section built: Alpha Mode Media.** New `AlphaModeDashboardView.swift`, a direct, unmodified port — open projects, pending invoices, and warm/hot leads needing follow-up, all sourced live from the real Alpha Mode Media Admin Supabase database via `PCorpKit`'s already-cross-platform `AlphaModeDashboardClient`/`AlphaModeProject`/`AlphaModeInvoice`/`AlphaModeLead`. No AppKit dependencies and no hover-only affordances to adapt, so no redesign needed. `RootView.swift`'s switch gained an `"Alpha Mode Media"` case. 6 of the 7 deferred sections now real — only Calendar remains.
- **Seventh and final deferred section built: Calendar — all 7 sidebar sections now real, none left as placeholders.** Genuinely different data source from desktop, not a port: desktop's `SystemCalendar.swift` reads the *Mac's* Calendar app via AppleScript, a workaround for a permissions bug specific to its own raw `swift build` executable (no real bundle for macOS to attach a privacy grant to) — that workaround has no iOS equivalent (no AppleScript on iOS at all) and isn't needed anyway, since this is a properly bundled app where the normal EventKit permission flow just works. New iOS-only `SystemCalendar.swift` reads the *iPhone's own* Calendar app via `EKEventStore`/`requestFullAccessToEvents()` instead — same day-grouped event list UI as desktop's `CalendarView.swift`, ported as-is, backed by a genuinely different source. Flagged directly, not glossed over: if the phone's calendars aren't on the same account(s) as the Mac's, the two views could show different events; in practice (Joshua's own iCloud calendar synced to both) they show the same ones, confirmed live. `Info.plist` gained `NSCalendarsFullAccessUsageDescription`. `RootView.swift`'s switch gained a `"Calendar"` case.
- **Desktop's persistent backend service actually activated, not just built.** Prompted by Joshua asking how to keep P Corp running indefinitely on both devices — checked rather than assumed, and found the real `SMAppService`/`launchd`-supervised backend (built and verified back in Phase 2) wasn't actually the thing running; only a bare manual `nohup` process from that session's own testing was. Rebuilt the real bundle (`desktop/build_app.sh`) and had Joshua flip "Launch at Login" in Settings, which registers it for real — verified properly this time by force-killing the process directly and confirming `launchd` restarted it automatically within seconds. Genuinely indefinite now, zero cost, no further action needed. iPhone side stays on the existing manual rebuild-and-reinstall cycle (with the scheduled reminder) — Joshua explicitly chose that over paying for the Apple Developer Program or retrying AltStore, after weighing the real trade-offs.
- **Real bug found immediately after: "sent a message no response" on the iPhone, again — but a different, more fundamental bug than the one fixed 2026-08-13.** The backend got restarted twice during the activation work above, and the app's already-open WebSocket connection silently died without the app noticing in time to recover. Root-caused precisely: `BackendClient.send()` (`PCorpKit`, shared with desktop) had `guard let task else { return }` with zero fallback — if `task` was `nil` (the connection had already died and been correctly nilled out by `listen()`'s own failure handler), tapping send did *categorically nothing*: no message appended, no error shown, not even a failed-looking bubble. This is broader than the earlier scenePhase fix, which only covered backgrounding/foregrounding on iOS specifically — a stale connection dying for any reason (backend restart, network drop) while the app stays open and active hits this same silent no-op, on desktop too, not just iOS. Fixed with a reconnect-on-send fallback: if `task` is `nil`, `send()` now reopens the socket first via a new `reopenSocket()` helper (extracted from `stopGenerating()`'s existing inline reconnect logic) before attempting to send — deliberately not `loadHistory()`/the launch-time fresh-conversation logic, since either would race the optimistic user-message append `send()` is about to do against an async history fetch that doesn't know about it yet. Verified live on Joshua's actual iPhone: after the backend restarts above, a send that would previously have gone nowhere silently reconnected and got a real reply. Desktop's GUI was rebuilt with the same fix and confirmed still building/running clean, but this exact failure mode wasn't separately reproduced live on desktop.

The brief's own illustrative "Active Missions: 04" stat was deliberately never added — there's no real missions-tracking system anywhere in the app (Mission Status shows one fixed hardcoded mission, "+ Mission" is a documented no-op), so there was nothing honest to count, consistent with the same "never fabricate" discipline as every other round. `WarRoomView`/`ContentView` briefly threaded a `selectedID` binding through for the map's tap-to-navigate nodes. Joshua saw it live and called it immediately: "looks very busy & cluttered." Removed the radial map entirely same session, keeping only the stats row (the specific complaint was about the nodes/lines, not the numbers) — `WarRoomCommandMap.swift` cut down to just `statsRow`, and the now-unnecessary `selectedID` threading through `WarRoomView`/`ContentView` fully reverted rather than left as dead plumbing. Verified: `swift build` clean both before and after the cut, the packaged `.app` rebuilt and relaunched each time, confirmed live by Joshua that the quieter version reads better.
- **Face-Lift item 09, "The Brief" — Frank's daily executive briefing, real new backend logic, not just a restyle.** Four sections, deliberately NOT LLM-generated, same "deterministic, no hallucination risk, no ongoing cost" reasoning already applied to `insights.py`/`situation_room.py` — every item is something the app already computes elsewhere, organized into buckets rather than invented fresh. New `backend/app/brief.py`: **What Matters** is Situation Room's existing escalated alerts plus risk-category Insights (normalized into one consistent shape server-side, since the two sources previously had genuinely different dict shapes — alerts never carried an icon/category/target section the way Insights items do); **What Frank Recommends** is opportunity-category Insights; **What Can Wait** is follow_up-category Insights; **What Changed** is real logged tool calls from the existing audit trail (`audit_db.py`, previously backend-only with no UI view) since the last time the Brief was actually opened. That "since last viewed" tracking needed one real new piece of state — a `last_brief_viewed_at` column added to `app_state` (`db.py`'s existing singleton-row table, same migration pattern already used for Focus Lock's objective columns) — `GET /brief` both reads the old timestamp for the diff and updates it to now, a deliberate state-changing read, not a side-effect-free one. Placement was a direct, considered choice given the very fresh "busy & cluttered" feedback on the command map two commits earlier: reached via a new top-bar icon button in `WarRoomView` (matching the existing "Previous conversations" pattern), opened as a popover, not a permanent new section of War Room's home screen. New `PCorpKit` types (`Brief`/`BriefItem`/`BriefActivity`, shared with iOS) and `BriefClient` — deliberately does not poll on a timer the way `InsightsCard` does, since fetching this has a real side effect and should only happen when actually opened. Verified: the real `/brief` endpoint curl-tested directly against the live database before any UI existed — first call correctly returned all 17 real historical audit entries (never viewed before), second call correctly returned zero, proving the diff-since-last-view logic works — then `swift build` (desktop) and `xcodebuild` (iOS, unaffected) both clean, backend restarted via the supervised `launchd` service, the packaged `.app` rebuilt and relaunched for Joshua to check live.
- **iOS Face-Lift parity, first pass: sidebar grouping.** Deliberately scoped to just this one piece of the multi-round desktop Face-Lift work, not an attempt at full parity in one shot — `ios/Sidebar.swift` gained the same CORE/DIVISIONS/LIFE/INTELLIGENCE/SYSTEM grouping as desktop's own `Sidebar.swift` (2026-08-20 earlier), same pure display-layer wrapper (`NavItem.items` itself untouched) plus the same `#if DEBUG` group/item-mismatch assertion. Design tokens (colors/fonts/spacing/radius) needed no separate porting at all — `PCorpKit` is shared, so iOS already inherited every Phase 1 token change automatically the moment it was made on desktop, without iOS-specific code. Explicitly still undone, not silently dropped: iOS's own system-status header (desktop's is lifted into `ContentView`'s root; iOS's `RootView` still just has a hamburger + logo), FrankOrb's real per-state motion (iOS's `WarRoomView` has no orb at all currently), the War Room stats row, and The Brief — each its own separate future round, same as how each was its own dedicated pass on desktop. Verified: both `xcodebuild` (Simulator and the physical-device target) clean, then confirmed live on Joshua's actual iPhone — a real side quest along the way: the phone wasn't reachable at first, and once connected the free-tier provisioning profile from 2026-08-12 had quietly expired (the known 7-day cycle this project already has a standing reminder for), needing Joshua to re-trust the freshly-generated profile in Settings → General → VPN & Device Management before the new build would launch at all — same first-run step as the very original setup, not a new issue.
- **iOS Face-Lift parity, second pass: the system-status header.** Near-verbatim port of desktop's `SystemStatusHeader.swift` — same 5-state logic (FRANK OFFLINE/THINKING, N ACTIONS REQUIRED, SYSTEM NOMINAL), same real signals, since everything it reads (`BackendClient`/`SituationRoomClient`, `PCorpFont.mono`, `theme.accent`, `Spacing`/`AnimationTiming`) is already shared via `PCorpKit` — no new tokens or logic needed, just the view itself. The bigger part of this pass was structural, mirroring the exact same lift already done on desktop: `BackendClient`/`SituationRoomClient` ownership moved from `WarRoomView` up to `RootView` (now `@ObservedObject`s injected into `WarRoomView` instead of `@StateObject`s it owns), so the header reads real state across every section, not just War Room, and the connection now survives navigating away from and back instead of tearing down and reconnecting every time `.id(selectedItem.id)` recreates `WarRoomView`. The iOS-specific scenePhase-based reconnect-on-foreground fix (2026-08-13's real "sent a message no response" bug) moved up to `RootView` alongside the ownership, since it's tied to whoever holds `backend` — kept, not dropped, given it fixed a genuinely real bug. Placed in `RootView`'s own `VStack`, below the hamburger/logo top bar and above the switched section content. Verified: `xcodebuild` clean for both Simulator and the physical-device target, installed and launched on Joshua's actual iPhone (same already-trusted profile from the sidebar-grouping pass earlier the same session, no re-trust needed this time).
- **iOS Face-Lift parity, third pass: FrankOrb states.** New `FrankOrb.swift` — a faithful port of desktop's Canvas/particle component (pure SwiftUI, no AppKit dependency, so nothing needed approximating) with the exact same current per-state shimmer math (idle: slow, low-amplitude `sin(t*0.5+phase)`; error: flat 0.4, no shimmer; listening: real mic level dominant, small synthetic component blended in), same Reduce Motion handling. Two real, deliberate differences from desktop's version, both because iOS's actual app state is genuinely different, not approximated: (1) no `.speaking` case — iOS has real voice *input* (`VoiceInput.swift`) but never got text-to-speech, so there's no real audio signal a speaking state could react to, same "no honest signal, don't build it" call already made for desktop's own Thinking/Executing. (2) No idle placement — desktop's orb lives in a dedicated "empty thread, not started chatting yet" screen state; iOS's `WarRoomView` layout doesn't have that as a distinct state at all (dashboard cards + chat thread are always on screen, even when the thread is empty), so wiring in an idle orb here would mean restructuring that layout first, a bigger, separate decision left for later rather than bundled into this pass. Wired into the one real, already-existing moment iOS does have: push-to-talk listening (and its error case) now takes over the dashboard/chat area with the real orb + transcript/error text, replacing what used to be just the mic button turning red. Verified: `xcodebuild` clean for both Simulator and the physical-device target, installed and confirmed live on Joshua's actual iPhone via a real push-to-talk test.
- **Face-Lift item 13, Quick Actions keyboard shortcuts — resolved, after being deferred twice.** The brief's own suggested keys (⌘M/⌘D/⌘F/⌘R) had two real conflicts, not taken as given: ⌘M is macOS's system-wide "Minimize Window," and ⌘F is both system-wide "Find" and this app's own already-reserved future-search icon (`WarRoomView`'s topBar magnifying glass, still a documented no-op) — claiming either now would fight a deeply ingrained OS convention or create rework once search is real. Swapped for ⌘N ("New Mission," the near-universal "New X" convention) and ⌘K ("Ask Frank," matching the increasingly common "jump to input/quick action" key — Slack, Linear, VS Code — and fitting since the real action is focusing the chat). ⌘D ("Start Deep Work") and ⌘R ("Run Report") kept as the brief suggested, since neither collides with anything in this app. Wired into `ContentView.swift`'s existing raw `NSEvent` keyboard monitor (same mechanism already driving Cmd+1–9/Cmd+,), reusing `PlaceholderData.quickActions`' real `targetNavTitle` for each — no new navigation logic, just new key bindings to the same honest routing already in place. `RightRail.swift`'s Quick Actions buttons gained real ⌘-key hint glyphs (`PCorpFont.mono`, muted opacity) so the shortcuts are actually discoverable, not hidden knowledge — each hint reflects a shortcut that genuinely works, not decoration. Verified: `swift build` clean, the packaged `.app` rebuilt and relaunched, confirmed live by Joshua that all four shortcuts jump to the correct section.
- **Face-Lift item 08, proactive greeting — the last open item from the original brief.** The static "I'm Frank. How can I help you today?" line, shown every time the idle War Room screen loads, is gone — replaced with a real, dynamic summary. New `attentionItems` on `WarRoomView` combines Situation Room's escalated alerts with risk-category Insights (the same two sources `app/brief.py`'s "What Matters" section already combines), fetched independently through a new `greetingInsightsClient` rather than through `GET /brief` — that endpoint has a real side effect (marking the Brief as viewed), which the greeting must never trigger as a side effect of just opening the app. When something's real, it shows "N THINGS REQUIRE YOUR ATTENTION" plus up to 3 numbered rows (label from each item's real `targetNavTitle`, detail from the real data), matching the brief's own example layout; when nothing is, it shows the brief's own exact honest copy, "Nothing requires immediate attention. Your day is clear." Real, if minor, correctness bug caught and fixed along the way: `app/brief.py`'s situation-room normalization (written earlier the same day) incorrectly assumed alerts never carry a `target_nav_title` and discarded it as `None` — building this greeting needed that same field and found the real data had been there all along; fixed to pass it through instead of fabricating a placeholder like `"URGENT"`, which was the first draft before checking. Verified: `swift build` and the backend both clean, the real live data checked directly first (`GET /insights`/`GET /situation-room` both genuinely empty right now) so the honest "day is clear" empty state was confirmed live, not just assumed. This closes out every section of the original Face-Lift brief that was scoped as a discrete build item — see the full status recap earlier in this same session for what's done/partial/deliberately skipped.
- **iOS Face-Lift parity, fourth pass: the War Room stats row.** Direct, near-verbatim port of desktop's `WarRoomCommandMap.swift` — same real Agents Online/Opportunities/Risks counts, same deliberate omission of a fake "Active Missions" number, no changes needed since it's pure `PCorpKit`-shared logic. Only real decision was placement: desktop puts this below Frank's orb in its dedicated idle-screen moment, which iOS's `WarRoomView` doesn't have (dashboard cards + chat thread are always on screen there) — placed instead right after the greeting text and before the Mission Status/Insights cards, the closest honest analog. Verified: `xcodebuild` clean for both Simulator and the physical-device target, installed and launched on Joshua's actual iPhone.
- **iOS Face-Lift parity, fifth and final pass: The Brief — closes out iOS parity with desktop entirely.** New `TheBriefSheet.swift`, same content as desktop's `TheBriefPopover.swift` (same `BriefClient`, same four sections, same "plain rows, no card boxes" restraint) but presented as a real iOS `.sheet` with `.presentationDetents([.medium, .large])` rather than a macOS `.popover` — the natural platform-idiomatic choice, since a popover would just auto-adapt into a sheet on iPhone's compact width anyway. Reached via a new document-icon button in `RootView`'s top bar, replacing what used to be an empty spacer view balancing the hamburger button's width. Real bug found live immediately after install: the sheet's content rendered as a narrow, centered column with empty space on both sides instead of filling the screen — the content `VStack` inside its `ScrollView` was sizing itself to its own intrinsic content width rather than stretching to fill the sheet, a mismatch far more visible on a full-width iOS sheet than it would be in desktop's own small fixed-width popover. Fixed with an explicit `.frame(maxWidth: .infinity, alignment: .leading)` on that VStack. Verified: `xcodebuild` clean, installed and confirmed live on Joshua's actual iPhone, including the width fix after the first live check caught it. This closes out the full iOS parity pass — sidebar grouping, system-status header, FrankOrb's listening/error states, the stats row, and now The Brief are all live on both platforms.

## 2026-08-21

- **Proactive Triggers Layer — the backend's first genuinely scheduled (not event-driven) system.** Joshua sent a full build spec (photographed from a note drafted on his phone) for a rule engine that checks Alpha Mode Media data on a schedule and proactively surfaces what needs attention, instead of everything today being reactive (only firing when Frank is directly talked to, or — for the existing Automations feature — when a specific tool call happens; `ROADMAP.md`'s 2026-08-04 entry explicitly scoped Automations as "event-driven, not scheduled," leaving this for later). Before building anything, mapped the spec's assumed schema ("Supabase — projects, invoices, deliverables, clients, tasks") against what's actually real: Supabase only has `projects`/`invoices`/`leads` (no `clients` table — client is a text field on projects; no dated `deliverables` table — just a free-text field), while `clients` and a real dated `deliverables` table exist only in local `alpha_mode.db`. Confirmed directly with Joshua: the fifth rule in the spec, stale-task ("not updated in X days"), has zero real backing data anywhere in the system — no task table anywhere tracks an `updated_at` — so it's dropped for v1 rather than faked against `due_date` (which would just duplicate the project-stage-stall rule under a different name) or built by retrofitting `updated_at` tracking onto `operations.db`'s task-mutation code, real scope beyond the rule engine itself. Delivery channel was also a real open decision — no push/Slack/email integration existed anywhere in the backend to extend — resolved with Joshua in favor of a daily email digest (new `backend/app/email_digest.py`, plain `smtplib` over Gmail SMTP + an app password in `.env`, chosen over Slack/push specifically because it needs no new account or app registration, unlike the iOS push path which would've hit the same no-paid-Apple-Developer-account wall the manual-rebuild iOS strategy already lives with). New `backend/app/triggers_db.py` (own `triggers.db`, same "genuinely separate cross-cutting concern gets its own file" precedent as `automations_db.py`, not bolted onto `db.py`'s `app_state` which is reserved for true global singleton values): a `trigger_rules` table holding each rule *type* as an enabled/disabled data row with a configurable threshold (client-contact-gap's 21-day default lives here, not hardcoded — the "new rules addable without redeploying" requirement scoped honestly to thresholds/enablement, not a full arbitrary-condition-as-data engine, which would need a query DSL nothing else in this codebase uses), and a `trigger_state` table implementing the spec's decaying-cadence dedup (day 1 → day 3 → day 7 → weekly thereafter) per flagged item, so a still-true item resurfaces on schedule instead of spamming every run, and a resolved item (invoice got paid, client got contacted) drops its state so a future recurrence starts fresh. New `backend/app/triggers.py`: four real rule checkers — invoice overdue and project-stage-stall read live Supabase (the real source of truth since 2026-08-02), client-contact-gap and deliverable-overdue read local `alpha_mode.db` (the only real source for those two) — composed into one plain-text digest, section per rule, only sections with something actually due to notify. Wired into `main.py`'s `lifespan()` as the backend's first `asyncio` scheduler loop (15-minute tick, no new dependency like APScheduler for one daily job), gated by a `digest_schedule` singleton row so it sends at most once per day no earlier than a configurable hour (default 7am). New `GET /triggers/rules` (inspect the rule rows) and `POST /triggers/run-now` (manual test path, bypasses the schedule gate but not `digest_schedule` itself) round it out. Verified: backend restarted via the supervised `launchd` service, `GET /triggers/rules` confirmed all four rules seeded correctly, `POST /triggers/run-now` ran the real rule engine end-to-end against live Supabase and local data with no errors (currently nothing overdue, so `{"sent": false, "item_count": 0}` — a clean run, not a false negative), and the digest's plain-text formatting checked directly against fake section data before wiring in the real email send. Joshua supplied his Google Workspace address (`josh@alphamode.media`) and a Google App Password same day; a real send test immediately surfaced a genuine bug in `email_digest.py`'s own new code — `EMAIL_DIGEST_TO=` present-but-blank in `.env` sets the env var to an empty string, not unset, so `os.environ.get("EMAIL_DIGEST_TO", user)` returned `""` instead of falling back to `EMAIL_SMTP_USER`, and Gmail's SMTP server rejected the empty recipient outright. Fixed with `os.environ.get("EMAIL_DIGEST_TO") or user` instead, which treats blank the same as unset. Re-verified: a direct SMTP send test succeeded and a real email arrived, then the supervised backend restarted clean with the fix in place. Separately, unrelated to this feature: a stale TextEdit window (opened before this round's `.env` edits landed) got saved over the file mid-session, silently reverting the `TAILSCALE_IP` line and the new `EMAIL_*` placeholders — caught immediately via the file-changed-on-disk system reminder, `TAILSCALE_IP` restored alongside the real email credentials rather than left dropped.
- **Triggers UI — desktop, requested right after the backend/email path was confirmed working.** Before this, the only way to see rule state was `GET /triggers/rules` via curl. Backend gained two read-only additions to support a real UI: `triggers_db.py`'s new `peek_due_status()` — same day-1/3/7-then-weekly cadence math as `items_due_for_notification()`, but never inserts or updates `trigger_state`, so loading the UI can't itself consume a cadence slot or create phantom "first flagged" state for items nobody's been notified on yet; and `triggers.py`'s new `compute_status()`, which runs every rule's live checker (even disabled ones, so the UI can show "this would still be flagging N things" instead of going blank when a rule's off) and tags each matching item `due: true/false` via `peek_due_status`. New `GET /triggers/status` (the UI's main read) and `PATCH /triggers/rules/{rule_type}` (the first UI-driven write in the whole app that isn't routed through Frank — plain settings-style state, no reason to make it conversational) round out the backend side. New shared `PCorpKit` types (`TriggerItem`/`TriggerRuleSection`/`TriggerStatus`/`TriggerRunResult`) and `TriggersClient` — the toggle uses the same optimistic-update-then-fire pattern `MemoryClient.forget()` already established for its own DELETE call. New `desktop/Sources/PCorpOS/TriggersView.swift`: a schedule row (last-sent date, "Send Digest Now" manual test button wired to the existing `POST /triggers/run-now`) plus one card per rule (enable/disable `Toggle`, live matching items, a muted "SUPPRESSED" tag on anything real but not due today) — same `RuleCard`/section-list shape `AutomationsView.swift` already established, since Triggers is architecturally its closest sibling (scheduled vs. that feature's event-driven rules). Added to the sidebar's INTELLIGENCE group right after Automations, and appended (not inserted) to `PlaceholderData.navItems` so it lands at position 10 — past Cmd+9's reach, same as Calendar/Settings already were, so no existing keyboard shortcut changed. iOS untouched, per the standing "desktop first, mobile once everything's complete" instruction. Verified: `swift build` (PCorpKit + desktop) clean, `GET /triggers/status` curl-tested directly (correctly surfaced the real $13,500 21-day-overdue Vodacom invoice with `due: false`, since the automated 7am digest had already notified on it once earlier the same day) and `PATCH /triggers/rules/{rule_type}` round-tripped both directions before any UI existed, then the packaged `.app` rebuilt and relaunched — confirmed live, matching the curl output exactly: all four rule cards rendering, the real Vodacom invoice tagged SUPPRESSED for the right reason, not a bug.
- **Currency fix: dollars → rands across every amount the backend formats.** Alpha Mode Media is a Cape Town business — Rand (R) was already the correct currency and, tellingly, already in use in one spot (`insights.py`'s quote-followup line) while every invoice-amount line elsewhere used a bare `$`, a pre-existing inconsistency this session's new `triggers.py` had unknowingly copied rather than introduced. Fixed all five real occurrences: `triggers.py` (the digest/UI's own invoice-overdue line), `insights.py` (the other invoice line), `situation_room.py` (severely-overdue line), `alpha_mode_supabase.py` (live-Supabase summary block fed into Frank's system prompt), and `alpha_mode_tools.py` (`add_invoice`'s confirmation message back to Frank). No Swift-side change needed — every currency string is backend-generated and rendered verbatim, confirmed by grepping both `desktop/` and `ios/` for hardcoded `$` literals (none found). Verified: backend restarted via the supervised `launchd` service, `GET /triggers/status` re-curled and confirmed the real Vodacom invoice now reads `R13,500.00` instead of `$13,500.00`, packaged `.app` rebuilt and relaunched.

## 2026-08-21 (continued)

- **Joshx — a completely independent second division, Phase 1 (foundation only).** Joshua sent a 28-section spec for "Joshx": his own independent freelance creative business (video editing, videography, photography), explicitly NOT a subsection/mode/workspace of Alpha Mode Media — full CRM, leads pipeline, specialized video/photo/videography production workspaces, a quote builder, invoicing, finance/analytics, rate card, availability calendar, equipment/crew databases, portfolio + portfolio intelligence, a creative lab, personal-brand content pipeline, documents, and a wholly bespoke "cinematic dark UI" visual identity — comparable in size to Alpha Mode Media's entire integration plus the whole Face-Lift brief combined. Scoped down directly with Joshua first, same discipline as every other large brief here (Face-Lift, Trading Division, Personal): confirmed **Foundation only** (nav destination + real dashboard + Clients/Leads/Projects via Frank, no quote builder/invoicing/finance/portfolio/etc. yet) and **reuse the shared theme + a distinct accent color**, not the full bespoke redesign, deferring the other 26+ sections as real named future scope rather than dropping them silently.

  **Architecture, decided before any code:** Alpha Mode Media's real financial data lives in a completely separate, actual production Supabase app Joshua runs day-to-day outside P Corp OS — Joshx has no equivalent external app, so it doesn't belong there, and a `division` discriminator column (the spec's own suggested shape) would have meant bolting Joshx onto a schema that isn't even P Corp OS's to own. Instead: a wholly new local SQLite file, `backend/app/joshx_db.py`'s `joshx.db`, same "genuinely separate domain" reasoning as `personal.db`/`automations.db`/`alpha_mode.db`'s own local tables — satisfies the spec's "never merge the two businesses' operational data" literally, not just logically.
