A WebSocket connection that goes silently dead (common on iOS after backgrounding/sleeping or a WiFi↔cellular handoff — `URLSessionWebSocketTask` doesn't always notice via its own `.receive()` callback, which is what normally triggers cleanup on failure, see `listen()`'s `.failure` case) kept being reused by every subsequent `send()` call, since `send()`'s own `if task == nil { reopenSocket() }` guard only fires when `task` is actually nil — a task that still *exists* but is dead never gets replaced, so it fails the exact same way forever until the app is force-quit and relaunched. This exactly matched what Joshua saw: the same message, the same error, twice in a row. Fixed by tearing the task down inside the send-failure handler too (`self?.task = nil; self?.isConnected = false`), so the very next `send()` call correctly reconnects instead of retrying a connection that's already dead. Rebuilt and reinstalled to the real device (`xcodebuild -allowProvisioningUpdates` + `devicectl install` + `launch`); the relaunch itself also cleared the immediate stuck state.

## 2026-08-31

- **Joshx projects became directly editable from the UI — tap a job, change its status and payment status, see client info — instead of only through a Frank chat message.** Joshua asked Frank's own Engineering Agent (its real, approval-gated codebase access, shipped 2026-08-27) to draft a Claude Code implementation prompt first; that prompt was handed off to be actually built. Given it proposed a real schema change against an already-populated production SQLite file, its claims were verified independently against the actual code (two parallel research passes) before writing anything, rather than trusted outright — this caught two real inaccuracies worth recording: (1) it claimed there was "no ALTER TABLE precedent" in this codebase justifying extra caution around the migration, when in fact this exact guarded-migration idiom (`PRAGMA table_info` check, then `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN`) already exists 8 times across `alpha_mode_db.py`/`db.py`/`operations_db.py` -- following `operations_db.py`'s own `tasks.deleted_at` migration as the template resolved this cleanly; (2) it suggested presenting the new detail view via `.sheet` on both platforms, but desktop has zero `.sheet` usage anywhere in the app -- its real, established modal convention is `.popover` (`TheBriefPopover`, `ConversationListPopover`), while iOS's is genuinely `.sheet` (`TheBriefSheet`, `ConversationHistorySheet`); building this with `.sheet` on desktop would have silently introduced a brand-new convention instead of matching what's actually there.

  **Payment status is a real, new column** (`projects.payment_status`, default `'unpaid'`) -- deliberately separate from the existing `status` field, which already covers Josh's full production pipeline (`brief` -> `pre_production` -> `production` -> `post_production` -> `client_review` -> `revision` -> `delivery` -> `paid`/`archived`, already exactly right, untouched) but can't also track payment without either losing pipeline position or being unable to reflect an early deposit. Free-text (`unpaid`/`partially_paid`/`paid`), matching this file's own "no enums enforced at the DB level" convention. Migration tested against a scratch copy of the real `joshx.db` first (idempotent -- safe to run twice -- and confirmed existing real rows correctly default to `unpaid` rather than erroring or going null) before ever touching the live file; a timestamped backup of `joshx.db` was also taken first regardless.

  **Two new id-based backend functions** (`set_project_status_by_id`/`set_project_payment_status_by_id`, `joshx_db.py`) power two new UI-facing PATCH routes (`PATCH /joshx/projects/{id}/status`, `PATCH /joshx/projects/{id}/payment-status`, `main.py`) -- deliberately NOT reusing `update_project_status`'s existing fuzzy name-matching (`_find_row_id`), since the UI already knows the exact row id it's displaying and fuzzy-matching a precise click would be a real footgun. These are the first Joshx writes not gated by Frank's own judgment, so unlike the chat-tool path they validate the incoming value server-side against the known vocabulary (400 on anything else) -- confirmed `record_tool_call` audit logging is NOT automatic for REST routes (only the websocket chat-tool dispatch loop gets it for free), so both new routes call it explicitly. A new Frank-facing `update_joshx_project_payment_status` tool was also added, mirroring `update_project_status`'s existing fuzzy-match shape exactly, so Frank can still set payment status conversationally too.

  **Client info in the new detail view needed no new backend work at all** -- a real simplification over the original prompt, which suggested a new server-side lookup: `GET /joshx/dashboard`'s response already carries the full `clients` array alongside `projects`, so the detail view just matches `project.clientName` against the already-fetched `dashboard.clients` client-side (case-insensitive, honest "no client record on file" empty state if nothing matches, since a project can exist with just a free-text client name and no linked client row today).

  New `JoshxProjectDetailPopover.swift` (desktop) and `JoshxProjectDetailSheet.swift` (iOS) -- hand-ported per platform, matching `JoshxView.swift`'s own existing precedent (no shared screen-level view lives in PCorpKit; the one exception, `SimpleMarkdownView`, is a leaf view with zero platform-specific code, which this isn't, given the two platforms' different presentation modifiers). `ProjectRow` is now wrapped in a plain-style `Button` on both platforms (no visual change to the row itself) driving a new `@State private var selectedProject`, shown via `.popover(item:)`/`.sheet(item:)` -- a deliberate small deviation from the app's existing `isPresented:`-plus-boolean convention for overlays, justified since `JoshxProject` already conforms to `Identifiable` and, unlike the existing singleton overlays (The Brief, search, conversation history), this one inherently needs to carry which specific project was tapped.

  `JoshxClient.swift` gained `updateProjectStatus`/`updateProjectPaymentStatus`, following `TriggersClient.toggleRule`'s PATCH mechanics exactly, but simpler on the local-state side: `JoshxProject` carries far more fields than `TriggerRuleSection` did, so reconstructing a whole new struct for a true optimistic update risked a copy-paste field-omission bug for little real benefit on what's an occasional deliberate edit, not a rapid-toggle control -- fires the PATCH, then refetches from the server either way, which `TriggersClient`'s own doc comment already treats as an equally-valid choice.

  Verified end to end on the backend before touching any UI: the migration tested twice (idempotency) against a scratch copy; direct calls to both new id-based setters against a real project id, confirmed reflected in `dashboard_snapshot()`, then reverted; all three REST cases curl-tested directly (invalid value -> 400, valid value -> 200 with a real DB change, nonexistent id -> 404) and confirmed both new audit rows landed in `audit.db`'s `tool_calls` table; test changes to the real "Malondie SS26" project reverted back to its actual values afterward. Both Swift targets confirmed building clean, the real desktop `.app` rebuilt via `build_app.sh` (not plain `swift build`) and relaunched, the iOS build reinstalled and relaunched on the real device -- the dashboard itself confirmed rendering correctly post-relaunch with no regression to the existing rows. Actually tapping a project and exercising the pickers live is Joshua's to click through, per the same lesson from earlier tonight about not blindly automating native-app UI input. Confirmed live afterward: Joshua tested both platforms directly and reported it works correctly.

- **Real gap found immediately after shipping the above: Joshua noticed "Malondie" still showed up as a lead even though that job has already been shot and is sitting in PROJECTS at `post_production`.** Root cause, confirmed against real data: `leads`/`projects`/`clients` are three independent tables with no structural link at all (`client_name` is plain text everywhere, no foreign key), and nothing ever closed a lead out when it converted into a real project -- `_CLOSED_PROJECT_STATUSES`'s sibling, `_CLOSED_LEAD_STAGES = {"booked", "lost"}`, already existed and correctly excluded closed leads from the "Open Leads" *count*, but the LEADS section's actual row list showed every non-deleted lead unconditionally regardless of stage -- the count was honest, the list wasn't. Joshua also asked directly to be able to delete dormant leads, which had no capability anywhere in Joshx up to this point.

  **`add_project` (`joshx_db.py`) now carries two automatic side effects, bundled into the existing upsert itself** rather than left to whichever caller invokes it (same "one real event, one function call" reasoning `people_db.py`'s `log_interaction` already uses): (1) the matching client's status flips to `active` if a `clients` row for that name exists -- a client with a real project underway is active by definition; (2) if **exactly one** non-deleted lead exists for that client, it gets auto soft-deleted -- deliberately **not** attempted when zero or more than one exist, since guessing which of several live leads for the same client just converted risks silently destroying a real, unrelated, still-open opportunity. This is forward-only: it stops the problem recurring on every future booking, but doesn't retroactively touch a lead that already went stale before this shipped -- today's actual "Malondie" lead needed (and got) a one-time manual delete via the new capability below, not a silent side effect of a code change.

  **New lead-delete capability, mirroring `operations_db.py`'s existing `delete_task` soft-delete pattern exactly**: `delete_lead` (fuzzy-match by client name, Frank-facing, new `delete_joshx_lead` tool) and `delete_lead_by_id` (id-based, for the UI, same "the UI already knows the exact row it's showing" reasoning as today's earlier `set_project_status_by_id`) plus a new `DELETE /joshx/leads/{id}` route with the same server-side validation/audit-logging discipline as the two PATCH routes shipped minutes earlier.

  **LEADS now filters out closed-stage leads from the visible list** (`booked`/`lost`), on both platforms -- a small hardcoded Swift constant mirroring `_CLOSED_LEAD_STAGES`, same pattern already used for the status-picker vocabulary in the just-shipped detail view. Deliberately a display-only filter: `dashboard_snapshot()`'s raw data and `summarize()` (Frank's own context block, which already correctly frames closed leads as "N open of M total" rather than hiding them) are untouched -- Frank's own conversational awareness of a closed lead is a real, working feature, not the bug being fixed. `LeadRow` gained a `.contextMenu` "Delete Lead" action (right-click on desktop, long-press on iOS -- one shared modifier, unlike the detail view's platform-specific popover/sheet split) gated behind a confirmation dialog, since a soft delete is DB-reversible but disappears from the UI immediately.

All test data cleaned up, confirmed real data (Malondie/Herbish/Andiswa Mngomezulu) untouched by the test run itself. - **Explicit lead→project conversion, replacing `add_project`'s ambiguous "guess which lead closed" heuristic.** Joshua asked Frank's own Engineering Agent to draft a build prompt for this plus a Joshx invoicing system, grounded directly in the real schema/tools (correctly identified `alpha_mode_db.py`'s own `invoices` table as intentional dead code — real Alpha Mode invoices live in Supabase — so Joshx's new one has no equivalent external pattern to reuse). Invoicing raised a real product trade-off worth a direct answer rather than an assumption: once real invoices exist for a project, should a manually-set payment status get silently recalculated from invoice totals the next time an invoice changes? - **Priority 2 of the same brief: real Joshx invoices, shipped the same evening once Joshua confirmed he wanted to keep going.** New `invoices` table (`project_id` a genuine FK to an existing project — unlike `client_name`/`project_name` elsewhere in this file, an invoice can't exist ahead of the project it belongs to; `amount`, `amount_paid`, a richer `status` vocabulary than projects get, `issued_date`/`due_date`/`notes`, `deleted_at` from day one for the same "everything must be deletable" reason). Confirmed directly against the code (not assumed) that `alpha_mode_db.py`'s own `invoices` table was the wrong pattern to copy — it's explicitly dead code since Alpha Mode's real invoices moved to the actual production Supabase app in 2026-08-02; Joshx has no equivalent external system, so this is genuinely Joshx's own first invoicing implementation, not a port.

  Four new functions (`add_joshx_invoice`, `update_joshx_invoice_status`, `record_joshx_invoice_payment`, `delete_joshx_invoice`) and matching Frank tools, all resolving a project by fuzzy name match and then operating on that project's *most recent* non-deleted invoice — matches how Frank will actually be told about this ("mark Acme's invoice paid," never an invoice id). `record_joshx_invoice_payment` takes the running total paid, not an increment, specifically so being told the same fact twice can't double-count.

  **The real design decision, confirmed with Joshua directly before writing any sync code**: once real invoices exist for a project, should a manually-set payment status get silently recalculated from invoice totals? Confirmed yes — invoice totals become the source of truth once they exist. New `_sync_project_payment_status_from_invoices()` runs only at the end of the four invoice-writing functions above (never on a bare read, so it can never clobber a manual override between real invoice events) and computes `unpaid`/`partially_paid`/`paid` from the real sum of `amount`/`amount_paid` across a project's non-deleted invoices — leaving `payment_status` completely untouched if a project has zero invoices at all (a project can still be marked paid by Frank's own say-so with no invoice behind it, exactly as it already worked before tonight). `update_joshx_project_payment_status`'s own tool description updated to disclose this plainly, so Frank doesn't keep offering the manual path once real invoicing exists for a project.

  **Real scope correction, confirmed and flagged to Joshua before proceeding**: Frank's critique named "CardContainer, Sidebar, RightRail, and the War Room input bar" as the glassmorphism sources (~4 places, "biggest visual delta for the least code"). The real picture, confirmed by reading all 14+ affected files directly: the exact "material blur stacked under a doubled, opacity-tinted background" pattern was independently copy-pasted across the entire app — 9 distinct variants (different materials, radii, opacities, some missing borders entirely), no shared component anywhere (`RightRail.swift`'s own `CardContainer` is `private` to that one file, and that file *itself* has a second, different inline duplicate for its own outer panel background). Joshua confirmed doing the full consistent sweep rather than just the 4 places named, once shown the real scope.

  **The actual fix uses a token that already existed but was never used**: `AppTheme.surfaceElevated` (a real "tier 2" color, e.g. dark `#15161A`) was defined for exactly the "a card sitting above the base surface" purpose, but every one of those 14+ files faked elevation via `.regularMaterial`/`.ultraThinMaterial` stacked under `theme.background.opacity(0.35)` instead of ever using it. New shared `cardSurface(radius:)` view modifier (`PCorpKit/Sources/PCorpKit/Style.swift`) replaces the 3-line hack everywhere with a flat `theme.surfaceElevated` fill + `theme.surfaceBorder` hairline — one call, existing radius literals preserved exactly (not remapped onto `Radius.*` tokens; that's a separate, later cleanup Frank's own list already named, not bundled into a glassmorphism removal pass). Sidebar's and RightRail's own *outer panel* backgrounds (not individual cards) simplified to plain `theme.surface`, no border — a full-bleed panel edge isn't a card edge. Icon badge circles across `AgentsView`/`AutomationsView`/`TriggersView`/`ContentView` got the same material-to-`surfaceElevated` swap.

  **Input bar shadow — another real correction to Frank's own framing**: the critique said the resting shadow was "already partially removed"; the actual code had a real, unconditional shadow at rest (`radius: 16, y: 6`), just smaller than the focused one (`radius: 20, y: 8`) — not absent. Fixed to be genuinely absent at rest, present only when focused, combined with the same material-to-`surfaceElevated` swap for this element.

  **Three new semantic color tokens** (`statusGood`/`statusRisk`/`statusHot` on `AppTheme`, muted to roughly the accent color's own saturation register rather than SwiftUI's full-saturation system colors) replace raw `Color.green`/`Color.red`/`.orange` at 7 confirmed real call sites across `RightRail`/`FinanceView`/`Sidebar`/`AgentsView`/`AutomationsView` (all "operating normally" indicators → `statusGood`), `WarRoomView`'s `SituationRoomBanner` (a real escalated alert, kept visually distinct → `statusRisk`), and `AlphaModeDashboardView`'s hot-lead flame icon (sales urgency, a genuinely different meaning from either → its own `statusHot` rather than being folded into a generic "warning" that doesn't otherwise exist). `AgentsView`'s inactive-state `Color.gray` also moved to the already-existing, theme-aware `theme.textSecondary`. Two additional raw-color sites turned up during the sweep (`WarRoomView`'s mic-recording red, `TriggersView`'s due-item orange) that weren't part of the 7 confirmed/approved sites — left untouched rather than silently introducing a 4th token beyond what was scoped, flagged here as a natural small follow-up instead.

Renamed rather than just changing the internal shape, since keeping a type named "Pill" that no longer produces a pill shape would be exactly the kind of name-vs-reality drift this session has caught and fixed in other code all night.

Caught by checking the iOS build directly rather than assuming a shared-file change only affects the platform it was reviewed on; fixed with the same one-line rename, iOS confirmed building clean afterward. iOS's own glassmorphism (the same 14-file pattern, independently duplicated per-platform per this app's usual convention) was deliberately *not* swept in this pass — Frank's critique and Joshua's approval were both desktop-scoped; matching iOS is a real, straightforward follow-on, not silently done here.

  Verified end to end: both Swift targets confirmed building clean after every file; a final grep across the whole desktop source tree confirmed zero remaining `.regularMaterial`/`.ultraThinMaterial`/raw status-color references anywhere the sweep touched; the real desktop `.app` rebuilt via `build_app.sh` and relaunched. One real, unplanned system dialog hit along the way: macOS prompted for Desktop-folder file access on this fresh build (the app's embedded backend reads `backend/data/*.db`, which sits under `~/Desktop/P-Corp`) — left for Joshua to click through himself rather than automated, same established rule about native system dialogs. Confirmed visually afterward via screenshot: flat opaque cards throughout with no blur, the input bar shows no resting glow, and the "+ Mission" button now renders as a rounded rectangle rather than a capsule. Clicking through the input bar's actual focus state and checking colors up close on every affected screen is Joshua's to do, per the same established lesson about not blindly automating native-app UI interaction.

## 2026-09-01

- **Anthropic API credits ran out again, same recurring pattern already documented twice this week.** Joshua reported "i cant use P Corp" with Frank returning the raw 400 credit-balance error in the UI. Confirmed root cause directly (a minimal `client.messages.create()` call against the real backend key reproduced the same error), Joshua added credits, then verified restored using the actual model the app calls (`claude-sonnet-5`, not a since-deprecated one an earlier throwaway check happened to use) rather than assuming the fix worked from the billing page alone.

- **Real bug found and fixed: a voice-triggered turn could go completely silent — no spoken reply, no visible error — when the websocket connection dropped mid-reply.** Joshua reported "voice doesn't respond" after credits were restored; mic and Speech Recognition permissions were confirmed granted (checked directly in System Settings, not assumed), and a direct `curl` against `/speak` confirmed the backend's TTS path (ElevenLabs → Piper fallback) was itself producing valid, playable audio. The actual cause: `SystemStatusHeader`'s "FRANK OFFLINE" indicator was showing at the exact moment of the failed test, meaning the websocket had genuinely disconnected — and `BackendClient.swift`'s `listen()` had two different failure paths for a dropped connection that behaved differently. The `send()`-completion-handler path (fixed 2026-08-30, confirmed working at the very start of tonight's session) already appends a visible `[connection error: ...]` message. But `listen()`'s own `.receive()` failure case — the path that fires when the connection dies while *waiting* on Frank's reply, not while sending — appended nothing at all, leaving the in-flight assistant message exactly as empty as `send()`'s own optimistic placeholder had left it. For a typed message this just reads as a blank reply bubble, easy to miss; for a voice-triggered turn specifically, `WarRoomView`'s `pendingVoiceReply` handoff hands that same empty string straight to `VoiceOutput.speak()`, which correctly no-ops on empty text — so the mic would visibly transcribe, the connection would silently die, and Frank would never audibly reply, with zero visible symptom to debug from.

  Fixed by appending the same `[connection error: lost connection to Frank mid-reply — retry your message, it should reconnect automatically]` message in `listen()`'s `.failure` case too — but only when a turn was actually in flight (`isStreaming` was true going into the failure), since this same case also fires for a plain idle disconnect with no pending reply, and appending unconditionally there would have corrupted an unrelated, already-complete past message instead of the current one.

  Verified: both Swift targets (desktop + iOS, `BackendClient.swift` is shared via PCorpKit) confirmed building clean; the real desktop `.app` rebuilt via `build_app.sh` and relaunched. Joshua retested live afterward — connection stayed up this time and the spoken reply played correctly. Separately confirmed, still open: the ElevenLabs voice key itself is returning `401 Unauthorized` (backend log), so even on a stable connection Frank's replies are currently speaking through the local Piper fallback voice rather than the real ElevenLabs one — a distinct, already-flagged issue, not the cause of tonight's silence.

- **ElevenLabs voice fixed the next day**: the `401` above turned out not to be a bad key at all — a direct call against the real TTS endpoint (bypassing the app) returned ElevenLabs' own `payment_issue` detail, "Your subscription has a failed or incomplete payment." Joshua cleared the invoice on ElevenLabs' own billing page; re-tested the same endpoint directly afterward and confirmed `200`/`audio/mpeg` (the real ElevenLabs voice, not the Piper fallback) before telling him it was fixed, rather than assuming the payment alone was proof.

## 2026-09-03

- **P Corp OS systems audit** — Joshua handed over a 20-section brief to upgrade P Corp from a dashboard/chat app into a full business OS (chat fixes, drill-down intelligence, per-project financials, investments, PDF generation, file/image understanding, web research, connectors, automations, cross-module command-centre reasoning). Per the brief's own instruction ("audit before changing UI"), ran five parallel research passes across the whole codebase rather than assuming what existed from the UI alone, and published the findings as an Artifact (47 discrete capabilities checked, each with file:line evidence) rather than just describing them in chat.

  Headline finding: the plumbing is stronger than the brief assumed — a single, unified tool-dispatch architecture (~55-60 real tools across 8 modules), genuine token-level chat streaming, real Claude vision on attached images, and working Gmail/Calendar (read-only OAuth) plus a live Supabase integration into the actual production Alpha Mode Media app all already exist, none of it visible from the UI. But the brief's actual asks — per-project revenue/profit/margin, PDF generation, non-image file upload, real web browsing, user-definable automations, and a complete (not partial) cross-module business summary — are almost entirely unbuilt: 25 of 47 checked items were flat missing, only 11 fully worked. Recommended a leverage-ordered build sequence (cheapest/most-blocking first) rather than the brief's own phase order, since the chat input's single-line `TextField` blocks half of Phase 1/2 on its own, and a couple of Phase 5 items are one-line fixes (e.g. Trading Division is the one module never included in Frank's own context-building concatenation in `main.py`).

- **Chat input rebuilt — real multiline keyboard semantics, on both platforms.** First item from the audit's recommended build order, approved to start immediately since it's the literal foundation any future attachment UI (§8 of the brief) would need to sit inside — doing it before that work avoids rebuilding the input twice. The input was a single-line `TextField` on both desktop and iOS (`WarRoomView.swift`, nearly identical on each platform): Enter always sent with zero exceptions, no way to insert a newline, no auto-grow.

  New shared `PCorpKit/Sources/PCorpKit/GrowingChatInput.swift` — deliberately built on `TextEditor`, not the newer `TextField(text:axis:.vertical)`, specifically because "must never unexpectedly send from a keyboard shortcut" needed deterministic control over the Return key, and `TextField`'s own interaction with `.onKeyPress` for overriding Return isn't something verifiable without live testing across SwiftUI versions — `TextEditor` gives unambiguous ownership of every keystroke instead, at the cost of hand-building a placeholder overlay and an auto-grow (a `GeometryReader`-based invisible measurer `Text` twin drives the visible editor's height, clamped between one line and ~6 lines before it scrolls internally). One `.onKeyPress(.return, phases: .down)` branch covers the whole spec deliberately: Shift+Return is the only case that returns `.ignored` (falls through to `TextEditor`'s own newline); everything else — plain Return, Cmd+Return, Ctrl+Return — falls into the same "call `onSend()`" branch, since the predicate only ever inspects `.shift`. No separate Cmd/Ctrl handling needed to satisfy "Cmd/Ctrl+Enter = alternative send." Paste was confirmed safe by construction, not tested-and-hoped: Cmd+V dispatches a `V`-key event, never `.return`, so multi-line clipboard content always goes through `TextEditor`'s normal insertion path untouched by the key handler.

  Qualified for a shared PCorpKit component (rather than duplicated per-platform, which is otherwise this codebase's convention for screen-level views) on the same grounds `SimpleMarkdownView.swift` already established: zero platform-specific code, only `SwiftUI` + `@Environment(\.appTheme)` + `PCorpFont`. Both `WarRoomView.swift` files had their `inputBar` `HStack` changed to `alignment: .bottom` (today's default `.center` only worked because every child was a fixed ~32pt button; a control that can now grow to 120pt needed the buttons anchored to the bottom, matching how every real chat UI handles this) and their `TextField` swapped for one `GrowingChatInput(...)` call — mic button, image picker (`NSOpenPanel` vs `PhotosPicker`), send/stop button, and the input bar's background/border/shadow styling (all shipped correctly the prior week) were untouched.

  Two real compile errors caught and fixed during the first build, not shipped blind: `InputHeightKey`'s `defaultValue` referenced `GrowingChatInput.minHeight`, which was `private` and inaccessible from a sibling type in the same file (Swift's `private` is scope-, not file-scoped) — changed to `fileprivate`; and `.onKeyPress(.return) { keyPress in ... }` resolved to the wrong overload (the zero-argument `action: () -> KeyPress.Result` variant) since the single-argument closure needs the `phases:` parameter present to disambiguate toward the `(KeyPress) -> KeyPress.Result` overload — fixed by adding `phases: .down` explicitly.

  Named, not silently accepted: iOS's on-screen keyboard has no physical Shift key, so a software-keyboard-only user has no way to trigger Shift+Return — plain Return always sends there. This is not a regression (the old single-line `TextField` couldn't do multiline on iOS at all either), but it is a real, unsolved limit of this rebuild for iOS users without a paired hardware keyboard.

  Verified: both `swift build` (desktop) and the iOS `xcodebuild` confirmed clean after fixing the two compile errors above. The real desktop `.app` rebuilt via `build_app.sh` and relaunched; screenshotted the input bar at rest and confirmed the rounded pill, placeholder text, and mic/paperclip/send button alignment all render correctly with no visual regression from the `.bottom`-alignment change. Live iOS Simulator testing (typing a multi-line string to confirm real auto-grow, tapping the on-screen keyboard's actual Return key to confirm it sends rather than inserting a newline) was planned but blocked by a one-time local Xcode configuration issue (`xcode-select` pointing at the wrong path) that needs Joshua's own `sudo` — asked him to run the fix rather than attempting any workaround, and deferred that specific check rather than skipping it silently. Desktop's actual keyboard-shortcut behavior (Shift+Enter, Cmd+Enter, multi-line paste) is Joshua's to click through live, per this project's own standing rule against driving keystrokes into a live native app via blind automation.

- **Real bug found and fixed the same night: the new chat input never gained keyboard focus on iOS at all.** Once the Xcode configuration issue above was resolved, live Simulator testing found the rebuilt `GrowingChatInput` never showed a keyboard or accepted text on iOS — confirmed it wasn't just an automation artifact (tapping the exact same coordinates correctly hit the search icon, a sheet's "Done" button, and the mic button, which correctly triggered a real Speech Recognition permission dialog; only the text control itself never responded, including to direct text injection that bypasses tap coordinates entirely). Two real, narrower bugs were caught and fixed along the way and are correct regardless of the deeper cause: the control's outer `ZStack` never claimed the HStack's remaining width the way the old `TextField` did automatically, so it had collapsed to a sliver next to the paperclip button; and the invisible auto-grow height-measurer (a `GeometryReader`-backed `Text` twin placed in `.background()`) was missing `.allowsHitTesting(false)` — `.hidden()` alone does not disable hit-testing, only rendering, so this invisible view was silently intercepting taps meant for the editor above it. Neither fix resolved the focus issue on its own, and Joshua confirmed live on his own build afterward: "keyboard doesn't come up either" — a real, user-confirmed regression, not a simulator quirk.

  Root cause: plain SwiftUI `TextEditor` + `.focused()` never reliably gains first-responder status on this iOS version once wrapped in the custom `ZStack`/background/computed-height chain this control needs — a genuine platform difference from desktop, where the identical SwiftUI code (AppKit-backed) renders and (per visual inspection) behaves correctly. Rather than keep guessing at SwiftUI's internal focus plumbing, iOS now uses a real `UIViewRepresentable`-wrapped `UITextView` instead (`#if os(iOS)` branch inside the same shared `GrowingChatInput.swift` — desktop's `TextEditor` path is untouched, since it was never reported broken and AppKit's focus behavior is a different code path entirely). This is the standard, production-proven approach for exactly this class of problem: direct ownership of `becomeFirstResponder()`/`UITextViewDelegate` instead of depending on SwiftUI's own focus binding. Return-key semantics moved to real UIKit mechanisms to match: the on-screen keyboard's Return (no physical Shift key exists there) is handled via `shouldChangeTextIn` as "always send," matching the already-documented iOS limitation; a paired hardware keyboard's Return/Cmd+Return/Ctrl+Return/Shift+Return are handled via `UIKeyCommand`s registered on a small `ChatUITextView` subclass, so Shift+Return correctly inserts a newline even with a hardware keyboard attached, and the other three correctly send.

  Verified: both platforms build clean after the rewrite. Could not get automated Simulator taps or direct text injection to conclusively confirm the new native implementation gains focus either — by this point in the session that same automation had already proven unreliable for text-focus testing specifically (while remaining completely reliable for buttons and system dialogs throughout), so a further automated "still doesn't work" reading here isn't trustworthy evidence of a real remaining bug the way Joshua's own direct test of the previous version was. Flagged explicitly rather than claimed done on unreliable automated evidence — and confirmed correct shortly after: Joshua tested the rebuilt app himself and reported the keyboard works now. Real multiline chat input (Enter to send, Shift+Enter for a newline, auto-grow, safe paste) is live and confirmed working on both platforms.

This module's own hard boundary (read-only against the trading robot's real SQLite database, `mode=ro` enforced at the connection-string level, no write path anywhere in the file) is unaffected — this only adds a read to something already read elsewhere.

- **Fifth item from the same audit, and the first genuinely architectural one: real per-project financials — revenue, expenses, profit, margin — for Joshx.** Unlike the previous four mechanical items, this one went through a full design pass first given the real schema decisions involved (full plan on file, superseded now that it's shipped). Confirmed directly against the real schema before designing anything: `projects` has `budget` (a quote/estimate) and, since 2026-08-31, real `invoices`, but nothing for expenses, profit, or margin at all.

  **Revenue is deliberately not a new column** — it's `SUM(invoices.amount)` for a project's non-deleted invoices, falling back to `budget` only when zero invoices exist yet. This is the exact precedence rule `_sync_project_payment_status_from_invoices` already established for `payment_status` ("richer data wins once it exists, don't invent something before then") — a separate `revenue` field would have created two competing numbers with a real drift risk, the same bug class this file already has repeated comments about avoiding.

  **New `expenses` table** (`backend/app/joshx_db.py`, `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` in `init_joshx_db()` — brand-new table, no guarded `ALTER TABLE` needed): `id, project_id, amount, description, incurred_date, notes, created_at, deleted_at`. Built as a real table rather than a single running-total column on `projects` for the identical reason `invoices` is a table: a project accrues multiple real line items (props, fuel, crew meals) over its life, and a single field couldn't be itemized or corrected without hand-recomputing a total. Free-text `description`, not a category enum, matching this file's own "no enums enforced at the DB level" convention.

  **Profit and margin are never stored anywhere, backend or Swift** — always `profit = revenue - expenses`, `margin = profit / revenue` (guarded against `revenue == 0`), computed fresh at the point of use so neither can ever drift from the real rows behind it.

  **New Frank-facing writes: `log_joshx_project_expense` and `delete_joshx_project_expense` only** — add-and-delete-most-recent, mirroring `add_joshx_invoice`/`delete_joshx_invoice`'s own minimal surface exactly (invoices themselves have no amount-editing tool either, only add/delete/status/payment — this isn't a new pattern). No `set_project_revenue` tool: revenue derives entirely from `invoices`/`budget`, both already writable, so a parallel setter would just be a second way to state the same fact.

  **`dashboard_snapshot()`** gained a raw `expenses` list, identical treatment to `invoices` — no computed aggregate added at this layer, same discipline already applied twice in this file. **`summarize()` (Frank's own context)** gained a raw expenses listing plus computed per-project profit lines — but only for projects with real invoice or expense activity, never for a project sitting on just a budget quote (would silently imply an unverified 100% margin). This is a deliberately smaller, different thing than the business-wide revenue rollup this file has already declined twice: "how profitable was the Malondie job" is exactly the conversational use case real invoices/expenses exist for.

  **UI**: new `expensesSection` in both `JoshxProjectDetailPopover.swift` (desktop) and `JoshxProjectDetailSheet.swift` (iOS), placed between the existing `invoicesSection` and `clientInfoSection` — a revenue/expenses/profit/margin summary line at the top (nil, not "0%", when nothing real is logged yet), then the itemized expense list with the same explicit-empty-state convention `invoicesSection` already established. New `JoshxExpense` model (`PCorpKit/Sources/PCorpKit/Models.swift`, mirrors `JoshxInvoice`'s exact shape) and `JoshxDashboard.expenses` — `JoshxClient.swift` needed no changes at all, since its plain `JSONDecoder().decode(JoshxDashboard.self, ...)` picks up the new field automatically.

  Verified in the same order the plan specified, each layer before moving to the next: direct Python test of both new functions against a throwaway test project first (insert, fuzzy-match, no-match handling, delete-most-recent, and `summarize()`'s computed profit line all confirmed correct — including a real division check: R1,000 budget, R350.50 total expenses, correctly computed as R649.50 profit at 65% margin), all test data removed and confirmed gone from `dashboard_snapshot()` before touching anything real. Both Swift targets rebuilt clean. Backend restarted (new `expenses` table created on the next `init_joshx_db()` run) and confirmed healthy. A real temporary expense (R850, "props + transport") was added to the actual "Malondie SS26" project, confirmed correctly linked via `curl` before either UI was trusted to render it; both platforms rebuilt and relaunched — Joshua confirmed live on both, "looks right" — and the test expense was deleted immediately afterward, confirmed via a second `curl` that `/joshx/dashboard` returned an empty `expenses` list again.

## 2026-09-04

- **Sixth item from the same audit: PDF document generation — Frank's first capability that produces a real file, not just data.** The audit found zero existing infrastructure at all (no PDF library, no document tool, nothing in `backend/pyproject.toml` beyond `aiosqlite`/`anthropic`/`fastapi`/`httpx`/`piper-tts`/`python-dotenv`/`uvicorn`), so this went through a full design pass — two real decisions confirmed with Joshua directly before writing any code.

  **Delivery model: desktop-first, path-in-chat, confirmed directly rather than assumed.** The backend runs locally on Josh's Mac; for v1, Frank saves the PDF to disk and tells the user the real path in his reply — no new download endpoint, no in-chat "document ready" card, no iOS delivery mechanism. iOS can only be told the file exists on the Mac. An explicit, accepted limitation, not an oversight, and one that materially changed the scope of everything downstream (no new UI work on either Swift platform for this entire feature).

  **One generic tool, not twelve template generators.** The original brief named a dozen document types (proposals, quotes, reports, SOPs, contracts, financial summaries...). The right architecture is one `generate_pdf_document(title, content, document_type)` tool — Frank already has full context (every `build_*_block()` in his own system prompt, plus every other domain's tools to pull real numbers from) and decides what a document should actually say; the tool's only job is turning well-structured markdown into a professional PDF. `document_type` is a filename/metadata label only, never branching logic.

  **Library: `reportlab`, picked after checking real dependency metadata, not assumed.** Both `reportlab` and the lighter-seeming `fpdf2` actually pull in Pillow as a real dependency (confirmed via PyPI metadata directly) — so "fpdf2 is lighter" doesn't hold, and the real decision is API fit: reportlab's Platypus layer (`SimpleDocTemplate`, `Paragraph`, `Table`, `ListFlowable`) is a flow-layout document model built exactly for "headings + paragraphs + tables + running header/footer," where fpdf2 would mean hand-rolling table layout and page-break-aware text wrapping. Pillow ships as a self-contained platform wheel (statically bundled compiled code, no system `brew install`/`dlopen`), so it doesn't reintroduce the native-dependency fragility (à la WeasyPrint's Pango/Cairo/GDK) this app's embedded-runtime packaging was specifically built to avoid.

  **Real lockfile mechanics, not a hand-edit**: added via `cd backend && uv add reportlab` (found the actual `uv` binary at `~/Library/Python/3.9/bin/uv` after the bare `uv` command wasn't on this shell's `PATH`) rather than manually editing `pyproject.toml`, since `desktop/build_app.sh` runs `uv export` against `backend/uv.lock` specifically — a hand-edit to `pyproject.toml` alone would have silently done nothing for the actual packaged `.app`. Confirmed both `pyproject.toml` and `uv.lock` updated correctly before writing any application code.

  **New `backend/app/documents.py`** — one file, not split into a `_tools.py`/`_db.py` pair like Joshx/Finance: checked every existing domain module directly and confirmed the split only exists where there's a real, reusable data-access module to separate from the tool surface (`joshx_db.py` backs both `build_joshx_block()` and the tool dispatcher). PDF generation has no SQLite table at all — same one-file shape as `legacy_vault.py`/`decision_journal.py`/`focus.py`. Contains: a deliberately minimal markdown-subset parser (`#`/`##`/`###` headings, paragraphs, `-`/`*` bullets, `**bold**`/`*italic*`, simple `| a | b |` pipe tables) where every line is classified by one of four regexes and anything unmatched just falls through to plain paragraph text — meaning there is no unparseable input, only degraded-to-plain-text input; real PDF assembly via `SimpleDocTemplate` + `onFirstPage`/`onLaterPages` canvas callbacks (reportlab's idiomatic mechanism for a repeated header/footer around a flowing document, not `PageTemplate`, which is for structurally different pages); a header embedding the real, existing `desktop/Sources/PCorpOS/Resources/p_logo_black.png` (resolved relative to `documents.py` itself, not a hardcoded machine path) and a footer with page number + generation timestamp; output saved under `backend/data/generated_documents/` (same `backend/data/` convention every existing `DB_PATH` already uses) with a slugified-title-plus-timestamp filename so repeat generations never overwrite each other.

  **Deliberate fail-soft/fail-loud split, not one blanket policy**: malformed *content* (a ragged table row missing a trailing cell, a stray `#NotAHeading` with no space) degrades to readable text rather than crashing — verified directly with a deliberately broken test document, confirmed both edge cases rendered as plain text exactly as designed, no exception raised. A genuine *file-system* failure (disk full, permissions, a real reportlab error) is caught once in the dispatch function and reported to Frank as an honest error string — unlike `trading_division.py`'s fail-soft-on-a-missing-*external*-database, a document Josh is relying on as a real deliverable shouldn't silently pretend to succeed.

  Wired into `main.py` with the exact same three-point pattern every other domain already uses (one import, one entry in the `tools=[...]` concatenation, one `elif` branch matching `DOCUMENTS_TOOL_NAMES`) — no new registration mechanism invented, and no `build_documents_block()` added, since this tool is stateless per-call with nothing ongoing to fold into every turn's system prompt.

  Verified end to end, each step before moving to the next: `python3 -c "import app.main"` confirmed clean after wiring. Generated a real test PDF with realistic content (headings, bold/italic, a bullet list, a table) and actually read the rendered output back (not just checked `file` reported a valid PDF) — logo, title, styled table with alternating row backgrounds, bullets, and the footer's page number + timestamp all confirmed rendering correctly. Then deliberately generated a second test PDF with malformed content (a ragged table row, a malformed heading) and confirmed both degraded gracefully to plain text with no exception, exactly as designed. Both test PDFs deleted immediately after.

  One real deployment wrinkle caught and fixed, not glossed over: the live backend process runs from the **embedded** Python runtime bundled inside `desktop/.build/PCorpOS.app`, not `backend/.venv` directly — `uv add` only updates the dev venv and the lockfile, so `desktop/build_app.sh` had to be re-run to actually reinstall reportlab/Pillow into the embedded runtime before a restart could pick it up (confirmed by directly checking `import reportlab` inside the embedded interpreter before restarting). A second, more mundane hiccup followed: the first restart attempt was checked for health too soon — reportlab's own import-time cost (font metrics/style-sheet initialization) made this particular startup slower than the backend's usual near-instant one, which briefly looked like a stuck/crashed process; confirmed it was genuinely still starting (not crash-looping — `runs` count in `launchctl print` wasn't climbing) rather than assuming a real bug, and a clean restart with a longer wait produced a normal, error-free startup log.

- **Real bug found and fixed: Frank felt slow and unresponsive specifically on iOS, and it wasn't the network.** Joshua reported this live. Checked the actual network path first rather than assuming it was the culprit — Tailscale (`BackendHost.host` uses the Mac's Tailscale IP for iOS, confirmed in `PCorpKit/Sources/PCorpKit/BackendHost.swift`) showed a genuine direct peer-to-peer connection to the phone (`tailscale ping` — 59ms via the local LAN, not relayed), and the backend log showed no active flood of traffic at the time. The real cause was in `ios/P Corp OS/RootView.swift`'s own `scenePhase` watcher, added 2026-08-13 for a real, different bug ("sent a message no response," documented in `WarRoomView.swift`'s own doc comment: iOS suspends network activity whenever the phone locks or the app backgrounds, silently killing the websocket while `BackendClient`'s own `task` reference stays non-nil, so its `guard task == nil` in `connect()` never noticed and never reconnected).

  The fix that shipped for that original bug was too broad: `.onChange(of: scenePhase) { _, newPhase in guard newPhase == .active else { return }; backend.disconnect(); backend.connect() }` fires on *any* transition into `.active` — but iOS also passes through `.active` after purely transient `.inactive` states that never actually suspend networking at all: Control Center sliding down, an incoming call banner, even the app-switcher gesture starting. Every one of those routine, non-disruptive moments was paying the full cost this watcher exists for — tearing down a perfectly healthy socket, opening a fresh one, and refetching the *entire* conversation history via `BackendClient.connect()`'s own `loadHistory()` call (confirmed directly in `PCorpKit/Sources/PCorpKit/BackendClient.swift` — `connect()` calls `loadHistory()` on every call after the app's first launch, unconditionally). If Frank happened to be mid-stream when this fired, the reconnect would interrupt it and the history refetch could replace what was on screen with whatever's already persisted server-side (a turn still streaming isn't saved yet) — from Josh's side, that's exactly "I glanced away for a second and Frank stopped responding."

  Fixed by using the scenePhase closure's *old* phase (already available, just unused before) as an additional gate: `guard newPhase == .active, oldPhase == .background else { return }`. This restricts the reconnect to the one case the original 2026-08-13 fix actually describes — genuinely returning from the background, where the OS really might have suspended networking — while leaving every trivial `.inactive` bounce alone, since those never touched the socket in the first place. Confirmed via grep this is the only `scenePhase` watcher anywhere in the iOS codebase, not one of several copies.

  Verified: iOS `xcodebuild` confirmed clean. This fix only reaches Josh's real iPhone once he rebuilds and reinstalls from Xcode himself (his free Apple ID's 7-day provisioning profile means there's no way to push this from here) — flagged plainly rather than claimed fixed on his device sight unseen.

- **Connected Apps status — the first UI surface for Gmail/Calendar/Supabase's real connection health.** With the original 6-item audit build order exhausted, Joshua chose this next from a short list of real candidates. The audit found all three integrations genuinely working server-side (`google_oauth.py`, `supabase_client.py`) with zero UI anywhere — `GET /auth/google/status` has existed since 2026-08-27 with zero Swift call sites on either platform, confirmed by grep before writing anything.

  New `GET /connected-apps` (`backend/app/connected_apps.py`, matching the `insights.py`/`situation_room.py` "`compute_*()` in its own module" convention) returns three independent rows, deliberately not one rolled-up boolean — Gmail and Calendar share one Google token but have genuinely different sync stories, and Supabase is a static API key with no OAuth concept at all, so collapsing these away would hide real, meaningful distinctions. **Google Calendar** uses the already-real `synced_at` column on its periodic 15-minute background cache (`calendar_db.py`, filtered to `source = 'google'` — confirmed that column and those exact values already exist via direct read). **Gmail** needed new, minimal, real tracking: Gmail only ever syncs on demand (Frank's own `search_emails` tool), never on a tick like Calendar, so a nullable `last_gmail_sync_at` column was added to the existing singleton `app_state` table (`backend/app/db.py`) via the same guarded-`ALTER TABLE` idiom already used three times there — written in `email_db.py`'s `sync_recent_emails()`, gated on `google_oauth.is_connected()` rather than "did we get new messages," since the fetch layer can't honestly distinguish "nothing new" from "API failure." **Supabase** gets a live PostgREST round-trip (`select_rows("projects", {"select": "id", "limit": "1"})`) rather than a static "is the key configured" check — a `service_role` key can be present and still revoked, and only a real request catches that; affordable specifically because this endpoint is fetched on Settings' own `onAppear`, not a 30s background poll like Insights/Situation Room.

  New `ConnectedAppStatus` model and `ConnectedAppsClient.swift` (`PCorpKit/Sources/PCorpKit`), matching `SituationRoomAlert`/`InsightsClient`'s exact existing shape. UI lands as a new `CONNECTED APPS` section inside the existing Settings screen on both platforms (not a new sidebar nav item — three rows doesn't warrant a full page), reusing the existing `SettingsSection` wrapper and adding one new row type, `ConnectedAppStatusRow`. Colors use `theme.statusGood`/`theme.textSecondary`, not `theme.statusRisk` — that token is reserved for a real escalated alert (`SituationRoomBanner`'s own doc comment), and "not connected yet" is a neutral state here, matching the exact precedent `AgentsView.swift` already established for an inactive-but-not-failed agent dot.

  **v1 is read-only status, no "Connect" button — a real, identified constraint, not laziness**: `google_oauth.REDIRECT_URI` is hardcoded to `127.0.0.1:8731`, and iOS reaches the backend over this Mac's Tailscale IP, not loopback (confirmed via `BackendHost.swift`'s own doc comment) — triggering the OAuth flow from an iPhone would open Google's consent screen fine, then fail on the redirect back to a `127.0.0.1` nothing on the phone is listening on. Named explicitly as a deferred follow-on rather than silently left out.

  Verified end to end, each layer before the next: direct Python test of the new `db.py`/`calendar_db.py` functions against the real, already-populated database confirmed the migration ran cleanly and `last_gmail_sync_at` correctly read back `None` (honest "never synced," not fabricated) before any real sync had run under the new tracking. Direct call to `compute_connected_apps_status()` against real data returned a genuine recent Calendar timestamp (confirming the 15-minute background sync is actually running) — Supabase initially came back `connected: false` in one test run, investigated rather than accepted, and traced to the test script itself not loading `.env` (main.py does; a bare script importing only `app.db`/`app.connected_apps` doesn't) — re-tested with `.env` loaded and confirmed `connected: true` with a live, fresh timestamp, ruling out a real backend bug. Both Swift targets build clean. Backend restarted and confirmed healthy; `curl` against the live `/connected-apps` endpoint returned all three rows correctly shaped. Visual confirmation on both platforms' actual Settings screens is Joshua's to do — iOS Simulator navigation to Settings itself didn't cooperate (the sidebar drawer didn't scroll down far enough under automated swipes to reach it), and the backend-level verification above already stands as the real correctness proof.

- **One more real bug caught during the end-of-night full verification pass, before signing off**: a final round of `curl` checks against every endpoint touched tonight caught `/connected-apps` genuinely reporting Supabase as `connected: false` against the *live* running server (not a test-script artifact this time — `.env` was loaded, this was the real backend). Investigated immediately rather than dismissed: two retries seconds later both came back `connected: true` with a fresh timestamp, and five more consecutive checks afterward were all clean — a one-off transient network blip, not a real outage or a wrong key. Since this exact false-alarm would read as "Supabase is broken" to anyone glancing at Settings, added a single retry (0.5s delay) to `_supabase_status()` before it reports disconnected — a genuine key revocation or outage still correctly fails twice in a row and reports disconnected honestly; a one-off hiccup no longer does. Pure backend Python change, no new dependency — restarted the backend directly (no `build_app.sh` rebuild needed; `backend_shim.c`'s `PYTHONPATH` points at this real repo's live source, not a bundled copy, so a process restart alone picks up any backend-only source change) and confirmed via 5 consecutive live checks, all `connected: true`.

- **End-of-night full verification sweep, requested directly before closing the laptop for the night.** Confirmed exactly one desktop app process and exactly one backend process running (no duplicate/zombie instances from tonight's many restart cycles); confirmed the running `.app` bundle's build timestamp is later than every source file touched tonight, i.e. it's genuinely the latest build, not a stale one from earlier in the session. Backend launchd job confirmed healthy (`state = running`, no crash-loop signature). Ran a full health check across every endpoint touched tonight: `/insights` and `/situation-room` both 200; `/joshx/dashboard` confirmed 3 real projects with zero invoices/expenses remaining (every test row created and used for tonight's verification passes was already cleaned up as it went, re-confirmed clean here); `backend/data/generated_documents/` confirmed empty (no leftover test PDFs); `/connected-apps` confirmed returning real, correct data after the retry fix above.

  **What this means concretely for tonight**: the Mac backend is a real `SMAppService`-registered background agent (Settings → Launch at Login, confirmed enabled earlier this session) — closing the laptop lid puts the Mac to sleep, which pauses the backend like every other process and resumes it automatically on wake; this is normal macOS behavior, not something that needs a fix, and nothing about tonight's work changes it. The desktop app itself will keep running with everything shipped tonight already live.

  **iOS is the one real asterisk, named plainly rather than glossed over**: everything tonight builds clean and is verified correct in the iOS *Simulator*, but Joshua's real iPhone is still running whichever build was last installed there — it does not have tonight's scenePhase reconnect fix or the new Connected Apps section until he rebuilds and reinstalls from Xcode himself (no way to push that from here). That existing installed build will keep working exactly as it has been (nothing about tonight's changes threatens it) until its own already-in-progress free-Apple-ID 7-day provisioning window ends, whenever that is — this backend has no visibility into when he last did a real-device install, so an exact expiry date can't be stated from here. If he rebuilds and reinstalls tonight to get today's iOS fixes onto his phone, that action itself starts a fresh 7-day window from today.

- **Real device deployment, discovered live to be fully scriptable — a capability this project didn't know it had.** Joshua asked to actually get tonight's iOS fixes onto his real phone before closing up. Rather than punting to "you'll need Xcode," checked whether his phone was reachable for a command-line build first: `xcrun devicectl list devices` found it connected and paired (`Joshua's iPhone`), and `xcodebuild -destination "id=<device>" -allowProvisioningUpdates build` succeeded outright — automatic signing resolved cleanly against the project's existing `DEVELOPMENT_TEAM`/`CODE_SIGN_STYLE = Automatic` configuration with no interactive prompt at all. `xcrun devicectl device install app` and `device process launch` then installed and launched it directly. This whole path (no memory of it being tried before tonight) means real-device deployment no longer needs Joshua to touch Xcode himself at all — a real, load-bearing capability upgrade, not just tonight's fix reaching his phone.

  Also answered the standing "when does this expire" question with a real number instead of a guess: read the actual provisioning profile's `ExpirationDate` directly (`security cms -D` + `plutil -extract`) rather than assuming — created 2026-08-30, expires **2026-09-06 06:00 UTC**. Tonight's install reused this same still-valid profile rather than generating a new one (confirmed no `.mobileprovision` file was newly written), so it did not reset the clock — the 7-day free-tier window keeps counting from its original creation regardless of how many times an already-valid profile gets reused for a fresh install.

- **Real bug found and fixed minutes later, live: the keyboard was dismissing itself after every single character typed on iOS, making it impossible to type a full word.** Joshua hit this immediately after the real-device install above — a direct regression from tonight's earlier `GrowingChatInput` native-`UITextView` rewrite (the fix for iOS never gaining keyboard focus at all). Root cause: `ChatTextViewRepresentable.updateUIView` (`PCorpKit/Sources/PCorpKit/GrowingChatInput.swift`) called `uiView.resignFirstResponder()` reactively whenever `isFocused.wrappedValue` (a `FocusState<Bool>.Binding`) read `false` — but `@FocusState` is only reliably kept true by SwiftUI's own `.focused()` modifier, and nothing in this UIKit-backed path ever calls `.focused()` on anything (focus is owned by real UITextView first-responder state instead). With no `.focused()` anchor, SwiftUI's own focus engine had no reason to preserve the binding's `true` value, and every keystroke (which mutates the `text` binding, re-rendering this view and re-running `updateUIView`) gave the stale/reset binding another chance to read `false` — forcing the keyboard down again, one letter at a time.

  Fixed by removing the reactive resign path entirely: `updateUIView` now only ever *requests* focus (harmless and idempotent, for the rare case something external wants to focus the field programmatically) and never forces resignation based on this same unreliable read. Real dismissal — tapping away, an explicit Done action — already happens on its own via the user's native interaction with the keyboard/responder chain; this view never needed to force it. Desktop's own `TextEditor` path (which does call `.focused(isFocused)` directly, the case `@FocusState` is actually designed for) is unaffected and wasn't part of this bug.

  Verified: both Swift targets confirmed building clean. Rebuilt for the real device via the same command-line path discovered minutes earlier and reinstalled/relaunched directly on Joshua's actual iPhone (no Simulator-only verification this time, given the bug was only ever reported live on the real device) — confirmed via the same `devicectl` install + launch flow, immediately after the fix, rather than left for a next session.

- **Real bug found and fixed, later the same night: Frank gave a connection error on every single message from the iPhone, only fixable (until now) by a full force-quit and relaunch.** Joshua's own screenshot confirmed the exact failure path: `BackendClient.listen()`'s `.receive()` `.failure` case in `PCorpKit/Sources/PCorpKit/BackendClient.swift` — the connection was dying specifically while awaiting/receiving Frank's reply, consistently, not on initial connect (the backend log showed clean `WebSocket [accepted]` entries throughout) and not on plain REST calls (situation-room polls from the same phone kept succeeding the whole time). That the connection survived a background/foreground cycle in *some* prior state but a fresh relaunch reliably fixed it, while simply foregrounding the still-running app did not, pointed at something scoped to the long-lived process itself rather than a one-off network blip — most likely either `URLSession.shared`'s pooled connection state for this host going stale after sitting backgrounded for hours, or Tailscale's own network extension not yet finished re-establishing its tunnel by the moment `RootView.swift`'s scenePhase-triggered `connect()` fired right on waking.

  Investigated a blind "just auto-resend the failed message" fix first and confirmed it would be unsafe before writing it: a dedicated research pass through `backend/app/main.py`'s `websocket_chat`/`run_claude_turn` confirmed the backend persists the user's text to the `messages` table *before* calling Claude at all, with zero de-duplication anywhere, and each received websocket text unconditionally starts a brand-new independent turn with no server-side tracking of "this turn was already in flight" — meaning a resend after a mid-reply disconnect could not only duplicate a transcript row but could re-trigger an already-executed side-effecting tool call (a second invoice logged, a second email sent) with no idempotency guard anywhere in that path. Auto-resend was ruled out on that basis, not attempted.

  What shipped instead, all in `BackendClient.swift`: (1) every connection attempt now opens on a brand-new, explicitly-invalidated `URLSession` instead of the process-wide `URLSession.shared`, removing any possibility that a degraded pooled connection from before a long background period gets silently reused; (2) `listen()`'s `.failure` case now proactively reopens a fresh socket itself (with capped exponential backoff — 1s/2s/4s/8s, giving Tailscale time to finish re-establishing its tunnel — and a hard stop at 4 consecutive attempts so a genuinely offline phone can't spin this into a battery-draining loop) instead of just nilling `task` and waiting for the *next* manual send to lazily discover it's dead, so Joshua's own manual retry now lands on an already-healthy connection on the first try; (3) a new `isIntentionalDisconnect` flag so this auto-heal never fires behind `disconnect()`'s back and steals the `task == nil` check out from under `switchToConversation`/`startNewConversation`'s own explicit reconnect; (4) a new `connectionGeneration` counter closing a real race the self-heal fix would otherwise have reintroduced — `URLSessionWebSocketTask` delivers a cancelled task's `.failure` asynchronously, so a *stale* callback for an already-superseded connection could otherwise fire after a newer `reopenSocket()` call had already installed a good task, and its old unconditional `self.task = nil` would silently undo the very reconnect this fix exists to make reliable; `listen()` now captures its task and generation at the moment it starts listening and every branch bails out silently if a newer generation has since taken over.

  Verified: PCorpKit package builds clean (`swift build`), and the full iOS app target builds clean via `xcodebuild -destination "generic/platform=iOS"`. Rebuilt and reinstalled directly onto Joshua's real iPhone via the same command-line `devicectl` path (build → install → launch), same as the keyboard fix above, rather than waiting for him to do it manually — confirmed working live ("frank responded") immediately after.

- **Next audit item, picked directly by Joshua from a short list: live tool-execution indicators — "Frank is checking your calendar..." instead of a silent pause.** The audit's §1 flagged this as an explicitly deferred gap, confirmed directly in a doc comment on desktop's `FrankOrb`: "tool-use happens invisibly inside the same `isStreaming` window... there's no honest, distinct signal for either state." Went through a full plan-mode design pass (two parallel Explore agents against `main.py`'s turn loop and both platforms' chat rendering, then a Plan agent to work out the concrete diff shape) before writing any code, given this touches the core turn loop plus both platform UIs.

  **Backend: one new sentinel at the one true dispatch choke point.** `run_claude_turn`'s tool-dispatch loop (`main.py`) already funnels every tool call — all ~24 tool-name-groups, including every `consult_X_agent` delegate — through a single `for block in final_message.content:` loop before an unconditional `record_tool_call(...)`. A new `"\n[tool_start]{json}"` sentinel (matching the exact existing `[notify]`/`[approval_request]`/`[calendar_approval_request]` convention) fires right after confirming a block is a real tool call and before the dispatch chain runs, carrying a friendly label resolved by a new `backend/app/tool_labels.py` module.

  **Labels are per tool-group (~24 entries), not per individual tool** — 60+ individual tool names exist across the codebase; group-level labels ("Checking your calendar", "Consulting the Research Agent") are honest and useful without exploding scope tonight. One real exception found and handled: `OPERATIONS_TOOL_NAMES` uniquely mixes plain CRUD (`add_task`/`update_task_status`/`delete_task`) with the actual `consult_operations_agent` delegate call in one set — special-cased ahead of the group fallback so it reads "Consulting the Operations Agent" like every other delegate, not the flat "Updating operations." No other group mixes a delegate into a CRUD set this way (confirmed via grep).

  **No `[tool_end]` sentinel needed.** The client clears the indicator the instant real text (or a `"\n[backend error: ...]"` string, which has no recognized prefix and falls into the same plain-text branch) arrives, plus on `"\n[done]"`, disconnect, a manual stop, or a fresh `send()`. Confirmed by reading `run_claude_turn`'s own caller: a thrown exception mid-tool-call sends `[backend error: ...]` and never `[done]`, so this resolves the indicator correctly with zero dedicated error-handling code.

  **Shared state lives once in `PCorpKit/BackendClient.swift`** — a new `runningTool: String?` published property following the exact existing `pendingApproval` pattern (own prefix, own `Decodable` payload, cleared in the plain-text branch, `[done]`, the `.failure` reset block, `stopGenerating()`, and the top of `send()`). UI had to be built twice, since desktop and iOS `WarRoomView.swift` are confirmed fully separate, duplicated view code, not shared.

  **UI: label appears below already-streamed text, never replaces it** — covers both "tool fires before any text" (today's bare typing-dots bubble, label added alongside) and "tool fires mid/after some text" (the exact case `FrankOrb`'s doc comment named as unsupported), since a delegate's own streamed text can already be non-empty by the time a second tool fires later in the same turn. **iOS gained `TypingIndicatorDots`** (ported verbatim from desktop) as a direct side effect — iOS's chat bubble had no animated-typing-indicator concept at all before this, just a static `"…"` literal shown while a reply was pending, a real pre-existing gap fixed here rather than built around.

  Deliberately deferred, named rather than silently dropped: per-individual-tool labels (vs. per-group); a delegate sub-agent's *own* internal tool steps (e.g. Engineering Agent's `read_file`/`propose_file_edit` while inside "Consulting the Engineering Agent") — the outer choke point only sees the delegate call as one atomic unit; `SystemStatusHeader`'s global status strip gaining a matching tier (would duplicate a signal already visible a few pixels away in the chat thread, and surface a chat-local fact on unrelated sections like Finance); and a minimum-display-duration throttle for rapid back-to-back tool calls in one turn.

  Verified end to end: both Swift targets build clean. A direct raw-websocket test against the live backend confirmed exactly one `\n[tool_start]{"label":"Checking your calendar"}` arrives before any real text for a plain calendar question, followed by a clean reply and `[done]`. A second live test specifically targeting `consult_operations_agent` (a real SOP-drafting request) confirmed the label correctly read "Consulting the Operations Agent," not "Updating operations" — validating the one real exception in `tool_labels.py` against actual delegate-call behavior, not just unit-level string matching. Backend restarted (pure Python change, no new dependency, so no `build_app.sh` rebuild needed for it) and confirmed healthy. Desktop rebuilt via `build_app.sh` and relaunched; iOS rebuilt and reinstalled onto Joshua's real device via the same `devicectl` path used earlier tonight — confirmed the phone's websocket reconnected cleanly on launch. Visual confirmation of the indicator's actual on-screen appearance on both platforms is Joshua's own to do next time he messages Frank — the protocol-level behavior (correct sentinel, correct order, correct label, correct clearing) is verified directly; driving live keystrokes into the native macOS app to self-verify visually is deliberately avoided (screenshot-only observation is safe, simulated input into a real running native app isn't).

- **Real bug found and fixed, hours later, on the same night's connection-reliability fix: the exact same "connection error" symptom recurred on iOS, this time showing TWO stacked error strings in one reply bubble** ("...lost connection to Frank mid-reply" immediately followed by "...The request timed out"). Joshua's own screenshot caught it live. Root cause: a direct, confirmed gap in the earlier fix — `BackendClient.swift`'s `send()` has its own `task.send(...) { error in ... }` completion handler (separate from `listen()`'s `.receive()` failure handler), and only `listen()`'s got the new `connectionGeneration` staleness guard. Exact failure sequence: the original `task.send()` call was still pending on an old, already-dead task; `listen()`'s `.receive()` failed first (a `.receive()` failure typically surfaces much faster than a `.send()` timeout), correctly reset state and self-healed into a fresh task under a new generation — then, later, the ORIGINAL send's completion handler finally fired with its own timeout error and, having no staleness check at all, unconditionally nilled the *fresh* task right back out from under the very reconnect that was supposed to fix things. This explains why the fix "worked" once (the first live retest, "frank responded") and then recurred hours later: the race only manifests when a send's completion is slow enough to arrive *after* the receive-side has already recovered, which depends on real network timing, not something a single quick retest would reliably hit.

  Fixed the same way `listen()` was fixed: captured `connectionGeneration` at the moment `task.send(...)` is issued, and the completion handler now bails immediately if a newer connection has since superseded it. Also added a second, narrower guard for the case where both handlers fire for the *same still-current* generation (one connection genuinely failing in both directions around the same moment) — checks `isStreaming` before acting, so if `listen()`'s failure already reported the error and reset state first, this handler no longer piles a second, redundant error string onto the same bubble; this is exactly what produced the two-stacked-errors screenshot. Also brought this handler up to parity with `listen()`'s proactive self-healing reconnect (capped backoff, `isIntentionalDisconnect`-aware), which the original 2026-09-04 fix only added to one of the two failure paths.

  Verified: PCorpKit, desktop, and iOS all rebuilt clean. Rebuilt and redeployed live to both surfaces immediately — Joshua's real iPhone via the same `devicectl` install/launch path, desktop via `build_app.sh` + relaunch — confirmed both reconnected cleanly against the running backend afterward. No way to force-reproduce the original timing-dependent race on demand to prove the fix beyond the code-level correctness argument above; flagged honestly rather than claimed conclusively verified live.

- **Next audit item, picked directly by Joshua: real document upload (PDF/DOCX/XLSX/CSV) for Frank, generalized to multi-attach with drag-and-drop on desktop.** The audit found the only attachment capability on either platform was a single image (overwritten on every new pick) — no documents, no drag-and-drop, no multi-attach, and zero PDF/DOCX/XLSX/CSV parsing dependency anywhere in the backend.

  **Tiered document handling — a real scope reduction, not a shortcut.** PDFs are sent as a native Claude `document` content block (base64 pass-through, zero local parsing) — Claude reads the real PDF natively, including embedded images and layout. DOCX (`python-docx`) and XLSX (`openpyxl`) get local text extraction into a plain text block, since neither has a Claude-native equivalent; CSV needs no new dependency at all, stdlib `csv` only. Docx tables and CSV/XLSX sheets all render as the same `| a | b |` markdown pipe-table syntax `SimpleMarkdownView` was taught to render earlier tonight — one mental model for "a table" across the codebase. New `backend/app/document_attachments.py` owns `classify_attachment()`/`build_content_block()`, which never raises — a corrupted or password-protected DOCX/XLSX, an oversized file, or an unsupported type all degrade to an honest text block Frank can relay, verified live with a deliberately truncated DOCX ("could not read attached file... may be corrupted or password-protected").

  **Wire protocol generalized from one optional image to an always-present array** (`WireAttachment`/`WirePayload` in `BackendClient.swift`, `attachments: []` never omitted so both sides always index the same shape), with the kind derived server-side only — never sent by the client, one place that mapping can drift instead of two. New nullable `messages.attachments` column (JSON, guarded `ALTER TABLE`, same idiom used twice before in `db.py`) — `image_path` stays read-only for old rows, never written again, no disruptive migration.

  **Multi-attach UI**: both platforms' single `Data?`/`mediaType?`/preview-image trio became an array of a shared `PendingAttachment` (PCorpKit), with one new shared `AttachmentChipStrip`/`MessageAttachmentsView` pair replacing what used to be independently hand-duplicated, byte-for-byte, on both platforms — including the in-sent-bubble attachment display, not just the pre-send composer chip. Desktop's `NSOpenPanel` already supports mixed types and multi-select natively — one unified "attach" trigger. iOS's `PhotosPicker` is structurally photo-library-only, so it needed a second `.fileImporter` trigger behind a small Photo/Document menu on the same paperclip glyph — a real platform divergence, not an inconsistency. Fixed a real pre-existing bug in the same pass: iOS's picker hardcoded `attachedImageMediaType = "image/jpeg"` regardless of what was actually picked; now derived properly from the item's own content type.

  **Drag-and-drop shipped for desktop only**, a deliberate, named platform decision — a decades-old macOS convention (`.onDrop(of: [.fileURL], ...)`) attached to the whole compose surface, not just the input bar, feeding the exact same `stageAttachment(from:)` the picker uses; iOS drag-and-drop is a genuinely non-standard chat-input interaction on that platform, not built this pass.

  **Two real gaps found only by testing end-to-end, neither anticipated in the design pass**: (1) Frank had no way to know attachments land directly in his own turn's context — the only attachment-related text he'd ever seen was `consult_design_agent`'s own tool description ("the actual attachment is automatically forwarded to this agent"), which reads, from his side, like that's the *only* way he ever sees one. Confirmed live: asked to read a real PDF, he answered correctly (the content genuinely was there), then on the very next turn insisted his own correct answer must have been fabricated, since he reasoned he has no direct attachment-reading capability at all. Fixed with a new static `ATTACHMENT_CAPABILITY_NOTE` appended to every turn's system prompt, telling him plainly that an attachment in his context is real and he should never disclaim one that's actually there — confirmed fixed via the identical PDF re-test immediately after. (2) uvicorn/`websockets` defaults to a 16MB max websocket frame size — `MAX_TOTAL_ATTACHMENT_BYTES` (40MB raw) inflates past that once base64-encoded, so an oversized message never reached the graceful in-band rejection text at all; it killed the whole connection first with a raw `ConnectionClosedError` ("message too big"), exactly the "confusing error deep in the stack" outcome the size caps existed to prevent. Fixed by explicitly raising `ws_max_size` server-side (`main.py`, both listener paths) and `URLSessionWebSocketTask.maximumMessageSize` client-side (`BackendClient.swift`'s shared `openTask()`, rather than trust an unverified implicit default) to a matched, comfortable ceiling on both ends.
