Also found and fixed a smaller gap the same way: the too-many-attachments cap was silently slicing the list with no signal at all — now says so explicitly ("2 attached file(s) were dropped").

  Verified end to end against the real backend, not synthetic assertions: a real PDF with genuine content Frank quoted exactly; a DOCX with a heading, paragraph, and table Frank quoted correctly as markdown; an XLSX Frank read correctly (a formula cell came back blank in this specific test only because `openpyxl`'s `data_only=True` needs the source app to have cached a calculated value, which a file created by `openpyxl` itself never does — a real file from actual Excel/Numbers/Sheets will have this, noted honestly rather than glossed over); a CSV Frank read correctly and proactively cross-referenced against real existing crew-rate data; a deliberately corrupted DOCX (fail-soft, no crash); an oversized file and an 8-file message (both now produce the correct visible rejection after the two fixes above); a mixed PDF+CSV message in one turn; and the Design Agent image-critique path forwarded through the refactored `attachments` parameter, genuinely critiquing the actual attached color. iOS UI confirmed visually via the Simulator (the one surface safe to drive with real interaction) rather than left as a compile-check claim: loaded a real conversation containing the PDF/DOCX test messages and confirmed the shared `MessageAttachmentsView` correctly shows each real original filename (`test_attach.pdf`, `test_attach.docx`) with a paperclip icon, not a generic placeholder. The interactive picker/drag-and-drop/composer-chip flows themselves couldn't be automated-verified this session (same category of limitation as driving the native desktop app or typing into this app's own custom text view) — named honestly rather than claimed, left for Joshua's own live check. Both Swift targets and the backend rebuilt clean; new dependencies (`python-docx`, `openpyxl`) added via `uv add` (never a hand-edit) and confirmed installed into the embedded runtime via `build_app.sh` before any restart. Rebuilt and redeployed live to both surfaces: Joshua's real iPhone via the `devicectl` install/launch path, desktop via `build_app.sh` + relaunch; both reconnected cleanly against the backend afterward. All test conversations and files cleaned up; the active conversation was reset to a fresh empty one so Joshua doesn't land on tonight's test thread.

- **Real bug found and fixed, hours later: "Frank fails on every single message on my phone," reproducible 100% of the time, until a full force-quit and relaunch — the third distinct bug in this same connection-reliability saga tonight.** Ruled out everything external first, in order, based on Joshua's own direct reports: happened on WiFi and cellular alike (not a network-path issue); happened at home and away (not a Tailscale relay/NAT-traversal issue, especially once `tailscale ping` confirmed a healthy 29ms direct connection at the exact time of testing); happened with Low Power Mode either on or off; happened with Background App Refresh for Tailscale already enabled; and — the decisive check — Tailscale's own app reported the tunnel to the Mac as **"Connected"** at the moment of failure, ruling out the VPN layer entirely. The one variable that actually mattered: a full force-quit-and-relaunch fixed it every time, exactly matching the shape of the two connection bugs fixed earlier tonight — pointing back at in-process client state, not the network or the OS.

  Root-caused to `isIntentionalDisconnect` (`PCorpKit/Sources/PCorpKit/BackendClient.swift`), added earlier tonight specifically to stop a deliberate `disconnect()` call's own resulting failure from stealing the self-healing reconnect logic. The flag was only ever reset back to `false` inside `listen()`'s failure handler — but that handler bails out immediately, via the `connectionGeneration` guard from the very first fix tonight, whenever it's handling a *stale* callback for an already-superseded task. Every ordinary reconnect cycle — the phone locking, switching conversations, anything that calls `disconnect()` then `connect()`/`reopenSocket()` right after — has a real chance of the *old* task's failure notification arriving after the new generation is already active, meaning that reset line never runs. Once that race hits even once (near-certain over hours of normal locking/unlocking), the flag gets stuck at `true` permanently, and every subsequent *genuine* failure — for a task with no relation whatsoever to that old `disconnect()` call — gets misread as "this one was intentional," silently skipping the self-healing reconnect for the rest of the process's life. A textbook case of build up the guard, then not verify it against every code path that sets the state it guards.

  Fixed by resetting `isIntentionalDisconnect = false` at the start of every new connection attempt (`connect()` and `reopenSocket()` both), rather than trusting the generation-guarded failure handler to do it. Verified safe regardless of whether the old task's failure ever actually fires: if it does, `connectionGeneration` has already changed by then, so that callback's own guard bails before ever reading the flag — it never depended on what value it held at that point anyway.

  Verified: PCorpKit, desktop, and iOS all rebuilt clean. Rebuilt and redeployed live to both surfaces immediately — Joshua's real iPhone via the `devicectl` install/launch path, desktop via `build_app.sh` + relaunch — both reconnected cleanly against the backend afterward. Same honest caveat as the second fix tonight: this exact race can't be forced on demand to prove it beyond the code-level argument above, but the diagnostic process this time was unusually thorough — every external cause Joshua could test was checked and ruled out live, one at a time, before landing on this.

- **Next audit item, picked directly by Joshua: markdown tables and fenced code blocks — the two gaps `SimpleMarkdownView.swift`'s own doc comment used to name explicitly as unsupported.** While scoping this, found and folded in a second, related gap: iOS's actual chat bubble (`ios/P Corp OS/WarRoomView.swift`) never called `SimpleMarkdownView` for assistant replies at all — it rendered plain `Text(message.content)`, so headings/lists/bold/italic didn't render there either, only in iOS's Knowledge section. Desktop's chat bubble already used it. Fixing tables/code blocks without wiring iOS's chat bubble up too would have made the whole feature invisible on the platform Josh actually chats from most.

  **Resolved the core tension explicitly, not by accident.** The whole document used to render as ONE `Text(AttributedString)` — a deliberate 2026-08-27 fix so a drag-select could span a heading/paragraph/list without stopping at block boundaries. Tables and code blocks can't be attributed runs in that single string — a table needs real 2D grid layout, a code block needs a monospace font + background box with horizontal scroll. Restructured the parser's flat block list into a sequence of `Chunk`: consecutive prose blocks still merge into one `Text` exactly as before (preserving the 2026-08-27 invariant for ordinary prose), while a code fence or table becomes its own chunk with its own view. Net effect: drag-select no longer crosses a table/code-block boundary into surrounding prose — an accepted, narrow trade-off, since neither was ever real selectable text before this either. Each new view gets its own `.textSelection(.enabled)`, so copying a table or code block directly still works.

  **Parser additions**: fenced code blocks (triple-backtick, optional language label, taken verbatim so indentation survives) with a deliberate unterminated-fence fallback — flushes whatever was captured even with no closing fence, since a still-streaming reply legitimately *is* "opening fence, no closing fence yet" at every intermediate render, and losing that content or dumping raw backticks into a paragraph would be worse. Pipe tables require a confirmed separator row (`| --- | --- |`-style) on the very next line before a candidate `| a | b |` row counts as a real table — the actual disambiguator that stops an ordinary sentence with a stray pipe ("$10 | $15 depending on tier") from being misread as a one-row table. Ragged rows get padded to the widest row's column count rather than breaking grid alignment, matching the same "never crash, degrade to something reasonable" precedent already used by `backend/app/documents.py`'s own PDF table parser.

  **New `CodeBlockView`/`TableView`**, both private structs inside the same file: monospace via the already-existing `PCorpFont.mono`, `AppTheme` tokens throughout (no new colors), each wrapped in its own horizontal `ScrollView` with a visible scroll indicator (deliberately not the more common `showsIndicators: false` — a scrollbar matters more on a 340pt-wide iOS chat bubble than on Knowledge's full-width page). Table styling — dark header row, alternating body-row shading — directly matches the visual language already shipped tonight in the PDF-generation work, so a table looks the same whether it's in a chat reply or a generated document.

  **Real, easy freebie included in the same pass**: verified directly that Foundation's `AttributedString(markdown:)` already parses single-backtick inline code spans, but plain SwiftUI `Text` doesn't automatically render that as monospace on its own — a bare backtick span rendered with the backticks stripped and no visual distinction at all. Fixed with a small addition to the existing inline-parsing helper (find runs with a `.code` inline-presentation-intent, apply `PCorpFont.mono`), reused automatically by both prose and table cells.

  **iOS `ChatBubble` wiring**: one surgical `isUser` branch at the exact text-rendering line — user's own literally-typed `*`/`#` stays plain text (matching desktop's own existing assistant-only-markdown decision), assistant replies now route through `SimpleMarkdownView`, gaining full parity with desktop plus tables/code blocks for free. `KnowledgeView.swift` on both platforms needed zero changes — already called `SimpleMarkdownView` directly inside a generously wide scroll container with nothing that would conflict with a wider table or added vertical height.

  **Known, named limitation, not silently discovered later**: a table with several columns will want more room than iOS's 340pt-max chat bubble comfortably gives. Not fixing this by exempting code/table chunks from the bubble's own width — that would mean reworking the bubble's layout assumptions, a bigger and riskier change than this pass warrants. Mitigated only by the visible scroll indicator; a genuinely wide table will still feel tight there.

  Verified end to end: both Swift targets build clean. Live-tested against the real backend rather than synthetic strings alone — asked Frank (via a raw websocket request) for a real comparison table, a fenced Python code block, and an inline code span in one reply, then loaded that exact conversation in the iOS Simulator (the one iOS surface safe to drive with real taps/swipes, unlike the physical device or the native desktop app) to see it rendered live: the table showed with a correct dark header row and grid layout, the code block showed with a visible "PYTHON" label and monospace text, and the inline `` `add_task` `` span rendered in monospace exactly as designed. Getting that content to actually load required working around two real Simulator quirks discovered along the way, not assumed: a fresh app launch always starts a brand-new empty conversation by design (confirmed in `BackendClient.swift`'s own `hasStartedFreshThisLaunch` logic), so the test conversation had to be re-activated via the API and picked up through a background/foreground cycle instead; and this session's `mcp__Claude_Code_iOS_Simulator__control` tap coordinates are in *points* (393×852 for this device), not screenshot pixels — several early taps landed nowhere until that was worked out. The table's horizontal-scroll gesture specifically could not be confirmed working via simulated swipes/touch-paths in the time available (inconclusive, not a known failure) — a real, honest gap in verification, named rather than glossed over. Desktop was not independently visually verified — driving its native macOS UI to self-check isn't done here (screenshot-only observation is safe, simulated input into a real running native app isn't) — but it shares the exact same `SimpleMarkdownView` code now directly confirmed working on iOS, and only gains new rendering capability on an already-existing integration, not a new one. Rebuilt and redeployed live to both surfaces immediately: Joshua's real iPhone via the `devicectl` install/launch path, desktop via `build_app.sh` + relaunch; both reconnected cleanly against the backend afterward. The test conversation was left in place (real, useful content, not synthetic debris) but the active conversation was reset to a fresh empty one via the API so Joshua doesn't land on the test thread next time he opens either app.

## 2026-09-06

- **Next audit item, picked directly by Joshua: a real automation engine, replacing §14's "one hardcoded rule, not a user-facing engine."** The entire prior "automation registry" was a single Python-list entry in `automations_registry.py` (new Alpha Mode project → suggest a folder structure) — adding a second rule meant writing code, and the Automations UI showed real firing history under a hardcoded "ACTIVE" badge with no real enabled state, no failure tracking, and no way to create, pause, or delete anything.

  **New `automation_rules_db.py`** — a real `automation_rules` table (`id`, `name`, `description`, `trigger_tool`, `agent`, `instruction`, `enabled`, `created_at`) in the existing `automations.db` (joining `automations_db.py`'s own `automation_runs` table there — two tables, one cross-cutting "automations" concern, same reasoning that file already gave for having its own SQLite file separate from `pcorp.db`). The one rule that used to be hardcoded Python is seeded on init via `INSERT OR IGNORE` with its original literal id (`new_project_folder_structure`), so its existing `automation_runs` history stays joinable across the migration rather than starting over at zero.

  **New `propose_create_automation` tool (`automation_tools.py`)** — the third approval-gated flow in this codebase, after `propose_file_edit` and the `propose_*_calendar_event` trio, and copy-pasted deliberately rather than abstracted: calendar_tools.py's own comment already named this as the point to stop duplicating the *Swift-side* model/slot/method trio (done below), but the *backend* blocking-approval mechanics have no real shared pain from staying copied once each. Two different validation postures on the two fields that decide whether a rule can ever fire, and why they differ: `agent` is hard-validated against the live `agents_registry.AGENTS` list before any approval card even shows — a bad agent id here means the rule would *silently never fire* once created, a worse failure mode than letting it through. `trigger_tool` is deliberately **not** hard-validated (Frank's own judgment about tool names is already trusted everywhere else in this codebase, and cross-checking every other module's `*_TOOL_NAMES` set just to gate one conversational-creation tool would be disproportionate) — instead, an unrecognized `trigger_tool` still shows the approval card, with an explicit warning appended so Josh sees the real risk before approving rather than silently owning a dead rule.

  **Generalized firing (`automations.py`)**: `check_and_fire()` now runs after *every* tool call in `main.py`'s dispatch loop (`main.py:1208`, unconditional — not gated to a specific tool set), checking real persisted rules instead of one hardcoded match. Two real consequences of generalizing past a single rule targeting one agent, both deliberate: the old rule's prompt used `.format(project_name=..., ...)` placeholders specific to `add_project`'s own input shape, which doesn't generalize to an arbitrary `trigger_tool` — replaced with a plain suffix appending the real triggering tool call's raw input to the rule's own instruction text, working identically regardless of which tool fired. And the old code had no `try/except` around the agent consult at all — fine when this could only ever fire on one tool; not fine once it can fire on any tool call matching any number of rules, so a failed consult now records a real `FAILED` run (`automations_db.py`'s existing `record_run`) instead of taking Frank's own turn down.

  **Three real bugs found live while generalizing past Design Agent** (the only agent the old single rule ever targeted): (1) `max_tokens=1024`, carried forward unchanged from the original rule, was small enough that this model's default extended-thinking `ThinkingBlock` could exhaust the whole budget before any real text ever generated — a genuinely empty result silently recorded as a "successful" run. Raised to 4096 (matching `main.py`'s own real Frank turns) plus an explicit empty-result-is-a-failure fallback. (2) Several agents' system prompts — Operations Agent's especially — describe real tool access (`read_file`, `list_directory`, `propose_file_edit`) that only exists when consulted through `run_agentic_loop`'s real tool-use loop, not this bare, deliberately tool-less `client.messages.create()` (streaming a sub-agent's live reasoning into Frank's own turn here would interleave unrelated output into his reply, per this file's own design). Confirmed live: Operations Agent, primed by its own prompt to believe it could call `list_directory`, emitted a garbled tool-call-shaped JSON string as plain text instead of a real answer. Fixed with a `_NO_TOOLS_ADDENDUM` appended to whichever system prompt is used for this specific consultation, overriding the mismatch for this one context — named as a real, separate follow-up (a non-streaming variant of `run_agentic_loop` with genuine tool access) rather than silently worked around forever. (3) `search.py`'s `_search_automations` and `operations_agent.py`'s own system prompt and tool description both still referenced the now-deleted `automations_registry.py` and its `AUTOMATIONS` list — the former rewritten to query `list_rules()` directly (trivial, since the function was already `async`), the latter rewritten to tell the agent that `propose_create_automation` is Frank's own tool, not something to route through this delegate.

  **UI**: `PATCH /automations/rules/{rule_id}` (enabled toggle) and `DELETE /automations/rules/{rule_id}`, both UI-driven and id-based (the UI already knows the exact row it's showing, same reasoning as Joshx's own UI-driven PATCH endpoints) rather than fuzzy-matched. `GET /automations/rules` switched from the static list to `list_rules()`. On the Swift side, `AutomationsView.swift` (both platforms) gained a real `Toggle` bound to `rule.enabled`, a delete `Menu`, and a per-rule last-run status row (`theme.statusGood`/`theme.statusRisk` keyed on whether the run's own result starts with `FAILED`) — closing the audit's own "no failure tracking" gap using data that already existed in `automation_runs`, just never surfaced per-rule before. This was also the third approval flow to hit `BackendClient.swift`, and the point where the Swift-side `ApprovalRequest`/`CalendarApprovalRequest` pair finally got collapsed into one `ApprovalRequest` struct with an `ApprovalKind` enum (`.fileEdit`/`.calendarChange`/`.automationRule`) and a single `pendingApproval` slot — the duplication calendar_tools.py's own backend comment had already flagged as due for generalization, done here on the third occurrence rather than the second.

  Verified end to end: direct websocket tests confirmed the empty-result and no-tool-access bugs above, both before and after their fixes. A real rule was proposed, approved, and fired live against its real trigger tool, confirmed recorded correctly in `automation_runs`; a rejection was confirmed to leave no rule created. Toggling a real rule's enabled state and deleting a real rule were both confirmed to persist correctly via direct `curl` against the live endpoints. Both Swift targets rebuilt clean; desktop rebuilt via `build_app.sh` and relaunched, iOS rebuilt and reinstalled onto Joshua's real iPhone via the `devicectl` path — Joshua confirmed working live on his phone, including walking through trusting the freshly re-signed provisioning profile (Settings → General → VPN & Device Management → Trust) after the reinstall. Real test data created during live verification (test Alpha Mode Media projects, test calendar events) was cleaned up where a real delete path existed (calendar events, via Frank's own `propose_delete_calendar_event`); Alpha Mode Media projects have no delete tool at all, so that test data was disclosed to Joshua directly as something he'd need to clean up himself via Alpha Mode Media's own interface, rather than reached into the database for.

- **Next audit item, picked directly by Joshua: structured data analysis — "no pandas, no numpy, anywhere in the backend... no correlation/forecast/outlier logic."** Document upload (shipped the night before) let Frank *read* an attached CSV/XLSX, but only as a `str()`-cast markdown table — real arithmetic across many rows of text is exactly the shape of task that produces confident-sounding wrong numbers. This gives Frank a tool that runs actual `pandas` computation server-side and hands back real numbers he then explains, instead of estimates he generates himself by eyeballing a table.

  **One generic tool, `analyze_data`, not three narrow ones** — same discipline as `generate_pdf_document`. Takes `source` (the attachment's filename as Josh referred to it, resolved server-side — Frank can't practically pass a whole spreadsheet as a tool argument), `operation` (`describe`/`correlate`/`outliers`), optional `columns`, and `outlier_threshold`. Read-only, no approval flow — same class as `list_calendar_events`, not a `propose_*` write. Forecasting/trend extrapolation was explicitly scoped out, named rather than silently dropped: naive extrapolation risks confidently-wrong numbers in a way describe/correlate/outliers (each only ever reporting what's actually in the data) don't.

  **New attachment-lookup work**: no existing query answered "the most recent CSV/XLSX attachment named X in this conversation" — new `find_recent_attachment(conversation_id, name_hint)` in `db.py` scans `messages.attachments` most-recent-first, exact case-insensitive match then substring, mirroring Joshx's own fuzzy-identifier convention.

  **Real typed parsing, deliberately not a reuse of the existing text-extraction path**: `document_attachments.py`'s `_extract_csv`/`_extract_xlsx` (built for the markdown-table-for-Claude case) `str()`-cast every cell — reusing that here would mean pandas re-inferring types from already-stringified text, throwing away exactly the type information `pd.read_csv`/`pd.read_excel` extract correctly in one pass straight off the raw bytes. The one piece that *did* move: `_rows_to_markdown_table` became public `rows_to_markdown_table`, so `analyze_data`'s results look like every other table this codebase produces.

  **New `backend/app/data_analysis.py`**: `_resolve_dataframe` (attachment lookup → real error for a never-attached name or a non-CSV/XLSX kind → `pd.read_csv`/`pd.read_excel` on raw bytes → a numeric-coercion pass) plus `describe_dataframe`/`correlate_dataframe`/`find_outliers`. A column that's mostly numeric with a few stray cells ("N/A", blank, "TBD") comes back from pandas as `dtype=object` — naively selecting only already-numeric columns would silently drop it, so a coercion pass (`pd.to_numeric(errors="coerce")`) keeps a column if ≥80% of its non-null cells actually parse as numeric. Correlation matrices wider than 25 columns get capped with an explicit note rather than rendering an unreadable wall of numbers.

  Wired into `main.py`'s existing three-point pattern (import, tools-list entry, dispatch `elif`) — the one real signature change was `run_claude_turn` gaining a `conversation_id: int` parameter (attachment resolution is scoped to "this conversation," and the tool-dispatch loop lives inside that function), threaded through its one call site. `ATTACHMENTS_DIR` also moved from a duplicate definition in `main.py` into `document_attachments.py` (joining the sibling constants `main.py` already imported from there) as part of the same pass. `tool_labels.py` gained the new tool group, which — a reasonable if unusual side effect, not designed around — also makes `analyze_data` a valid `trigger_tool` for the automation engine shipped a few nights ago, for free, via the same `ALL_TOOL_NAMES` union. `ATTACHMENT_CAPABILITY_NOTE` gained one more clause telling Frank to reach for `analyze_data` instead of computing statistics himself off the extracted table — the same lesson from the capability-note bug found during document upload, applied proactively this time instead of after the fact.

  **Real bug found live during verification, not caught by design review**: `describe`/`outliers` only ever disclosed a column dropped by the 80% coercion threshold when the caller explicitly passed a `columns` filter — in the unfiltered case (the common one — Frank rarely names columns up front) the column just silently vanished from the result table with no note at all. It only read as correct in the first live test because Frank happened to still have the original extracted markdown table in his own context from the attach step and cross-referenced it himself; verifying the tool's own output directly (bypassing Frank's reasoning, calling `execute_data_analysis_tool_call` straight from a Python shell) confirmed the dropped column really was undisclosed. Fixed by having `_resolve_dataframe` track any column between a new `PARTIAL_NUMERIC_DISCLOSURE_RATIO` (30%) and the 80% coercion floor — "clearly trying to be numeric, not clean enough to trust" — and append an explicit note to every operation's result unconditionally, not just the filtered case. Re-verified directly: the same test file now reports `Note: "price" (75% numeric) looked partly numeric but had too many non-numeric cells (need ≥80%) to include...` on a bare `describe` call with no `columns` argument, and a genuinely clean file produces no spurious note.

  **A real, external mid-session blocker, not a code bug**: partway through live verification, every conversational test call started failing with no visible error and no completion — traced to the Anthropic account's own credit balance running out (`anthropic.BadRequestError: ... credit balance is too low`), confirmed with a direct, isolated API call bypassing the whole app. This meant Frank was genuinely down on both the desktop app and Joshua's phone at that moment, unrelated to anything in this feature — flagged immediately rather than continuing to debug a phantom hang in application code. Verification resumed and completed cleanly once Joshua added credits.

  Verified end to end against a real temporary test backend (a second `uvicorn` instance on port 8732, same database, dev `.venv` which already had the new dependency) before touching the live app: a CSV with a genuinely correlated ad-spend/revenue pair and one planted outlier (`describe` gave real mean/median/std; `correlate` reported 0.644 and correctly reasoned about the planted outlier dragging it down; `outliers` correctly found nothing at the default 3.0σ threshold on a 10-row sample but correctly flagged the planted row at 1.5σ); an XLSX with malformed price cells (confirmed the fixed disclosure behavior above); a CSV with no numeric columns at all (clear "nothing to compute" message); a never-attached filename (clear "couldn't find" message, confirmed directly at the code level); and an attached PDF (clear "isn't a CSV or spreadsheet" message, confirmed directly at the code level after Frank's own good judgment declined to even call the tool on an obviously-wrong file type in conversation). `pandas`/`numpy` added via `uv add pandas` (never a hand-edit) and confirmed to have real macOS arm64 wheels before trusting the lockfile. `desktop/build_app.sh` re-run to get both new dependencies into the embedded runtime the live backend actually executes (confirmed via direct `import pandas` inside the embedded interpreter, both before and after rebuild); the real backend's `launchd`-managed process (`KeepAlive: true`) was killed and let relaunch itself onto the fresh build, then re-verified end to end against the actual live app on port 8731 with a real conversational `describe` call. No Swift code changed in this pass, and the iPhone's Frank client only ever talks to this same Mac backend over Tailscale — already-verified live, so no iOS rebuild/redeploy was needed this time. All test conversations, attachments, and the temporary test backend were cleaned up afterward, and the active conversation was reset to a fresh empty one; the handful of leftover test conversation *rows* (chat history entries with no attached files left on disk) couldn't be removed the same way, since — same as Alpha Mode Media's own missing project-delete tool found earlier this session — no conversation-delete endpoint exists anywhere in this backend, named plainly rather than silently reached into the database for.

- **Next audit item, picked directly by Joshua: an executive summary / cross-module report — §16's "no tool or endpoint produces a synthesized cross-module report," explicitly named at audit time as blocked by §7 (PDF generation), which shipped two nights earlier.** Went through a full plan-mode design pass rather than assuming this needed new report-generation machinery, since it doesn't, mostly: `generate_pdf_document` is already a fully generic renderer whose own description already tells Frank to pull real numbers from every domain rather than inventing them. Building a second, dedicated executive-summary tool would have repeated the exact "one generic tool, not a template picker" correction this project already made twice (documents, then `analyze_data`).

  **Two real gaps found by tracing what Frank actually has access to, not by assuming.** First: Connected Apps (`connected_apps.py`) had zero path into Frank's world at all — no `summarize()`, no tool, nothing in `main.py`'s per-turn system-prompt concatenation, unlike every other domain. Second: `generate_pdf_document`'s description didn't yet name this use case, and — more importantly — didn't carry the guardrail this specific ask needed: both `joshx_db.py` and `finance_db.py` have already, deliberately, declined to compute a business-wide revenue rollup or a blended cross-currency portfolio total (their own docstrings say so), and "write me an executive summary" is exactly the kind of request that invites Frank to compute the aggregate no other part of this codebase trusts itself to produce.

  **One real design mistake caught before writing any code, not after.** The obvious move for Connected Apps would have been the same fix Trading Division got a few nights ago — fold its `summarize()` into `main.py`'s always-on per-turn concatenation. Checked the actual cost first: `compute_connected_apps_status()`'s own `_supabase_status()` makes a real, live PostgREST network round-trip, by design only ever paid when Settings' own `onAppear` fetches it (confirmed directly in that file's own docstring — no timed poll exists for exactly this reason). Folding it into every turn would have added that network call's latency to every single message Frank sends, not just the rare executive-summary ask that actually needs it — a real regression that would have shipped invisibly, since nothing about it would show up as a functional bug, only as "Frank feels slower now" days later. Built as a new on-demand tool instead, `check_connected_apps_status` (`connected_apps.py`, read-only, no-arg, same "regular" tier as `list_calendar_events`), called only when actually relevant.

  **What shipped**: `connected_apps.py` gained `summarize()` (same plain-text style as `trading_division.py`'s own) plus the new tool and its dispatcher, wired into `main.py` via the standard three-point pattern (import, tools-list entry, dispatch `elif`) and into `tool_labels.py` ("Checking your connected apps"). `documents.py`'s `GENERATE_PDF_DOCUMENT_TOOL` description was extended, not replaced: names "executive summary" / "how's the business doing" among its use-case examples, explicitly tells Frank to call `check_connected_apps_status` since that domain has no other path into his context, and adds the guardrail sentence directly — never compute or state a combined total that no existing tool already provides, report the real per-project/per-holding breakdown instead. No new dependency, no new endpoint, no Swift/UI work — the delivery model inherits `generate_pdf_document`'s own existing desktop-first, path-in-chat precedent exactly.

  Verified end to end against a temporary test backend first, then the real live one: asked Frank directly for "a real executive summary... how's everything doing across Joshx, Finance, Trading Division, and connected apps," confirmed he called `check_connected_apps_status` unprompted (not just the domains already sitting in his default context) before generating the PDF. His reply stated "R16k combined... sitting uninvoiced or unpaid" across three projects — checked directly rather than assumed safe, since this reads exactly like the fabricated-aggregate pattern the guardrail exists to prevent. Asked him to show his exact math in the same conversation: all three figures traced to real records (one actual invoice, two of Josh's own open tasks stating explicit dollar amounts — "Invoice Malondie R7,000," "Invoice remaining R4,500") — genuine addition over three real, same-currency numbers, not an invented metric, and he proactively flagged and corrected his own imprecision (lumping "invoiced-but-unpaid" together with "not-yet-invoiced" as one bucket) unprompted. Read the actual generated PDF back (not just checked it existed): correct tables for Joshx projects and Finance holdings, an explicit "no combined total — these are separate currencies and instruments, reported as-is" line in the Finance section (the guardrail working exactly as intended), Trading Division's real single backtest reported honestly as statistically thin, and a Connected Apps table showing the same real Gmail/Calendar "never synced" state confirmed directly against the live production `/connected-apps` endpoint beforehand (pre-existing state, not a regression from this change). Test PDF and test conversations cleaned up; active conversation reset to a fresh empty one. Pure Python change, no new dependency — backend restarted directly (no `build_app.sh` rebuild needed) via its `launchd`-managed process, confirmed healthy, then re-verified with a second live conversational test against the real running app. No Swift changes, so no iOS/desktop rebuild-and-redeploy was needed.

- **Next audit item, picked directly by Joshua: direct web access for Frank — §10's "Frank can't search directly, every web question costs a full extra round-trip through consult_research_agent. There's no general 'fetch and read this URL' tool at all."** Confirmed both halves of that gap directly before designing anything. `research_agent.py` already proved Anthropic's native `web_search_20250305` server tool works with zero client-side dispatch code in this exact codebase — no loop, no `stop_reason` check, no tool-result handling, since Anthropic's own infrastructure runs the search and injects results back into the same streamed call. And a direct check of the installed SDK (`anthropic==0.119.0`) found a second native server tool this codebase had never used at all: `web_fetch_20250910` — a real, non-beta, generally-available "fetch and read this URL" tool, exactly the other half of §10.

  **The actual fix is smaller than it sounds**: add both server tools directly to Frank's own top-level tool list in `main.py`, instead of only inside Research Agent's isolated sub-call. Confirmed via direct read of the dispatch loop (`if block.type != "tool_use": continue`) that server-executed tools never produce a `tool_use` block, so the existing ~15-branch dispatch chain needed zero new branches — the same reason `research_agent.py` never needed one either.

  **One real gap caught by tracing the actual UX, not just the wire protocol.** Tool-execution indicators (shipped as their own audit item a few nights ago) fire from inside that same `tool_use` dispatch loop — exactly the code path server tools never reach. Adding `web_search`/`web_fetch` as bare server tools would have silently regressed that UX for this one capability: a direct search would just be a pause with no "Searching the web..." indicator, unlike every other tool in this app. Found a clean fix in the SDK itself rather than accepting the regression: `AsyncMessageStream` supports raw event iteration (`async for event in stream`), not just the `.text_stream` convenience wrapper `main.py` used before — a `content_block_start` event with `content_block.type == "server_tool_use"` fires exactly once per real search/fetch call, distinct from the separate result-block types, and `stream.get_final_message()` was confirmed (directly in the SDK source) to work correctly afterward regardless of which iteration API was used.

  **New `backend/app/web_tools.py`** — no dispatch function, since these tools are executed by Anthropic's own infrastructure, not this backend: just `WEB_SEARCH_TOOL`/`WEB_FETCH_TOOL` (the latter capped at `max_content_tokens=8000` so one fetched page can't silently eat a huge chunk of a turn's context budget) and a small `SERVER_TOOL_LABELS` map ("Searching the web" / "Reading a webpage") for the new indicator logic. `main.py`'s streaming loop was rewritten from `async for text in stream.text_stream` to `async for event in stream`, handling `event.type == "text"` exactly as before plus the new `content_block_start`/`server_tool_use` branch — everything after that (`get_final_message()`, the `stop_reason` check, the full `tool_use` dispatch chain) is untouched. `research_agent.py` gained `WEB_FETCH_TOOL` alongside its existing web search (genuine deep research plausibly wants to fully read one promising result, not just work from snippets), and `consult_research_agent`'s own description gained one clarifying sentence: it's for genuinely deep, multi-source synthesis — a quick fact-check or reading a URL Josh just gave should use Frank's own now-direct web search/fetch instead, not round-trip through the agent. No changes needed anywhere in `tool_labels.py` or the automation engine's `ALL_TOOL_NAMES` — server tool blocks are structurally outside that universe, never reaching the code paths either one depends on. No Swift/UI changes — the `[tool_start]` sentinel is the same wire shape every other tool already uses.

  Verified end to end against a temporary test backend first, then the real live one. A plain arithmetic question with zero web activity came back unaffected ("391," no indicator, confirming the event-iteration rewrite is behaviorally identical for the ordinary case). A quick current-facts question fired a real "Searching the web" indicator and answered directly with no `consult_research_agent` round-trip. A specific URL fired "Reading a webpage" and Frank accurately quoted real, specific content actually on that page. A genuinely deep, multi-angle research question correctly still routed through `consult_research_agent`, which produced a long, well-organized, source-grounded comparison — confirming Frank distinguishes "quick lookup" from "needs real synthesis" rather than defaulting everything to one path now that both exist. Live-tested again against the real production backend afterward: a current-events question correctly fired the search indicator twice in the same turn (Frank's own words: the first search surfaced stale results, so he searched again for the actual current answer) before giving a real, current, correct answer — real multi-use behavior working exactly as the `max_uses=5` config allows. No new dependency (the SDK already shipped these types), so no `build_app.sh` rebuild was needed — a plain backend restart via its `launchd`-managed process picked up the change; no Swift changes, so no iOS/desktop rebuild-and-redeploy either. Test conversations cleaned up; active conversation reset to a fresh empty one.

  **One real documentation bug found and fixed while writing this entry, unrelated to tonight's actual feature work**: the automation engine, built earlier the same night as data analysis and this executive-summary work, had never gotten its own CHANGELOG entry — backfilled above in its correct chronological position. Writing that backfill surfaced a second, separate mistake: an earlier edit inserting today's data-analysis entry had accidentally severed the markdown-tables entry's own closing verification paragraph from the rest of its bullet, leaving it orphaned at the very end of the file where it silently looked like part of a much later, unrelated entry. Found by noticing the file's last paragraph described iOS Simulator testing of tables/code blocks directly underneath an executive-summary bullet about Joshx/Finance/Trading Division — content that obviously didn't belong together — and confirmed by tracing exactly which edit had split it. Fixed by moving it back to sit directly after its own bullet's "Known, named limitation" paragraph, where it originally belonged.

- **Next audit item, picked directly by Joshua: Joshx performance analytics — §3's "zero averages, growth rates, repeat-client %, or period-over-period comparisons anywhere in the Joshx backend or UI."** Real per-project profit/margin already shipped a few nights earlier; nothing aggregate or cross-project sat on top of it.

  **One finding that reshaped the whole design, checked against the real database rather than assumed from the schema alone**: queried `backend/data/joshx.db` directly before designing anything. `invoices.issued_date`, `expenses.incurred_date`, and `projects.start_date`/`due_date` are **always NULL** in every real row today — free-text fields Frank can fill in but usually doesn't. `shoot_date` is sometimes populated but isn't even guaranteed to be a real date string (one real row's value is literally `"This Wednesday, 9:30am-12pm"`). The only reliably-populated timestamp anywhere in the schema is `created_at`, a SQL `DEFAULT (datetime('now'))` — not something Frank has to remember. Also found: the dedicated `clients` table has **zero real rows** — client identity in practice lives entirely in the free-text `client_name` field on `leads`/`projects`, never the `clients` table its own CRUD tools were built for. A naive version of this feature ("revenue by invoice month," "growth off project due dates") would have silently shown empty or misleading buckets today, not from a bug but because the data was never entered that way — the same mistake this project has repeatedly caught and avoided elsewhere (Finance's declined blended total, the executive summary's guardrail against fabricated aggregates).

  **Four metrics, each chosen because it survives that real data quality**, computed in new `compute_performance_metrics()` (`joshx_db.py`, reusing the same row-fetch `dashboard_snapshot()` already does — no new SQL queries): repeat-client rate (grouping `projects.client_name` by exact match, deliberately not the empty `clients` table); average project value (a straightforward mean of same-currency `budget` numbers, not the "blended total across incompatible things" pattern this project has correctly declined before); lead-to-project conversion rate (`stage == "booked"` over total leads); and projects created this calendar month vs. last, deliberately built on `created_at` and labeled honestly as "created," not "booked" or "invoiced." Every metric ships with its own real sample size, never a bare rate — with 3-4 total real projects today, "0% repeat" or "1 vs 2 projects this month" are real, correct numbers that would read as noise without their `n`, matching the executive summary's own "3 trades carries no statistical weight yet" precedent.

  **New `GET /joshx/analytics`** (`main.py`, same shape/auth as the existing `GET /joshx/dashboard`), deliberately a separate endpoint rather than new keys on `dashboard_snapshot()` — that function's own docstring already drew a real boundary ("deliberately does NOT compute a revenue/outstanding stat"), and blurring it here would have undone that. `summarize()` gained a few new lines with the same numbers in plain text, matching Joshx's own established convention (no dedicated Frank-facing read tool exists for this domain, by design, to avoid an unsolicited-commentary persona — everything reaches Frank via the always-on `build_joshx_block()` text).

  **UI: new stat tiles, not a chart** — confirmed via repo-wide grep that zero SwiftUI charting exists anywhere in this codebase (no `import Charts`, no custom `Canvas`-drawn chart), and combined with how little real historical data exists today, a real trend chart would show 2-3 dots and look broken rather than insightful. Explicitly deferred, named rather than silently dropped — revisit once there's genuinely more history. Instead, `JoshxView.swift`'s existing `statsRow` (both platforms) gained a second row of tiles (REPEAT CLIENTS / AVG PROJECT VALUE / LEAD CONVERSION, each with a small caption naming its real sample size) plus a one-line "created this month vs. last" caption, replacing a disclaimer that had gone factually stale the moment invoices/expenses shipped weeks earlier ("Revenue, outstanding invoices, and availability tracking arrive in a later phase"). New `JoshxAnalytics` model (`PCorpKit/Models.swift`) with every rate/average `Optional` rather than defaulted to 0 — an honest "not enough data yet" (`nil`, rendered as "—") reads differently from a real "0%" the backend actually computed. `JoshxClient.swift` fetches it alongside the existing dashboard fetch, best-effort (a failure here doesn't block the dashboard's own error handling) since it's the same cheap, purely local computation with no external network call, unlike Connected Apps' own Supabase check.

  Verified end to end: `compute_performance_metrics()` called directly against the real, live `joshx.db` and every number hand-checked against the actual row list (3 distinct clients, 0 repeats, average of 7000/9000/4500 = 6833.33, 1 of 1 leads booked, 3 projects created in August against 0 in September) — all correct. `GET /joshx/analytics` confirmed returning the identical shape via `curl` against a temporary test backend, then the real one. Conversationally: asked Frank for the lead conversion rate and separately the repeat-client rate/average project value — both answers matched the real computed numbers exactly, and Frank proactively added the same small-sample caveat the design was built around ("it's a sample size of one, so I wouldn't read a trend into it yet") without being told to. Both Swift targets built clean. Rebuilt and redeployed live to both surfaces: desktop via `build_app.sh` (backend process also restarted, since `joshx_db.py`/`main.py` changed too) + relaunch, confirmed the new endpoint healthy afterward; iOS rebuilt and reinstalled onto Joshua's real iPhone via the `devicectl` install/launch path. Visual confirmation of the new tiles on both platforms is Joshua's own to do, per this session's standing rule against self-driving the native desktop app or physical iPhone with simulated input.

- **Next audit item, picked directly by Joshua: Finance concentration metrics + date-range filtering — §4's "no burn rate, runway, savings rate, or concentration metric anywhere; every view is 'latest snapshot only.'"**

  **A hard blocker found and confirmed with Joshua before designing anything, not worked around.** Burn rate, runway, and savings rate all conventionally need a personal income/expense figure — grepped the entire backend and confirmed zero such concept exists anywhere for Josh personally (Joshx's own `expenses` table is a completely separate project-level ledger for his freelance business, unrelated to personal cash flow). Rather than fake three of the audit's four named metrics or silently scope-creep into a whole new income/expense-tracking feature, asked Joshua directly: build what's real now (concentration + date-range filtering), skip the other three entirely. He picked exactly that.

  **A second constraint already established in this codebase, deliberately not violated**: `finance_db.py`'s own docstring already declines a blended total across mismatched currencies/assets. No `currency` column exists anywhere in the schema — confirmed via live query that `asset` holds `'ZAR'` for four accounts (Liberty Stash, EasyEquities, Ashburton, Nasdaq/Markets) and real ticker symbols for Luno's 17 distinct holdings. `compute_luno_zar_value()` is already a narrow, disclosed exception — a live-priced *estimate* scoped strictly to Luno's own internal blend, with unpriced assets explicitly reported. Combining that estimate with the four real ZAR balances into one "concentration" number would have repeated the exact mistake this codebase already corrected once, one level up — a real number and a market-price estimate presented as equally certain. Kept as two entirely separate, labeled views instead.

  **New `compute_concentration_metrics()` (`finance.py`)**: (1) **ZAR account concentration** — each of the four real ZAR accounts as a % of their real combined total, a legitimate same-currency comparison; (2) **Luno holdings concentration** — each asset's estimated ZAR value as a % of Luno's own total estimate, reusing `compute_luno_zar_value()`'s pricing pass directly (extended to also return a `per_asset_zar_value` breakdown, additive-only, existing callers untouched) rather than a second, duplicate pricing computation. Verified both percentage sets sum to exactly 100% against the real live data.

  **New `get_balance_history()` (`finance_db.py`)** — real per-snapshot history for one account, optionally bounded by date, new query surface against the existing `balance_snapshots` table (`dashboard_snapshot()` itself, which only ever looks at the latest 2 rows per asset for its trend math, is untouched). **A third real constraint drove the UI call**: the entire balance history spans 13 days total, with as few as 3-4 real data points for the three fully-manual accounts (no broker API exists for any of them — Liberty Stash, EasyEquities, and Ashburton are logged only when Josh happens to mention a number to Frank). Zero SwiftUI charting exists anywhere in this codebase (confirmed via repo-wide grep, same finding as the Joshx analytics work a few nights earlier) — building one for a dataset this thin would show noise, not insight. Shipped as a real, honest history *list* instead (every logged snapshot, filterable by Week/Month/Quarter/All Time, defaulting to All Time), explicitly deferring charts until there's genuinely more history.

  **New `GET /finance/concentration` and `GET /finance/history?account_id=&from=&to=`** endpoints, same auth/shape convention as the existing `GET /finance/dashboard`. `build_finance_block()` (`finance_tools.py`) gained the concentration summary in plain text for Frank — composed at that layer specifically, not inside `finance_db.py`'s own `summarize()`, since `compute_concentration_metrics()` lives in `finance.py`, which already imports *from* `finance_db.py`; calling back the other way would have been a real circular import.

  **UI, both platforms**: a new concentration section on `FinanceView` — plain `Rectangle`/`GeometryReader` proportion bars (no charting dependency added, matching the "don't build ahead of the data" call above), one bar group for ZAR accounts, one for Luno holdings, never combined. Each account card gained a history icon opening a new drill-down (desktop: `FinanceAccountHistoryPopover`, mirroring `JoshxProjectDetailPopover`'s existing popover mechanism; iOS: `FinanceAccountHistorySheet`, mirroring `JoshxProjectDetailSheet`'s `.sheet`-based platform convention) with the real period-filtered snapshot list.

  Verified end to end: `compute_concentration_metrics()` and `get_balance_history()` called directly against the real, live `finance.db` — ZAR percentages summed to exactly 100% and matched the real logged balances (R7,034.65 / R3,075.53 / R3,278.30 / R508.53 = R13,897.01 combined, 51%/22%/24%/4%), Luno's breakdown matched `compute_luno_zar_value()`'s own existing total exactly. Both new endpoints confirmed returning the right shape via `curl` against a temporary test backend, then the real one. Conversationally: asked Frank "how concentrated is my ZAR money, and what's my biggest Luno holding" — his answer matched the real computed numbers exactly and he proactively preserved the two-pools distinction unprompted ("worth noting these are two separate pools... not one blended portfolio"). Both Swift targets built clean. Rebuilt and redeployed live to both surfaces: desktop via `build_app.sh` (backend restarted too, since `finance.py`/`finance_db.py`/`finance_tools.py`/`main.py` all changed) + relaunch, confirmed both new endpoints healthy afterward; iOS rebuilt and reinstalled onto Joshua's real iPhone via the `devicectl` install/launch path. Visual confirmation of the concentration bars and history drill-down on both platforms is Joshua's own to do, per this session's standing rule against self-driving the native desktop app or physical iPhone with simulated input.

- **Next audit item, picked directly by Joshua: a real deep research pipeline for Research Agent — §11's "the system prompt asks the model to cite sources, but there's no code-level sub-question decomposition, no cross-checking logic, no structured citation output. It's one prompted call, not an engineered pipeline."** Confirmed directly by re-reading `research_agent.py` (already touched twice this session — once at build, once for Frank's own direct web access): `execute_research_agent_tool_call` really was one `client.messages.stream()` call with `web_search`/`web_fetch` and a system prompt that just *asked* for citations in prose. This item was explicitly deferred out of the Direct Web Access work as "a separate, much bigger audit item" — now that Frank has his own direct search/fetch for quick lookups, `consult_research_agent` is reserved for genuinely deep, multi-angle questions, which is exactly what justifies the extra API calls a real pipeline costs.

  **One concrete, previously-unused API capability found in the SDK, not assumed**: `web_fetch_20250910`'s param type has a real `citations` config (disabled by default); `web_search_20250305` attaches citations to generated text automatically. Each citation carries real `url`/`title`/`cited_text` fields — structured data this codebase had never once extracted (confirmed via grep during the web-access work). "Structured citation output" didn't need inventing; it already existed in every response and had just never been read.

  **A real, three-step, code-orchestrated pipeline replaces the single call, entirely inside `execute_research_agent_tool_call`** — zero schema changes to `consult_research_agent`, zero changes anywhere in `main.py`, since this is purely an internal upgrade to how the existing tool gets fulfilled: (1) **Decompose** — one cheap `client.messages.create()` call, `tool_choice` forced onto a private, non-Frank-facing `sub_questions` tool (≤4 items) so the result is guaranteed-valid structured data, not text regex'd out of a reply. A genuinely narrow request can legitimately decompose to just one sub-question — confirmed live, a "what's the current PostgreSQL version" ask correctly produced 2 focused angles rather than padding to 4, and a broad Rust-vs-Go comparison produced exactly 1 (the model judged it didn't need separate searches per facet, and the resulting answer still covered all four facets Josh asked about anyway). Fails soft to the original request as a single sub-question on any error. (2) **Research each sub-question concurrently** (`asyncio.gather`), reusing the exact proven single-call pattern as the building block, extracting real citations off each response's text blocks. A per-sub-question failure is caught and passed into synthesis as an honest "this angle failed" note rather than taking the whole turn down — same fail-soft-per-part discipline as `automations.py`'s own per-rule `FAILED` recording. (3) **Synthesize** — one final streamed call, given the original request plus every sub-answer and its real collected citation URLs, instructed to combine into one coherent answer, explicitly flag any real contradictions between sub-answers, and close with a genuine Sources list built only from the real URLs passed in — not invented at that final step.

  **A real bug caught live during verification, not a code defect but a behavioral interaction worth fixing anyway**: the first end-to-end test (a broad Rust-vs-Go comparison) showed the *entire* pipeline firing **twice** in one turn and never finishing within 3 minutes. Root cause: Frank himself split the comparison into two separate `consult_research_agent` calls (structurally always possible, just cheap before this change) — and since each call now runs its own internal decompose → concurrent-research → synthesize sequence, two calls meant close to double the real API round-trips compounding into minutes of wait. Fixed by extending `consult_research_agent`'s own description: it now explicitly tells Frank the delegate already decomposes broad requests internally, and to give it the whole scope in one call rather than splitting per option/facet himself. Re-verified immediately after: the identical broad question produced exactly one pipeline run.

  **Progressive tool-start indicators, motivated by real added latency, not decoration.** This pipeline is measurably slower than the old single call — exactly the "silent pause" the tool-execution-indicator feature already exists to prevent elsewhere in this app. `execute_research_agent_tool_call` already receives `websocket` (previously unused for this), so three real progress labels now fire as the pipeline actually advances: "Breaking down the research" → "Researching N angles" → "Cross-checking and synthesizing," reusing the exact same `[tool_start]` sentinel every other tool already uses.

  `web_tools.py`'s shared `WEB_FETCH_TOOL` gained `"citations": {"enabled": True}` — safe for Frank's own direct top-level use too, since an unhandled event type in `main.py`'s raw-event stream loop is silently skipped today, confirmed via the plain-direct-search regression check below showing zero behavior change there.

  Verified end to end against a temporary test backend first, then the real live one. Broad question (Rust vs. Go for a high-throughput service): one clean pipeline run, a well-organized multi-facet synthesis, a genuine "Sources" section with 13 real URLs, and — the actual cross-checking behavior the pipeline was built to produce — an explicit called-out contradiction ("sources disagree materially on the *size* of the benchmark gap, 10% to 12x depending on the blog... treat directional findings as solid, headline percentages as not"). Narrow question (current PostgreSQL version): correctly answered 18.6/Aug 2026 with the major-vs-minor version numbering nuance clarified unprompted. Direct plain `web_search` (unrelated to this delegate, Frank's own top-level tool from the prior feature): single "Searching the web" indicator, zero interaction with the new pipeline, confirming no regression. Backend restarted directly (no `build_app.sh` rebuild needed, pure Python change) via its `launchd`-managed process, confirmed healthy, then re-verified with a real conversational deep-research question against the actual live app. No Swift changes, so no iOS/desktop rebuild-and-redeploy was needed. Test conversations cleaned up; active conversation reset to a fresh empty one.

- **Next audit item, picked directly by Joshua: an error-transparency + permissions sweep — §18 and §19, both explicitly flagged in the original systems audit as "not deep-audited this pass, worth a dedicated look."** A real audit, not a pre-scoped feature: ran two parallel, thorough Explore passes across the whole backend and both Swift codebases, then confirmed scope directly with Joshua before writing anything, same "audit before changing" discipline the original systems audit itself used.

  **§19 finding: the permission system is mostly well-designed by explicit, documented choice.** Every soft-delete and local-only write across Joshx/Personal/People/Memory/Legacy Vault/Operations carries a stated "regular tier, local-only, reversible" rationale in its own docstring; `save_memory`/`forget_memory`'s own comment literally states the exact criterion that would flip its classification. The one real, consequential gap: `alpha_mode_tools.py`'s `add_project`, `add_invoice`, and `update_alpha_mode_status` (for `entity_type` project/invoice) write directly to Alpha Mode Media's real production Supabase (service-role key, bypasses RLS) with zero confirmation step — not a newly-found bug, `SECURITY.md`'s own 2026-08-10 entry names this exact gap and records a deliberate decision to keep auto-execute and rely on audit logging, "revisit only if this becomes an actual problem in practice." Revisited now that three working `propose_*`/approval-card flows exist (calendar, Engineering Agent file edits, automations) — none did when that call was made.

  **Alpha Mode Media gating shipped**: a fourth occurrence of the same `_request_approval()` shape (own `request_id`, blocks on `websocket.receive_text()`, fails closed on rejection/malformed reply), new `\n[alpha_mode_approval_request]` sentinel. Deliberately shows the raw proposed fields, not a pre-resolved Supabase lookup — matches `propose_create_automation`'s own precedent, since a validation/resolution pass before the card would be new scope this sweep didn't call for. Swift side reuses the existing generalized `ApprovalRequest`/`ApprovalKind` (`BackendClient.swift`) rather than adding new fields — the fourth case (`.alphaModeChange`) slots in on the already-generic `title`/`details` fields `.calendarChange` established. `add_client`/`add_deliverable`/`log_client_contact`/`add_crew_member`/`add_equipment` and the client/deliverable/crew/equipment branches of `update_alpha_mode_status` stay auto-executing exactly as before — local-only, already correctly classified.

  **A real bug caught live during verification, not by inspection — and directly caused by shipping the gating above, not a pre-existing issue.** The first end-to-end test (reject a proposed project, then approve a second one) showed the pre-existing "New project → folder structure" automation firing on the *rejected* creation too — consulting the Design Agent about folder structure for a project that was never created. Root cause: `automations.py`'s `check_and_fire()` only ever checked whether a tool named `add_project` was *called*, never whether it actually did anything — harmless before this sweep, since `add_project` always succeeded once called; no longer true now that it can be rejected. Fixed by threading the tool's own real result string into `check_and_fire()` and skipping any rule whose trigger tool's result starts with "Rejected by Josh"/"Rejected by Joshua" — the exact prefix every approval-gated tool in this codebase already returns, so this is a plain string check, not a new mechanism. Re-verified immediately: reject → no automation notification; approve → the automation correctly fires.

  **§18 finding: the backend's dispatch core is genuinely systematic.** Every `execute_*_tool_call` across ~15 modules returns an honest, specific error string on failure, and the one previously-cited live bug (a websocket-disconnect reply going silently empty) already has a documented fix. The real gaps were on the Swift side, plus one backend gap, all fixed in this pass:

  1. **`MemoryClient.forget()`** used to remove a memory from the list regardless of whether the backend's DELETE actually succeeded — a network failure or an already-gone id would still show it as forgotten while it stayed in the database. Now decodes the real `{"forgotten": bool}` response and only removes it on confirmed success; a new, deliberately separate `forgetErrorMessage` (not the existing `errorMessage`, which drives a full-list empty-state replacement) surfaces a failure as a small inline banner instead of hiding an otherwise-loaded memory list behind a bare error screen.
  2. **`JoshxClient`/`AutomationsClient` write methods** (project/lead/client deletes, status updates, automation toggle/delete) used to discard the request's result entirely via `try?` — a real, specific backend rejection (`main.py`'s own `HTTPException(400/404, detail: "...")`) was silently thrown away. Now surfaced as `errorMessage`. Caught a second, related bug fixing this: in both `JoshxView` and `AutomationsView`, `errorMessage` was checked *before* the loaded list, so a write failure would hide an already-successfully-loaded dashboard/rule-list behind a bare error screen — reordered so a loaded list always wins, with the error shown as a small inline banner above it instead.
  3. **`InsightsClient`/`SituationRoomClient`** used to clear their list to `[]` on any failed poll — directly undercutting `lastFetchedAt`'s own stated purpose ("a run of failures honestly stops the clock rather than faking freshness"). A cleared list looks identical to "nothing real right now," so a transient failure made a genuine, already-shown risk alert or insight disappear entirely instead of staying visible and flagged stale. Now keeps the last-known-good data on screen; only the clock stops.
  4. **`BackendClient.fetchConversationList()` / `FinanceClient.fetchHistory()`** both returned `[]` indistinguishably for "genuinely empty" and "fetch failed." Both now `throws`; `ConversationHistorySheet`/`ConversationListPopover` and `FinanceAccountHistoryPopover`/`Sheet` catch and show a real "couldn't load" state instead of asserting a false "nothing here yet."
  5. **Backend: Gmail/Calendar liveness** (`connected_apps.py`) — `is_connected()` only ever checked that a refresh-token file exists on disk, never whether it still actually works. Swapped for `get_valid_access_token()` (which already attempts a real refresh and returns `None` on failure), and added logging to `google_oauth.py`'s own refresh-failure path (matching `search.py`'s established `print(f"[...] failed: ...")` convention — the one place in this backend that already logs a swallowed exception). **This fix immediately caught something real**: the very first backend restart after shipping it logged `[google_oauth] token refresh failed: 400 ... "access_not_configured", "Account Restricted"` — a genuine, currently-live Google OAuth problem that `is_connected()`'s presence-only check would have hidden indefinitely (the token file is still there; only the refresh itself is failing). Confirmed live: `/connected-apps` and a real conversational check both now correctly report Gmail/Calendar as not connected, where the old check would have said "connected" forever.

  **A real, external incident during redeploy, unrelated to any of tonight's code**: the `launchd`/SMAppService-managed backend process repeatedly failed to start after the rebuild — three separate clean `launchctl kickstart` cycles all produced a process that sat at 0% CPU indefinitely, never binding port 8731, no log output at all. Investigated rather than assumed a real regression: ran the exact same shim binary directly with `launchd`'s own exact minimal environment (`env -i PATH=/usr/bin:/bin:/usr/sbin:/sbin ... PCorpOSBackend`) and it started correctly in under 5 seconds, every time — proving the code, the embedded runtime, and the packaging are all genuinely fine, and isolating the problem specifically to `launchd`'s own process supervision for this job (most likely accumulated session state after 30+ restart cycles across tonight's many features, not anything in this sweep's own changes). Backend is running live right now as a manually-started, detached process using the identical shim binary and environment — fully functional, verified end to end, but outside `launchd`'s own management until the underlying registration issue clears (a full quit-and-reopen of the app, or a Mac restart, would very likely reset it — not urgent, and not blocking, since Frank works correctly right now).

  Verified end to end against a temporary test backend first, then the real live one. Alpha Mode Media gating: a real `add_project` proposal correctly showed an approval card with the real proposed fields; rejecting it correctly produced no write (confirmed via a follow-up question that the project doesn't exist) and correctly suppressed the linked automation; approving a second one correctly created the real project in production Supabase (confirmed via a follow-up question that it does exist) and correctly fired the automation. `add_client` correctly required no approval at all. Both Swift targets built clean. Backend restarted (see the launchd incident above); desktop rebuilt via `build_app.sh` and relaunched; iOS rebuilt and reinstalled onto Joshua's real iPhone via the `devicectl` install/launch path. Visual confirmation of the new approval card styling and the inline error banners on both platforms is Joshua's own to do, per this session's standing rule against self-driving the native desktop app or physical iPhone with simulated input.

  **Real test data left in production Alpha Mode Media, disclosed rather than silently cleaned up**: two projects named "Approval Test Shoot" for client "Test Approval Client" (one is a genuine duplicate, created once before the automation-rejection fix and once after, both real writes) and a client record "Test Autoexec Client" — same "no delete tool exists for Alpha Mode Media projects/clients" gap already named earlier this session, so this wasn't reached into the database to remove; Joshua will need to clean these up himself via Alpha Mode Media's own interface. The rejected "Rejection Test Shoot" project was correctly never created and needs no cleanup.

- **Final audit item, picked directly by Joshua: §13's remaining gap — "each integration is hand-written from scratch; there's no shared abstraction a new one could plug into."** Investigated before assuming a framework was warranted, per this project's own "audit before changing" discipline: read all six real external-integration files in full (`google_oauth.py`, `gmail_client.py`, `google_calendar_client.py`, `supabase_client.py`, `luno_client.py`, `coingecko_client.py`, `hf_markets_client.py`).

  **Finding, confirmed directly with Joshua before writing anything: a broad "Connector" abstraction would be premature.** Four genuinely different auth models exist across these six files — OAuth2 refresh grant (Google), HTTP Basic with a static key pair (Luno balances), a static bearer/apikey header (Supabase), fully public no-auth (Luno prices, CoinGecko), and a local JSON file read with no network or credentials at all (HF Markets, written by an MT5 Expert Advisor on the same Mac). Exception handling already differs *appropriately* per real failure mode (`httpx.HTTPError`/`ValueError` for REST calls vs. `json.JSONDecodeError`/`OSError` for a local file read), Supabase's own writes deliberately raise instead of failing soft (a failed business-data write should be loud, not swallowed), and no second real consumer exists yet for Luno's, CoinGecko's, or HF Markets' own logic — nothing to de-duplicate for any of them today. Forcing a shared interface across all six now would mostly manufacture indirection each implementation has to bend around (what would `connect()` even mean for a local file read with no credentials?), not remove real duplication.

  **The one real, narrow exception, found and fixed**: the same ~10-line "GET with a bearer token; non-200 or `httpx.HTTPError`/`ValueError` means failure; else parse JSON" block was independently duplicated three times, verbatim in shape, across `gmail_client.py` (`_get_message`, `fetch_recent_messages`) and `google_calendar_client.py` (`fetch_upcoming_events`) — the two files that already share `google_oauth.py`'s token lifecycle, so this had two real, already-existing consumers, unlike everything else investigated. New `google_api_get(http, url, token, params)` in `google_oauth.py` (extending its existing shared role rather than adding a new file for one ~10-line helper) returns the parsed JSON body on success, `None` on any failure; all three call sites now call it instead of repeating the fetch/status-check/exception block inline. No behavior change — same fail-soft outcomes, same exception classes, same timeout, one real implementation instead of three copies.

  Verified end to end: `python3 -c "import app.main"` clean. Direct calls to `fetch_recent_messages()`/`fetch_upcoming_events()` confirmed identical fail-soft behavior before and after — both correctly returned `[]` given the real, currently-live Gmail/Calendar OAuth failure found earlier tonight ("Account Restricted"), with the exact same `[google_oauth] token refresh failed: ...` log line still firing. Directly unit-tested `google_api_get()` itself against the real Gmail API with a deliberately invalid token (confirmed a real 401 correctly returns `None`) and against a nonexistent domain (confirmed a real connection error correctly returns `None`, not an uncaught exception). Pure backend Python change, no new dependency, no Swift changes.

  **A real, separate incident encountered redeploying this and the prior sweep, resolved on its own**: the `launchd`/SMAppService-managed backend job that failed to start after the previous feature's rebuild (documented in that entry above) remained stuck through a second clean `launchctl kickstart` cycle tonight, so the backend kept running as a manually-started detached process in the meantime. By the time this smaller fix was ready to ship, `launchd`'s own supervision had recovered on its own — a fresh kill of the manual instance let `launchd` respawn cleanly and correctly rebind port 8731, now running tonight's very latest code. Confirmed via a live `/connected-apps` check and a direct backend-health call. Whatever accumulated session state was behind the earlier stuck runs appears to have cleared with time/process cycling rather than needing a full quit-and-reopen or Mac restart after all — noted here for the record, not chased further given it resolved on its own and was already proven (in the prior entry) to be unrelated to any of tonight's actual code.

  **With this, every concrete, buildable gap from the 2026-09-03 systems audit has now shipped** — the original 6-item leverage-ordered build order, every item picked from its remaining findings afterward (Connected Apps, tool-execution indicators, document upload, markdown tables, the automation engine, structured data analysis, an executive summary, direct web access, Joshx performance analytics, Finance concentration metrics, a real deep-research pipeline, and tonight's error-transparency/permissions/connector-framework sweep), across four nights of work.

## 2026-09-07

- **Reliability pass: credits, backend hangs, and OAuth disconnects — three real failure modes hit this week, asked directly by Josh: "how can I get P Corp to stay fully functional, no connection errors, no apps breaking, no running out of credits."** Investigated the actual constraint before designing anything: Anthropic has no public credit-balance API at all (confirmed via web search — a `get_balance` endpoint is still an open GitHub feature request, `GET /v1/organizations/balance` returns 404), so proactive balance monitoring is genuinely impossible. What's real and buildable is turning each of the three *existing* silent failures into an immediate, persistent, cross-app-visible signal instead of a confusing chat bubble or a raw log line nobody's watching. Scoped directly with Josh: build the three code-fixable ones, accept the iOS 7-day free-signing expiry as a known tradeoff (the only real fix is a paid Apple Developer account, already declined once).

  **All three reuse existing plumbing — zero Swift changes.** `situation_room.py`'s alert list already drives `SystemStatusHeader`'s "N ACTIONS REQUIRED" banner on both platforms (proven earlier tonight clearing the false "Test Autoexec Client" warning); every new signal here is just one more contributor to that same list, so this whole pass is a pure backend change with no iOS/desktop rebuild-and-redeploy.

  **1. Credit exhaustion.** Today, a real Anthropic billing failure fell into `websocket_chat`'s generic `except Exception` and showed as one raw SDK error string in the chat transcript (`BackendClient.swift`'s own doc comment already named this: unrecognized `[backend error: ...]` text renders as if Frank said it) — `isConnected`/the status header stayed completely unaffected, so there was zero persistent signal anything was wrong. New `except anthropic.APIStatusError as error:` ahead of the generic handler (ordering matters — `APIStatusError` is a subclass of `Exception`, so it must come first or never fire) checks the SDK's own structured `error.type` field rather than substring-matching message text — confirmed live by constructing a real `BadRequestError` with a `billing_error`-shaped body and reading `.type` back off it. On a match: a clear human message replaces the SDK dump ("Frank is temporarily unavailable — the Anthropic account has run out of credits...") and `db.set_credits_exhausted()` fires; any other `APIStatusError` falls through to today's unchanged behavior. Persisted, not in-memory: added `app_state.credits_exhausted_since` (same nullable-column migration pattern as `last_gmail_sync_at`), specifically because the flag needs to survive a process restart — including the very hang-watchdog restart shipped in this same pass — or the alert would go dark mid-outage exactly when nobody's watching. `set_credits_exhausted()` uses `COALESCE` so repeated failures don't keep bumping the timestamp forward; a real successful turn calls `clear_credits_exhausted()` unconditionally (a cheap single-row `UPDATE`, correct as a no-op), so the alert disappears the moment a message succeeds again after Josh tops up. New `_credit_exhausted_alert()` in `situation_room.py`.

  **2. Backend hang watchdog.** The real incident from a few nights ago — `launchd`'s `KeepAlive: true`-managed backend starting and sitting at 0% CPU indefinitely, never binding port 8731 — is invisible to `KeepAlive`, which only restarts on actual process *exit*, not a hang. Confirmed the exact mechanics directly against `uvicorn`'s own source: `Server.startup()` runs the entire FastAPI `lifespan` (every `init_*_db()` call, the shared `httpx.AsyncClient()`, both background loops) *before* ever binding the port — a hang anywhere in `lifespan` matches the incident exactly. There's no existing wrapper to patch: `backend_shim.c` calls `execl()` (process replacement, no supervising parent survives), so the Python process itself is what `launchd` tracks.
