New daemon `threading.Thread` (not an asyncio task — has to keep running even if the main asyncio loop itself is what's deadlocked; `urllib`'s blocking call releases the GIL during the real network wait, so this thread isn't starved by a stuck main thread), started in `run()` before the blocking `uvicorn.run()`/`_run_dual()` call: after a 30s startup grace period, hits the real `GET /health?token=...` endpoint every 30s — a genuine HTTP round trip (accept → ASGI dispatch → route handler → response), deliberately not just a raw TCP connect, since a bare connect only proves the kernel completed a handshake and would pass forever against a process that bound the port fine and wedged afterward. Three consecutive failures (kill fires at t=90s total, stated explicitly rather than left derivable) calls `os._exit(1)` — not `sys.exit()`, which would only raise in the watchdog thread itself and do nothing to a hung main thread — letting `KeepAlive` restart a fresh process; no zombie-port risk, since the kernel closes every FD on exit and uvicorn's socket uses `SO_REUSEADDR`. Extracted the previously-independent-literal `8731` (repeated three times) into one `BACKEND_PORT` constant shared by the real server binds and the watchdog. Explicitly accepted: this only self-heals under `launchd`; a bare dev process has nothing to restart it.

  **3. OAuth/Connected-Apps disconnection.** `connected_apps.py`'s `compute_connected_apps_status()` already does a genuine liveness check (real Google token refresh + a live Supabase round-trip) — but by existing deliberate design it's expensive and only ever called on-demand from Settings, never on a timer, specifically to avoid adding real network latency anywhere else. `situation_room.py` is polled every 30s by `SituationRoomClient.swift`; calling the live check directly from there would have reintroduced the exact per-call-latency regression already caught and avoided once for the executive-summary feature, now worse (a 30s timer instead of an occasional Settings visit). Instead: piggybacked on the existing 15-minute `_trigger_scheduler_loop()` tick (already running three unrelated `maybe_*()` jobs, each in its own try/except — the established, explicitly-documented pattern for "ride this tick rather than start a second loop"). New `refresh_connected_apps_cache()` in `connected_apps.py` — deliberately *not* named `maybe_*` like its neighbors, since every existing `maybe_*` function means "gated to once a day" and this one re-checks unconditionally every tick — calls the real check and stores the result in a module-level cache. New `_connected_apps_alerts()` in `situation_room.py` reads that cache (never the live function directly), yielding one alert per disconnected app. An empty cache (fresh restart, first tick hasn't fired within 15 minutes) reads as "nothing to report" rather than forcing a redundant live call just to cover a brief startup gap. `GET /connected-apps` (Settings' own on-demand path) is completely untouched — still always a fresh live call.

  **A real, previously-undiscovered bug found live during this pass's own verification, fixed in the same pass**: the very first restart after adding the new `credits_exhausted_since` column crashed on startup — `sqlite3.OperationalError: duplicate column name`. Root cause: `_run_dual` (used whenever `TAILSCALE_IP` is set, which it is) runs two `uvicorn.Server` instances against the same `app` object, and FastAPI's `lifespan` context manager runs independently for each — already known and guarded for the ElevenLabs client and both scheduler tasks (a comment on that guard even names the exact prior incident: Finance's Luno snapshot writing every asset twice before it was added), but the block of `init_*_db()` calls itself was never guarded. `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` tolerates a concurrent double-run fine, which is presumably why this was never hit before — but a plain `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN` migration doesn't, and every `init_*_db()` function has at least one by now. Fixed with the identical `app.state` guard pattern already used for the other two, with one deliberate correctness detail: the flag is set *before* the `await init_db()` sequence, not after — since both lifespan entries share one single-threaded event loop, a synchronous check-then-set with no `await` in between can't be interleaved by the other coroutine, whereas setting it only after all the awaits finished would have left the exact same race open (the second entry could reach the check during any one of those await points). Verified directly: reproduced the concurrent race against a throwaway DB copy with the column artificially removed — before the fix, both coroutines ran the migration and the second one crashed; after, the second one correctly saw the flag and skipped entirely.

  Verified end to end: constructed a real `anthropic.BadRequestError` with a `billing_error`-shaped body and confirmed `.type` parses correctly; ran the full `db.py` migration against a throwaway copy of the real schema (add/re-add-is-a-no-op/clear, confirmed `COALESCE` prevents timestamp drift on repeated failures); populated `connected_apps.py`'s cache directly and confirmed `situation_room.py`'s two new alert functions produce the exact right shape, individually and composed into the full alert list; simulated the watchdog's own timing logic at scaled intervals and confirmed it doesn't fire against a healthy backend but does fire at exactly the third consecutive failure; confirmed the real health-check URL (host/port/token) resolves correctly against the actual live backend. Restarted the real `launchd`-managed backend twice (pure Python changes, no rebuild) — the first restart surfaced the dual-lifespan migration race live; the second, after the fix, started clean on both listeners with no traceback. Re-verified the live app end to end afterward: `/health` 200, `/situation-room` empty (no false alerts), `/connected-apps` showing all three integrations genuinely connected, `credits_exhausted_since` correctly `NULL`. No Swift changes, so no iOS/desktop rebuild-and-redeploy needed.

- **Daily Brief: email → real macOS notification at 6am, picked directly by Joshua from a screenshot of the current email digest** ("instead of receiving these as an email, can it rather pop up as a notification from P Corp every morning at 6am?"). Investigated feasibility on both platforms before designing anything, since "a real notification with genuinely fresh content, delivered at an exact time" means different things on each: **desktop is fully buildable today, no new setup** — `PCorpOSApp.swift`'s existing `SystemNotification.poster` already proves `osascript display notification` works with zero bundle identity or entitlement needed, unlike `UNUserNotificationCenter`, which that same file documents as hard-crashing on this build. **iPhone is a hard Apple platform wall, not a code gap**: a notification with real, freshly-computed content at an exact time requires actual push (APNs), which requires a paid Apple Developer Program membership — the same paid-account decision Joshua already declined once for the iOS signing-expiry issue (a plain local-scheduled notification could fire at 6am without one, but could only ever carry static, potentially-stale content baked in ahead of time, not what was actually asked for). Confirmed directly with Joshua: ship desktop now, leave iPhone on the status quo rather than build a compromised best-effort version.

  **A delivery-channel swap, not a new pipeline** — the digest's actual content computation (`compute_due_digest_sections()`, `_format_digest_body()` in `triggers.py`) is completely unchanged; only how the finished text gets delivered changes. New `backend/app/digest_notification.py` replaces `email_digest.py` outright (confirmed via grep: exactly one caller existed, no other consumer, safe to delete rather than leave as dead code) — `send_digest_notification(title, body)` shells out to `/usr/bin/osascript` via `subprocess.run(..., check=True, timeout=10)`, mirroring `PCorpOSApp.swift`'s own `appleScriptString` escaping exactly (backslashes first, then double-quotes) so a client/project name containing a quote can't break the AppleScript literal — verified directly with a deliberately quote-and-backslash-containing test message. Works from the backend process itself (not the SwiftUI app) because the backend's own `launchd` job already runs in the user's GUI session, same reasoning as the existing Swift-side comment about needing no bundle identity. **Fails loud, not soft, preserving `email_digest.py`'s own explicit contract**: `check=True` raises on a nonzero exit — verified directly by feeding it deliberately invalid AppleScript and confirming a real `CalledProcessError` — so a failed send still skips `mark_notified` and retries on the next scheduler tick exactly as before, just with a new failure mode (AppleScript trouble) instead of SMTP trouble.

  `send_hour`'s two source-level literals (`triggers_db.py`'s schema default and seed `INSERT`) changed `7` → `6`, plus a one-time direct `UPDATE` on the real, already-existing `digest_schedule` row in `backend/data/triggers.db` (the seed-insert only fires once per fresh DB, so editing the source default alone wouldn't move today's already-provisioned value) — same "confirmed a threshold directly with Joshua, just set it" precedent already used elsewhere in this codebase, not a new settings-API surface, since none was asked for. `backend/.env.example`'s now-stale `EMAIL_SMTP_USER`/`EMAIL_SMTP_APP_PASSWORD`/`EMAIL_DIGEST_TO` documentation block removed, since it described a config surface that no longer exists; the real, gitignored `backend/.env` was left untouched since its now-unused values are harmless.

  Verified end to end against the real live backend: `python3 -c "import app.main"` clean with the deleted module gone (confirmed zero remaining code references, only historical mentions in the new file's own docstring explaining what it replaces); fired a real test notification directly (`send_digest_notification`) with no exception; confirmed `compute_due_digest_sections()`/`run_daily_digest()` still work correctly against real live data (currently nothing due, so a genuine, correct no-op — confirmed it returns `{"sent": False, "item_count": 0}` without touching schedule state or firing anything spurious). Restarted the real `launchd`-managed backend (pure Python change, no rebuild) — clean startup on both listeners, no traceback. Confirmed live via `GET /triggers/status`: `send_hour: 6`. Today's digest had already sent once (at the old 7am, via email, before this shipped) per `last_sent_date`, so tomorrow morning is the first real end-to-end firing under the new schedule and channel — deliberately not forced/faked today given nothing is currently due anyway. No Swift changes, so no iOS/desktop rebuild-and-redeploy needed. Visual confirmation that the actual banner appears on Joshua's screen is his own to do, per this session's standing rule against self-driving/self-verifying native UI.

- **iOS cold-start slowness and "connection errors," reported directly by Joshua: "very slow" especially right after swipe-closing and reopening the app, connection errors "most of the time."** This has been an actively-worked area — 7 prior connection-reliability fixes in `BackendClient.swift`/`RootView.swift` earlier this project — but every one of those targeted a connection going bad *after* being established (background/lock, backend restart, a stale task); none touched the cold-start path itself. Investigated rather than guessed, and found the real, confirmed root causes rather than a single dramatic bug.

  **Root cause 1, confirmed via grep across the whole shared client package and both platforms: zero uses of `timeoutInterval`/`URLSessionConfiguration` timeout properties anywhere.** Every launch-time REST call and the WebSocket's own handshake attempt inherited Foundation's default **60-second** timeout. If Tailscale's own route negotiation is even briefly slow right after a cold launch, nothing failed fast and retried — it just sat, silently, for up to a full minute, which is exactly what "very slow, then a connection error" looks like from the outside. Directly verified the backend's Mac IP is currently correct (`100.93.170.24`, matches `TAILSCALE_IP` and the hardcoded value in `P_Corp_OSApp.swift`), so a stale destination address wasn't today's problem — though the single-hardcoded-IP-with-zero-fallback design remains a known, already-documented, deliberately-deferred limitation, not touched here. Separately confirmed the existing exponential-backoff reconnect logic (1s/2s/4s/8s) *already* correctly covers the cold-start WebSocket case — `listen()` runs immediately after every `connect()`, cold-start included — so the actual gap was purely that the first failure signal took up to 60s to arrive at all; fixing the timeout, not adding new retry machinery, was the correctly-scoped fix.

  **New `PCorpKit/Sources/PCorpKit/BackendURLSession.swift`**: a shared `URLSessionConfiguration` with both timeout properties set to 10s (a ~6x improvement, verified directly against a genuinely unreachable host: failed at 10.1s instead of 60s) plus a `shared` `URLSession` for plain REST reuse. `BackendClient.swift`'s `openTask()` keeps its existing, deliberately-documented "brand-new `URLSession` per connection attempt" design (a real 2026-09-04 fix for pooled-connection staleness after long backgrounding, not undone here) — just builds that fresh instance from the new shorter-timeout configuration instead of `.default`. Every other `URLSession.shared` call on the launch path (`BackendClient`'s own conversation/history REST calls, `AgentsClient`, `FocusClient`, `InsightsClient`, `SituationRoomClient`) swapped to the new shared session. The other 13 `URLSession.shared` call sites elsewhere in `PCorpKit` (Finance, Joshx, Automations, etc.) are untouched — not part of the cold-launch sequence actually being complained about.

  **Root cause 2: `AgentsClient`'s error message was desktop-specific text shown verbatim on iOS** — `"Couldn't reach the backend — is it running? \`cd backend && uv run python -m app.main\`"`, genuinely confusing advice for a phone user, and one of the only launch-path fetchers with any visible error at all (Focus/Insights/SituationRoom fail silently). Very likely one of the actual messages Josh was seeing and being annoyed by. Replaced with a platform-neutral message, and added one silent retry after a 2-second delay before surfacing it at all — `AgentsClient` (unlike the WebSocket) had zero retry of its own, so a single transient cold-start blip used to leave a wrong-looking, permanently-stuck error until manually re-navigating (its `.task` only ever fires once per view appearance).

  **Root cause 3: `GET /insights` fired twice on every cold launch** — `WarRoomView` and its child `WarRoomCommandMap` each independently owned a separate `InsightsClient` instance and fetch, confirmed identical on both platforms. Fixed on both: `WarRoomCommandMap` no longer owns its own instance or fetch, instead taking the parent's existing one as an `@ObservedObject` parameter, reactively observing the same already-fetched data. **A related, pre-existing duplication found live during verification, not touched**: desktop's `RightRail.swift` (an always-visible sidebar, separate from War Room) owns yet a *third*, independent `InsightsClient` instance — a real, distinct fetch for a genuinely different persistent UI surface, not part of what was actually asked (a same-screen duplicate), so left as a named, disclosed follow-up rather than folded in as scope creep.

  Verified end to end: `swift build` clean on desktop, `xcodebuild` clean on iOS. The new 10s timeout confirmed directly against a deliberately unreachable host. Rebuilt desktop via `build_app.sh` (not plain `swift build`) and relaunched; rebuilt iOS and reinstalled onto Joshua's real iPhone via the `devicectl` install/launch path. Watched the real backend log during both fresh launches: desktop went from two `/insights` calls to one (`WarRoomCommandMap`'s own now gone, `RightRail`'s separate one still legitimately present); the iPhone's real cold-launch burst — `/insights`, `/agents`, `/situation-room`, and a WebSocket accept — showed exactly one clean, fast round of each, all `200 OK`, no errors. Visual/subjective confirmation that cold starts actually *feel* fast and error-free on the phone is Joshua's own to do, same standing limitation as every other native-UI change this session.

- **Real progress signals during long waits, asked directly by Josh: "when Frank is taking long to respond I want to know what it's doing while I wait."** The existing tool-execution-indicator feature already covers every tool Frank's own outer turn can call directly (confirmed via `tool_labels.py` — no silent fallthrough for any dispatched tool). Investigated where the actual remaining gaps were rather than assuming the whole feature needed rework.

  **Confirmed root cause: the worst blackout is inside sub-agent consults, not the outer turn.** `run_agentic_loop` (`agent_codebase_tools.py`, shared by Engineering/Design/Operations Agents' codebase access) showed exactly one static "Consulting the X Agent" label for an entire consult — every internal action (`read_file`, `list_directory`, `git_log`, `git_diff`, `git_show`, `run_build_check`) dispatched with zero signal at all, confirmed via grep. This loop's own in-code comment already documented a real, previously-observed case that hit `stop_reason: "max_tokens"` with *zero visible text*, at up to 16000 tokens of adaptive thinking — a confirmed, not hypothetical, multi-thousand-token silent stall, and very likely the actual experience behind Josh's complaint.

  **Fix**: new `_label_for_codebase_tool()` in `agent_codebase_tools.py`, sending a specific `[tool_start]` label right before each internal tool dispatch — "Reading backend/app/main.py", "Checking recent commits", "Looking at commit d45a074", etc. — reusing the exact existing sentinel/UI (`websocket` was already a parameter of both `run_agentic_loop` and `execute_codebase_tool`, so no new plumbing needed). **Verified live end to end**, not just unit-tested: connected a real websocket to a temporary test backend and asked for a real multi-step Engineering Agent consult (read a file, check git log, look at a commit) — confirmed the running status line updated through all three real actions in real time, exactly replacing the one-frozen-label experience.

  **Secondary gap, smaller but real**: the deep research pipeline's middle phase ("Researching N angles") stayed static for the entire parallel research phase regardless of how long any individual angle took. New `_research_sub_questions_with_progress()` in `research_agent.py` replaces a blind `asyncio.gather()` with an index-preserving variant using `asyncio.as_completed()` — sends "Researched N of M angles" as each sub-question actually finishes, only when there's more than one (a single sub-question's existing label already covers that case). Correctness risk here was real: `_format_sub_answers_for_synthesis()`'s "Angle 1/2/3" numbering depends on original decomposition order, not completion order — **verified directly with a mocked, deliberately out-of-order test** (three fake sub-questions with staggered delays finishing Q2 → Q3 → Q1): progress messages fired in real completion order while the returned list correctly preserved original Q1/Q2/Q3 order for synthesis.

  **A real, previously-shipped bug found live during this verification, fixed in the same pass — not a hypothetical**: the reliability pass's credit-exhaustion detection (shipped earlier the same night) checked `error.type == "billing_error"`, based on a synthetic test at the time. A live research-pipeline verification run genuinely exhausted the account's real credits mid-test, and the actual error Anthropic returned had `error.type == "invalid_request_error"` — not `"billing_error"` — meaning the shipped detection would have silently never fired for the real-world case it was built for. Fixed by adding a message-substring fallback (`"credit balance" in str(error).lower()`) alongside the structured-type check, verified against a faithful reconstruction of the SDK's own real error-string format (`_base_client.py`'s `_make_status_error_from_response`: `f"Error code: {status} - {body}"`) — confirmed the real captured error now correctly triggers, and confirmed an unrelated 400 (a plain malformed-request error) correctly does *not* false-positive. Re-verified live against the real production backend: the friendly "Frank is temporarily unavailable..." message now fires correctly, `credits_exhausted_since` is set, and the Situation Room alert appears with a real timestamp — all against the account's actual, currently-real low-credit state (not simulated), confirmed via a direct, isolated API call. **Test messages from this verification landed in Josh's real active conversation** (a raw test websocket script bypassed the Swift client's own fresh-conversation-on-launch logic) — cleaned up via the existing, legitimate `POST /conversations` endpoint (the same one a normal app launch already uses), not a raw database edit; the polluted messages remain reachable via Chat History only, same disclosed "no delete-conversation capability exists" limitation as earlier this project.

  No Swift changes for the progress-signal work itself — pure backend, reusing the exact existing `[tool_start]` sentinel and UI. Backend restarted twice (pure Python, no rebuild): once for the progress-signal fix, once more for the credit-detection correction. **Real, unresolved as of this writing**: the Anthropic account's credit balance is genuinely exhausted right now, confirmed via a direct, isolated API call — Frank is down on both apps until Josh adds credits, correctly surfaced now via the fixed detection rather than silently.

- **Real WebSocket regression from the iOS cold-start fix, found and already fixed on disk, deployed and verified this session — not caught before shipping.** `608f66a` (the iOS cold-start timeout fix) swapped `BackendClient.swift`'s `openTask()` from a bare `URLSession(configuration: .default)` to `BackendURLSession.configuration`, which sets both `timeoutIntervalForRequest` *and* `timeoutIntervalForResource` to 10s. The second property isn't an idle timeout — per Apple's own docs it's a hard ceiling on a `URLSessionTask`'s *total* lifetime regardless of active traffic — so every chat WebSocket was being killed by `URLSession` itself exactly 10 seconds after opening, independent of whether Frank was still replying. Any reply involving tool-use, agent delegation, or web search commonly runs past 10s and got its connection torn down mid-stream, surfacing as `listen()`'s existing "[connection error: lost connection to Frank mid-reply]" — correlated with reply duration, not any real network condition, and undetected until now because the cold-start fix's own verification only checked initial-connect timing, never a long-running turn.

  **Fix**: new `BackendURLSession.webSocketConfiguration` — keeps the same fast-fail 10s `timeoutIntervalForRequest` for the initial handshake (still a real win against a slow Tailscale negotiation) but deliberately leaves `timeoutIntervalForResource` at Foundation's own default (a 7-day ceiling) — long enough that no real chat turn will ever hit it, still a real backstop against a task truly wedged forever. `openTask()` now builds from this configuration instead of the plain REST one; every other `URLSession.shared` call site (short-lived REST fetches) correctly keeps the 10s resource cap, since those genuinely should fail fast either way.

  **Verified with the real thing being tested, not a proxy**: a Python `websockets` client can't reproduce this bug at all (it's Apple `URLSession`-specific client behavior, invisible to the backend or a non-Foundation client) — so verification used a standalone Swift script constructing the exact real `URLSessionConfiguration` from the fix and opening a real `URLSessionWebSocketTask` against the live backend. A deep-research request confirmed to genuinely take 100+ seconds completed with zero `.failure` events and zero in-band connection errors — 10x past the old buggy ceiling — proving the fix, not just the reasoning behind it.

  Rebuilt and redeployed both platforms: desktop via `build_app.sh` (not plain `swift build`) and relaunched; iOS rebuilt via `xcodebuild`, reinstalled and relaunched on Joshua's real iPhone via the `devicectl` path. Test conversation traffic from both this verification and the credit-detection fix above landed in Joshua's real active conversation (raw test scripts bypass the Swift client's own fresh-conversation-on-launch logic) — reset via the app's own legitimate `POST /conversations` endpoint, not a database edit.

- **PDF filename duplication, reported directly by Joshua from a live invoice generation: `invoice-invoice-inv-001-herbish-...pdf`.** `generate_pdf_document()` (`documents.py`) unconditionally prepended `document_type`'s slug to `title`'s slug — but a natural title restates the type ("Invoice INV-001 - Herbish" for `document_type="invoice"`), which the tool's own schema explicitly invites via its example values ("proposal", "quote", "financial-report"), so this wasn't a one-off, it would recur for any of those. Fixed by skipping the type prefix when the title's own slug already starts with it (or equals it) — verified against the exact real reported case (correctly dedupes), an unrelated title/type pair (correctly keeps both parts), and an exact-match case (correctly collapses to one). Pure backend, no other code depends on the exact filename format (confirmed via grep) — backend restarted, no rebuild needed.

- **Generated PDFs are now actually viewable and saveable, asked directly by Joshua right after the filename fix above: "I want the pdfs to be viewable & saveable."** Until now `generate_pdf_document` only ever returned a raw Mac-local absolute path as text — Frank told the user that path in chat, and that was the entire delivery mechanism. Confirmed via direct investigation before designing anything: no HTTP route served the generated-documents directory, no code path sent generated file bytes to either client, and zero native save/share/preview code existed anywhere in either Swift target. This codebase already had the right precedent, found rather than invented: `knowledge.py` + `GET /knowledge/{filename}` — desktop reads project docs straight off local disk (same Mac, no network needed), iOS fetches the same content over an authenticated HTTP endpoint, "because the iOS app runs on a phone with no filesystem access to this Mac at all." This feature is the same split, applied to generated PDFs.

  **Backend**: new `GET /documents/{filename}` (`Depends(verify_token)`, same shape as `/speak`/`/knowledge/{filename}`), resolving safely within `DOCS_DIR` (`candidate.parent == DOCS_DIR.resolve()` — `agent_file_safety.py`'s own helper doesn't apply here, it explicitly denies everything under `backend/data`) — verified directly against a real file (200, exact byte match), a path-traversal attempt and a nonexistent filename (both correctly 404, no leak). `run_claude_turn` now accumulates any PDFs generated during a turn and sends a new `\n[document_generated]` sentinel (same JSON-sentinel convention every other backend signal already uses), and — the part that actually makes this durable, not just a live nicety — returns them alongside its reply so `websocket_chat` can pass them into the existing `save_message(..., attachments=...)` call for the assistant role, reusing a column that until now only the user role ever populated. Without this, reopening an old conversation would show no trace Frank ever generated a file, even though it's still sitting on disk.

  **Swift**: new `GeneratedDocument`/`ChatMessage.generatedDocuments`, a new `GeneratedDocumentRow` (deliberately not folded into the existing `MessageAttachmentsView`, which documents itself as "only ever used from a user message's own rendering" and has no tap-handling shape at all — the first real case of Frank attaching something to his own reply). `HistoryAttachmentEntry`'s fields had to become optional to safely decode two real shapes sharing one column (a user attachment's `original_name`/`media_type` vs. a generated document's `filename`/`title`) without throwing on a reopened conversation containing either kind.

  **Desktop** reads the real file straight off local disk via the already-established `ProjectPaths.repoRoot` same-machine shortcut (no network round trip needed) and hands it to `NSWorkspace.shared.open(...)` — opens in Preview, which already provides full view + save (Cmd+S, drag out, Reveal in Finder) natively, no custom save-panel code. Its `ChatBubble` needed the new row wired into *two* separate branches (confirmed asymmetric with iOS: the `runningTool`-present and plain-text assistant branches are structurally distinct here, unlike iOS's single unconditional attachment call site).

  **iOS** fetches bytes over the new endpoint (a phone has no access to the Mac's filesystem), writes them to a temp file under the real generated filename (already collision-safe via its own embedded timestamp), and presents it via SwiftUI's native `.quickLookPreview($previewURL)` modifier (`import QuickLook`, the correct idiomatic API for this project's iOS 26.5 target — not a hand-wrapped `UIViewControllerRepresentable`, which would be reaching for a pre-SwiftUI escape hatch this case doesn't need). QuickLook's own toolbar already has a native share button (AirDrop/Save to Files/Mail/etc.), so no separate custom `ShareLink` was built for v1 — shipped only if it turns out to actually be wanted later.

  Verified end to end against the real live production backend, not just a temporary one: generated a real PDF through the actual app, confirmed the `[document_generated]` sentinel fired with the correct filename/title, confirmed both delivery paths work on the exact same real file (`GET /documents/{filename}` returns byte-identical content; the direct local path desktop would construct exists and is readable), confirmed `messages.attachments` persisted correctly for the assistant role, and confirmed `GET /history` round-trips a decodable entry for it — proving the reopened-conversation case actually works, not just compiles. `swift build` clean on desktop, `xcodebuild` clean on iOS. Rebuilt and redeployed both platforms (`build_app.sh` for desktop, `xcodebuild` + `devicectl` install/launch for iOS). Visual confirmation that tapping the row actually opens Preview (desktop) or presents the QuickLook sheet (iOS) is Joshua's own to do, same standing limitation as every other native-UI change this session. Also fixed a real, self-inflicted CHANGELOG structural bug found while adding this entry: an earlier edit had stranded the iOS-cold-start entry's own closing verification paragraph at the very end of the file, after several unrelated later entries — moved back to its correct position, same class of mistake (and same fix) as a markdown-tables entry earlier this project.

- **Infrastructure Independence Phase 1, Stage 1 (audit) and Stage 2 (cloud skeleton), from Joshua's own detailed spec: remove the architectural dependency on his Mac being the machine P Corp fundamentally requires.** Stage 1 was assessment-only — a full dependency inventory gathered by direct inspection (76 backend modules, all 13 local SQLite databases with real table names queried live, all 46 routes, every credential and which file reads it, every AppleScript/NSWorkspace/hardcoded-IP call site) — published as an artifact, no code touched, explicit approval required before proceeding. Headline findings: `BackendHost.host` was a single mutable string with zero fallback/discovery; no shared HTTP-request helper existed anywhere, so every one of ~20 Swift client files independently built its own URL; the single-token auth model has desktop reading the token straight off local disk and iOS requiring it to be copy-pasted by hand into Keychain once; and exactly three capabilities are genuinely, structurally Mac-only (the Trading Division sibling-repo SQLite bridge, the HF Markets/MetaTrader-5-via-Wine JSON bridge, AppleScript Calendar access) — all three already fail soft today, which is the existing pattern a future "Mac Local Node" would formalize, not a new idea.

  **Stage 2, approved next: environment-config system + a real health endpoint, code-only** (provisioning actual cloud hosting/Postgres needs Joshua's own account and payment method, not something done on his behalf — deferred until he picks a provider). New `PCorpEnvironment` enum in `BackendHost.swift` (`.local`/`.staging`/`.production`; the latter two point at placeholder hosts on the reserved `.invalid` TLD — RFC 2606, guaranteed to never resolve to a real domain — until real cloud endpoints exist, so selecting them today fails to connect honestly rather than silently). `BackendHost.url(path:extraQueryItems:)`/`.wsURL(path:)` are the new shared builders every client migrates to — confirmed via grep that all ~20 files shared the exact same `URLComponents(string: "http://\(BackendHost.host):8731\(path)")!` + token-query-item shape (several already had their own private, verbatim-duplicate `url(path:)` wrapper), making this a mechanical, low-risk migration across all 17 `PCorpKit/*Client.swift` files, `BackendClient.swift`'s own 6 URL builders, and the app-target files (`KnowledgeClient.swift`, `VoiceOutput.swift` on both platforms). This *is* the fix for the two files the audit found bypassing `BackendHost` entirely with a hardcoded `127.0.0.1` literal (`desktop/ActivityTracker.swift`, `desktop/VoiceOutput.swift`) — once they call the same shared builder as everything else, there's no independent literal left to go stale. `ios/P_Corp_OSApp.swift`'s `BackendHost.host = "..."` becomes `BackendHost.localHost = "..."` (same value, renamed to reflect what it actually is now that `.staging`/`.production` also exist).

  New `GET /status`, deliberately separate from the existing `/health` — the hang-watchdog already depends on `/health` responding inside a 2-second timeout every 30 seconds, so adding real checks there risked the watchdog misdiagnosing a slow check as a hang. `/status` reuses real, already-existing functions rather than inventing new checks: a real query against `pcorp.db` (proves the DB is actually reachable), `connected_apps.compute_connected_apps_status()`, `db.get_credits_exhausted_since()`, and the real last-run timestamps already sitting in `triggers_db.py`/`finance_db.py`'s own schedule tables — no fabricated status anywhere, matching this codebase's existing discipline.

  Verified end to end: grepped for zero remaining `BackendHost.host`/hardcoded-`127.0.0.1:8731` references anywhere in `PCorpKit`/`desktop`/`ios` after the migration (only two stale doc comments remained, fixed for accuracy — no code). Cross-checked `/status`'s every field directly against the real databases (`digest_schedule`, `credits_exhausted_since`, `luno_snapshot_schedule`) to confirm exact matches, not coincidental ones; confirmed the database-unreachable branch actually detects a real failure by pointing `DB_PATH` at a nonexistent path and confirming the exception is caught correctly. Confirmed `/health` still responds in ~28ms, unaffected. `swift build` clean on desktop, `xcodebuild` clean on iOS. Rebuilt and redeployed both platforms; watched the real backend log during both fresh launches and confirmed the exact same request pattern as before this change (WebSocket accept, `/agents`, `/focus`, `/conversations`, `/situation-room`, `/insights`, all `200 OK`) — this stage is invisible to the running apps, per Joshua's own requirement that nothing cuts over until a real cloud endpoint exists.

## 2026-09-09

- **Desktop app sustained 85-92% CPU, found live during a routine "check phone and desktop app are healthy" request — not something Joshua reported, investigated on his explicit "yes, dig into it."** `ps` confirmed the load was real and sustained (91.7% over 12+ minutes, not a momentary spike). Ruled out the just-shipped Infrastructure Independence Stage 2 work directly (grep confirmed zero Swift call sites poll `/health`) before looking elsewhere. macOS's own `sample` profiler pinned the actual cause precisely: `FrankOrb.body.getter` (`WarRoomView.swift`) dominated the call stack, bottoming out in a `TimelineView(.animation(paused: reduceMotion))` driving a `Canvas` that redrew all 450 particles — per-particle position, a shimmer calculation, and a fresh `Path`+`context.fill()` — on every single display refresh (60-120fps, uncapped, gated only by the Reduce Motion accessibility setting), forever, regardless of whether the orb was actually changing state.

  **The fix actually verified to work: throttling the tick rate.** `.animation` (display-refresh-rate) swapped for `.periodic(from: .now, by: 1.0 / 15.0)` — the visible motion is a slow shimmer (~12.6s period), so sampling it at 15fps instead of up to 120fps reads as visually identical while cutting the tick rate 4-8x. (Reduce Motion's "paused" behavior had to become a 3600s interval rather than a literal pause, since a ternary between `.animation` and `.periodic` doesn't typecheck as one concrete `TimelineSchedule`; real state changes still redraw normally regardless, only the decorative idle ticking goes effectively static.) Measured live via repeated `ps`/`top` sampling before and after rebuild: **80-92% sustained → ~40-44% sustained**, roughly a 2x cut.

  **Two further optimizations were tried and reverted after honest measurement showed zero benefit — a real finding, not a wasted step.** Batching the 450 individual `context.fill()` calls into ~20 combined-path calls (particles grouped by quantized opacity) measured the same ~40% as the unbatched version. Cutting particle count 450 → 250 also measured the same ~40%. A targeted diagnostic settled it: a build with a completely empty `Canvas` closure — still ticking at 15fps, drawing nothing at all — measured that identical ~40%, proving the entire remaining cost is fixed per-tick SwiftUI/TimelineView/Canvas overhead, not the drawing content. Both changes were reverted rather than left in for no gain: particle count is back at 450 (preserving Joshua's own earlier "more particles... it must be bolder" feedback) and the fill loop is back to its original simple form.

  Verified via repeated `ps`/`top` sampling (cumulative CPU-time-over-elapsed-time, not just instantaneous snapshots) across five separate rebuild-relaunch cycles against the real running app — every number reported was read live off the actual process, never assumed. Rebuilt via `desktop/build_app.sh` each time (not plain `swift build`) and relaunched the real `.build/PCorpOS.app`. No iOS changes; this file is desktop-only.

## 2026-09-10

- **Follow-up to the FrankOrb CPU investigation, continued at Joshua's request ("let's pick back up") after the previous night's throttle fix landed at ~40-44% sustained, down from 80-92%.** Tried dropping the Canvas tick rate further, 15fps → 8fps — measured identical CPU to 15fps, undercutting the assumption that tick rate itself was still the active lever. Settled it with a clean diagnostic: rebuilt with FrankOrb's entire `TimelineView`/`Canvas` removed from the view hierarchy (not just emptied, actually absent) and measured the same ~35-45% anyway — proof the remaining cost was never FrankOrb's Canvas at all, something the previous night's investigation hadn't isolated.

  **A real second culprit, found the same way: `sample` on the Canvas-free build.** The profile showed heavy SwiftUICore layout-engine churn (`sizeThatFits`, `placeChildren`, `StackLayout`) — a strong signal, since `FrankOrb`'s own contact-shadow `Ellipse` animates its actual `.frame(width:height:)` (a real layout property, not a render one) via a continuous `.easeInOut(duration: 3.4).repeatForever`, forcing SwiftUI to re-run layout on every interpolated frame of that 3.4s cycle, forever. Fixed by keeping the frame fixed (72x10) and animating a `scaleEffect` instead (a pure render transform) to reach the same two endpoint sizes — 72x10 at rest, 100x14 floating — identical visual result, none of the layout-triggering mechanism.

  **Measured honestly, this fix did not move the needle either** — a second `sample` profile taken afterward (Canvas still removed) came back diffuse: no single dominant function, just general SwiftUI view-diffing / AppKit layout-check / Swift retain-release overhead spread across many small contributors. Kept the fix anyway since animating a layout property inside a `repeatForever` is a real anti-pattern regardless of whether it explained the specific number being chased tonight.

  **The actual reason measurement kept contradicting itself, found last**: CPU readings swing enormously — 1.4% to 33.2% across consecutive 10-second windows — even while the app's window is confirmed continuously frontmost, almost certainly macOS App Nap engaging and disengaging on its own schedule rather than anything code-level. Confirmed directly: `osascript`-verified frontmost state checked every 10 seconds alongside `ps`'s cumulative CPU time showed no correlation between focus state and the swings. This means every specific percentage reported across both nights of this investigation — the original 80-92% baseline included — carries more measurement uncertainty than presented at the time.

  **Decision, made with Joshua**: stop chasing tighter numbers through scripted `ps`/`top`/`sample` sampling, since it's now the noisiest instrument in the investigation, and leave the two real, independently-justified fixes (Canvas throttled to 15fps; shadow ellipse using `scaleEffect` instead of an animated frame) as the shipped result — both correct regardless of the exact percentage, neither claimed to fully explain a number that measurement noise means nobody can pin down precisely. No iOS changes; desktop-only.

- **Infrastructure Independence Stage 3 prep: real, tested SQLite → Postgres migration tooling, built and run against a local Postgres instance rather than waiting on a real cloud one.** Actually provisioning cloud hosting/Postgres needs Joshua's own account — not something done on his behalf — so instead of leaving Stage 3 as an unstarted line item, he chose to have the real migration tool written and proven now, against Homebrew-installed PostgreSQL 16 running locally, so it's a working tool the moment a real cloud instance exists rather than untested code written against an assumption. Along the way, flagged and resolved a real conflict between `TECH_STACK.md` (which had already recorded libSQL/Turso as the intended sync layer, specifically to avoid a storage-engine migration) and the Stage 1 audit (which named Postgres instead, without reconciling that earlier decision) — confirmed directly with Joshua that Postgres is the intended target.

  **New `backend/app/migration/` subpackage** — `manifest.py` (the tool's own inventory of all 12 real domains/tables/primary-key kinds, hardcoded rather than sniffed from SQLite DDL text at runtime, since `PRAGMA table_info` can't distinguish `AUTOINCREMENT` from a `CHECK(id=1)` singleton-row table), `introspect.py` (reads real schema/rows from a SQLite file, always via the `?mode=ro` URI form — read-only enforced by SQLite itself, not just code discipline), `checksum.py` (canonical row serialization + SHA-256, rows always sorted in Python by primary-key value rather than via SQL `ORDER BY` — Postgres's `en_US.UTF-8` collation and SQLite's binary default can order TEXT differently in edge cases, which would silently break a comparison even when the data matches), `backup.py` (the only file that ever opens a live `backend/data/*.db` file, via SQLite's *online backup* API rather than a raw copy — these databases use rollback-journal mode, not WAL, so a raw `cp` mid-write risked a torn snapshot), `replicate.py`, `verify.py`, and the `run_all.py` entrypoint (`uv run python -m app.migration.run_all`). No existing file touched — `app/db.py` and all 12 `*_db.py` modules are completely untouched; the only existing-file changes are one new dependency line (`psycopg[binary]`, added via `uv add`) and one new documented, optional `MIGRATION_POSTGRES_DSN` entry in `.env.example`.

  **A real design correction made live, from real data, not assumed**: the first full run failed on a genuine `ForeignKeyViolation` — two actual `alpha_mode.db` `projects` rows reference a `clients` row that doesn't exist (`clients` is genuinely empty). Root cause, confirmed directly: `PRAGMA foreign_keys` is `0` on every one of these 12 databases (and never turned on anywhere in `app/*.py`), meaning every declared `REFERENCES` clause across the entire schema has always been documentation, never a live guarantee — the original design had created *real*, enforced Postgres FK constraints for the 5 domains with declared intra-file FKs, which is stricter than the source has ever actually been. Fixed by not creating FOREIGN KEY constraints in the Postgres DDL at all, preserving the columns as plain data — faithfully replicating current (unenforced) behavior rather than silently upgrading it, and critically not touching the real orphaned row to make it fit.

  Other real fixes made during design review before implementation, not left as follow-ups: FK-safe table insertion order was corrected from an initial guess of 2 dependent domains to the real 5 (`alpha_mode`, `finance`, `pcorp`, `joshx`, `people`) after direct schema inspection; the Postgres sequence fix after loading `identity`-kind tables uses the correct 3-argument `setval(seq, COALESCE(MAX(id), 1), MAX(id) IS NOT NULL)` rather than the 2-argument form, which would have skipped id `1` on the next real insert for any of the several tables that are currently empty; `OVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUE` was dropped entirely once confirmed unnecessary, since `GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY` (chosen specifically so a real future live-cutover insert still works normally) already accepts explicit values in a plain `INSERT` — collapsing three different insert shapes down to one used for every table regardless of primary-key kind.

  **Verified end to end against all 12 real databases, not a subset or a synthetic fixture**: a single-domain smoke test (`personal.db`) first, then the full run — all 38 real tables across all 12 domains `PASS`, cross-checked independently via `psql`'s own `pg_tables` count (12 schemas, 38 tables, exact match). Negative test: deliberately tampered one live-replicated row via `psql`, confirmed `verify.py` correctly reported `FAIL` for exactly that one table with a non-zero exit code — proving the check actually detects real mismatches rather than green-lighting everything; reverted via `replicate.py`'s own upsert and confirmed `PASS` again, which doubled as the idempotency proof (same `.bak` files, re-run, row counts unchanged, no duplicates). Confirmed the real running backend was never touched: `backup.py`'s source connections are opened read-only at the SQLite level, and the live `backend/data/*.db` files' own mtime changes during testing trace to the live backend's own ordinary activity (activity-log entries, background calendar sync), not this tool, which cannot write to a `mode=ro` connection by construction. Test-run backup files and manifest were pruned afterward per the tool's own documented practice of not auto-deleting from within itself; the one pre-existing manual backup (`joshx.db.bak-20260831012229`) was left untouched.

  Actual cloud provisioning, DEFAULT/CHECK-constraint replication, and upgrading cross-schema references into real FKs all remain explicitly out of scope for this prep step — Stage 5/7 concerns, not this one.

- **A second, deeper Infrastructure Independence audit, delivered as an updated artifact and approved, then Stage 4 prep started the same way as Stage 3: real code, tested locally, no cloud account needed yet.** Joshua's own detailed 32-section Phase 1 spec asked for a full re-audit — file:line precision across every subsystem, not a recap of the original. Three parallel research passes (client/transport/auth/secrets, backend domains/data/agents/triggers, integrations/WebSocket/observability) surfaced real findings the original audit missed: two backend-side `osascript` calls (`system_calendar.py`, `digest_notification.py`) with zero isolation boundary today — the single most consequential gap, since unlike client-side AppleScript this breaks outright the moment the backend leaves the Mac; `google_oauth.py:39`'s redirect URI hardcoded to `127.0.0.1:8731`; `hf_markets_client.py` confirmed structurally, permanently Mac-only (reads a file inside a Wine bottle, no API exists); pending-approval state living only on one blocked coroutine tied to one live socket, with no persistence or recovery path; zero cross-client broadcast between simultaneously-connected devices; iOS notifications confirmed a complete no-op (`SystemNotification.poster` never set, no toast UI anywhere); and `SUPABASE_URL`/`SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` missing from `.env.example` despite being real, live credentials. Published, approved.

  **Stage 4 ("move safe read-only routes to the cloud API") hit the same wall as Stage 2/3: nothing exists yet to move reads *to*.** Rather than wait, built and proved a dual-backend read proof of concept the same way Stage 3's tooling was built — real code, tested against the local Postgres instance already populated by a full real-cutover-style Stage 3 rehearsal run this session (fresh backup, full replicate, full verify, all 38 tables `PASS` again).

  **New `DATA_BACKEND`/`CLOUD_POSTGRES_DSN` env vars** (`backend/app/main.py`), following Stage 2's own `PCorpEnvironment` precedent — defaults to `sqlite` (today's exact, unchanged behavior); deliberately a separate variable from Stage 3's own `MIGRATION_POSTGRES_DSN`, since a one-off migration script's destination and the live app's read source are different concerns that could legitimately diverge later. `lifespan()` gains a conditional `psycopg.AsyncConnection` (reusing the driver already added for Stage 3 — no new dependency), created only when `DATA_BACKEND=postgres`, stored on `app.state` next to the existing `elevenlabs_client` pattern; fails loud with a clear `RuntimeError` if the DSN is missing rather than silently falling back to SQLite.

  **One domain converted as the proof of concept**: `backend/app/personal_db.py`'s `dashboard_snapshot()` (backs `GET /personal/dashboard`) becomes a thin dispatcher — the original SQLite body renamed to `_dashboard_snapshot_sqlite()`, untouched otherwise, plus a new `_dashboard_snapshot_postgres()` reading the identical schema the Stage 3 tooling already creates. The other 11 domains and every write function (`add_goal`, `update_goal_status`, `delete_goal`, `add_habit`, `delete_habit`) are deliberately untouched — Stage 4 is reads only, writes are Stage 5, and extending this to the other domains is a mechanical repeat of one proven pattern, not a new design question.

  Verified with real, executed evidence, not code review alone: the SQLite and Postgres paths return byte-identical results for the real (all soft-deleted) current data, and — since that alone doesn't exercise the row-mapping code — a throwaway non-deleted test row inserted into the disposable Postgres replica only (never the real `personal.db`) confirmed correct field-by-field mapping, then removed. The connection guard's four branches were each tested directly: SQLite mode never attempts a Postgres connection; `DATA_BACKEND=postgres` with no DSN raises the intended `RuntimeError`; an unreachable DSN raises psycopg's own `OperationalError` (no silent fallback either way); a valid local DSN connects successfully. `python3 -c "import app.main"` clean, confirmed `DATA_BACKEND` defaults to `"sqlite"`. No changes to the live running backend process — all verification ran as standalone scripts calling the real functions directly, never restarting the actual `launchd`-managed process serving Joshua's real app.

- **Stage 5 prep: dual-backend writes proof of concept, same domain and same "build and test locally" discipline as Stage 4.** Stage 5 is "move approved writes to the cloud, preserving the audit-log discipline and every approval gate" — same wall as every stage since 2 (nothing to write to without a real cloud Postgres), same fix: build and prove the real code against the local instance. Continued `personal.db` from Stage 4 rather than starting a new domain — it already had a working read path and populated test data, and its writes are genuinely the simplest in the codebase to convert first: no delegation layer, and — confirmed directly against the Infrastructure Independence audit's own classification — **not approval-gated** ("regular" under `SECURITY.md`, local-only and reversible). That let this prove the core dual-backend-write mechanism on its own, before tackling the genuinely different mechanism approval-gated writes use (the blocking `websocket.receive_text()` round-trip), which needs its own separate proof of concept later.

  **All 5 write functions in `backend/app/personal_db.py` become dispatchers** (`add_goal`, `update_goal_status`, `delete_goal`, `add_habit`, `delete_habit`), same shape as Stage 4's `dashboard_snapshot()` — each gains a `postgres_conn` parameter, each SQLite body renamed `_<name>_sqlite` and left untouched, each gains a new `_<name>_postgres` sibling. `backend/app/personal_tools.py`'s `execute_personal_tool_call` gains a pass-through parameter; `backend/app/main.py`'s one call site (inside `websocket_chat`'s tool-dispatch loop) computes it from `websocket.app.state.postgres_conn` — the same `app.state` object Stage 4 populates in `lifespan()`, just reached from the WebSocket handler instead of a REST route, since `WebSocket` exposes `.app` the same way `Request` does.

  **A real correctness bug found during design, before any code was written**: `personal_db.py`'s fuzzy-match lookups (`_find_goal_id`, and an inline equivalent in `delete_habit`) rely on SQLite's `LIKE`, which is case-insensitive for ASCII by default — confirmed by reading the actual queries (`COLLATE NOCASE` for the exact match, plain `LIKE` for the substring fallback). Postgres's `LIKE` is case-sensitive; using it unmodified would have been a live regression (e.g. "mark 'read more books' done" silently failing to match a stored "Read More Books"). Fixed with `ILIKE` for both steps in the new `_find_goal_id_postgres` and `_delete_habit_postgres`'s inline lookup — verified directly with a case-mismatched identifier and a partial-substring identifier, both resolving correctly.

  **A second real bug found live during verification, not assumed away**: the first test `add_goal` call failed with a genuine `NotNullViolation` on `status` — Stage 3's `replicate.py` deliberately doesn't replicate SQLite's `DEFAULT` clauses into the Postgres DDL (documented at the time as "Stage 5's concern"), so `personal.goals`/`personal.habits`'s `status`/`created_at` columns are `NOT NULL` with no default in Postgres, unlike the SQLite tables which have `DEFAULT 'active'`/`DEFAULT (datetime('now'))`. Confirmed no partial row was left (Postgres rolled the failed `INSERT` back atomically) before fixing it: `_add_goal_postgres`/`_add_habit_postgres` now supply `status`/`created_at` explicitly rather than relying on a default that doesn't exist on the Postgres side.

  Verified end to end against the disposable Postgres replica, never the real `personal.db`: added a goal and a habit, updated and soft-deleted a goal (confirmed the row still exists with `deleted_at` set, never a hard delete), soft-deleted a habit, and confirmed a soft-deleted goal correctly stops matching further lookups (`WHERE deleted_at IS NULL` behaving identically on both engines) — all cleaned up afterward, confirmed via direct query that `pcorp_migration_test` ended in the exact same state (same 3 goals, 1 habit, same ids/timestamps) it started in. Confirmed the SQLite paths are untouched — every `_<name>_sqlite` function is the original query body under a new name, no logic changed, diffed directly against the actual patch. No changes to the live running backend; every test called the real functions directly against the disposable Postgres replica.

- **Stage 6 prep: a real, currently-latent double-fire bug found live in the daily digest, fixed with an atomic job-claim primitive — the exact class of bug the Risk Register had already named, this time before it happened rather than after.** Stage 6 is "move the trigger scheduler to a cloud worker... add idempotency guards so a brief overlap between the old Mac scheduler and the new cloud worker can't double-fire anything." Reading `backend/app/triggers.py`'s actual `maybe_run_daily_digest()` to design that guard found it already had the exact bug being guarded against: it checked `last_sent_date != today`, then only *after* actually sending the digest called `mark_digest_sent(today)` — a classic check-then-act race. Two concurrent schedulers (today's single Mac loop plus a future cloud worker, during any real migration overlap) could both pass the check before either had written, both send a real duplicate macOS notification, and only then both harmlessly update the flag — by then the duplicate send already happened. Harmless today only because exactly one scheduler exists; a live bug the moment Stage 6's real cutover introduces a second one.

  **New `claim_digest_send()`** (`backend/app/triggers_db.py`, SQLite + Postgres dispatchers, same shape as every Stage 4/5 write) replaces `mark_digest_sent`: one conditional `UPDATE ... WHERE id = 1 AND (last_sent_date IS NULL OR last_sent_date != ?)`, using the cursor's own `rowcount` to report whether *this* call actually won the claim or lost it to a caller that already claimed today — atomic by construction in both engines, since a single `UPDATE` is what makes the check-and-write indivisible where two separate statements weren't. `maybe_run_daily_digest()` reordered to claim *before* running, not after, so only the winner ever calls `run_daily_digest()`.

  **A second real correctness issue found while fixing the first, not shipped blind**: reordering to claim-before-run would have silently broken `run_daily_digest()`'s own pre-existing, deliberate contract — its docstring already stated a failed send must not be recorded as sent, so it retries next tick. With the claim now committing *before* the send, a real send failure would have left the claim in place with nothing to revert it, meaning the next tick would wrongly see today's digest as already sent and skip the retry entirely. Fixed with a new `revert_digest_claim()`, called in a `try/except` around `run_daily_digest()`: on failure, reverts `last_sent_date` back to its pre-claim value and re-raises, so the outer scheduler loop's existing logging is unaffected and the next tick genuinely retries — the exact same guarantee the code had before, just re-established under the new claim-first ordering instead of accidentally dropped by it.

  **Verified with real concurrent execution, not sequential calls that wouldn't exercise the race**: two independent `psycopg` connections (simulating two independent schedulers, not one connection making two calls) raced `claim_digest_send()` against each other via `asyncio.gather` on the disposable Postgres replica — exactly one returned `True`, confirmed via real output, not reasoning; a third call the same "day" correctly returned `False`. Separately verified the revert-then-retry path: claimed, reverted (simulating a failed send), confirmed the schedule reverted to its exact pre-claim value, confirmed a subsequent claim then succeeded again. The SQLite path was verified identically against a throwaway temporary database (`tempfile`-created, `triggers_db.DB_PATH` monkeypatched to point at it) — deliberately never the real `backend/data/triggers.db`, since a real claim/revert cycle against it risked tripping a genuine duplicate digest notification. The disposable Postgres replica's `digest_schedule` row was restored to match the real live SQLite value (`2026-09-10`) afterward, confirmed via direct comparison of both. The other three jobs riding the same scheduler tick (Luno/HF Markets/market-movers snapshots) are untouched — same class of fix, deliberately not applied yet.

- **Stage 7 prep: real per-device auth sessions, built additively alongside the existing single shared token rather than replacing it.** Stage 7 is "Mac and iPhone become clients of the cloud API... the single shared token is replaced by a real session architecture." The client-switch half has nothing new to build yet — Stage 2 already built the actual switching mechanism, there's just no real endpoint to switch to. The auth half is a real, currently-existing gap independent of hosting location: `backend/app/auth.py`'s one token has existed unrotated since 2026-07-24, stored in plaintext, no expiry, and the only revocation path is manually deleting the file and re-pasting a new value into iOS's Keychain by hand.

  **New `backend/app/auth_db.py`** (new file, new `backend/data/auth.db` — the same "genuinely separate domain gets its own db" pattern as `automations.db`/`triggers.db`): a `device_sessions` table storing only a **SHA-256 hash of each token, never the plaintext** — a stolen copy of this database doesn't hand over a live credential the way today's plaintext `auth_token` file does. `create_device_session(device_name)` returns the real token exactly once; there's no function anywhere that can recover it afterward. `verify_device_session(token)` checks for a matching, non-revoked, non-expired row and slides the expiry forward another 90 days on every successful use — an actively-used device never needs manual renewal, an abandoned one lapses on its own. `revoke_device_session`/`list_device_sessions` round out real device management (no UI built yet, not in scope here).

  **Bootstrap uses the existing trust model rather than inventing a new one**: the new `POST /auth/register-device` route is gated by today's real `AUTH_TOKEN` directly, not the new mechanism — minting a first device session can't itself require a device session to already exist. Possessing today's one shared secret is what lets you mint a new, distinct per-device credential going forward — the same trust boundary that exists today, just a better starting point than it currently has.

  **`verify_token()` and the WebSocket's own inline check both become additive, not replaced**: a request now passes if it presents either the unchanged `AUTH_TOKEN` (today's real desktop/iOS apps keep working, unmodified, indefinitely — no Swift changes at all) *or* a valid device session. No existing route's protection is weakened; a request satisfies one of the two checks, never neither. All 44 `Depends(verify_token)` call sites are untouched — only the function's internals changed, not its signature or any caller.

  Verified end to end against the real `auth.db` (this is meant to become real, permanent infrastructure, not a disposable test replica — every test session was revoked afterward, confirmed via direct query, leaving no active test credentials behind): registered a device and confirmed the stored row holds a 64-character hash, never the plaintext token; confirmed the new token verifies and a made-up one doesn't; confirmed today's real, completely unmodified `AUTH_TOKEN` still passes through the exact same `auth.py` mechanism; revoked the device and confirmed its token stopped working immediately; backdated a session's expiry directly and confirmed it's correctly rejected; called `register_device` directly with a wrong token, an empty token, and the real token, confirming the gate works in both directions; confirmed `verify_token()` itself accepts both the real `AUTH_TOKEN` and a real device session, and correctly rejects a bogus token and an already-revoked one. No Swift changes — desktop and iOS keep using today's token exactly as they do now.

- **Stage 8 prep: the Mac Local Node's first real capability, formalized — "Is the Mac Local Node online?" is now an answerable, real fact instead of a missing one.** Stage 8 bundles four different Mac-only capabilities (AppleScript Calendar, the Trading Division bridge, the HF Markets/MT5 bridge, Shadow Mode) behind a formal Local Node boundary — picked Shadow Mode first, same "one thing at a time" discipline as every prior stage: it's the simplest, already gated by the auth Stage 7 just built, and touches nothing with real external side effects. Unlike Stages 4-7, this one isn't blocked on a nonexistent cloud endpoint, so it's the first of these proof-of-concept passes to include a real, rebuilt-and-relaunched Swift change alongside the backend piece, not backend-only.

  **The real gap this closes**: `POST /activity/log` only fires on an actual app-switch event — confirmed in `ActivityTracker.swift`'s own comments, event-driven, not polled. A Mac that's on but idle (no app-switching) looks identical, from the backend's point of view, to a Mac that's off. Section 24 of the original spec names this exact fact — *"Is the Mac Local Node online?"* — as something the system should be able to answer; today it couldn't.

  **New `app_state.local_node_last_seen_at`** (`backend/app/db.py`), added via the exact same `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN` migration pattern already used for `last_gmail_sync_at`/`credits_exhausted_since` — nullable, since "never seen" is a real, honest state. New `POST /local-node/heartbeat` route, and `/activity/log`'s existing handler now also touches the same timestamp (a real activity post is real evidence of liveness too, free signal alongside the dedicated heartbeat) — neither route's existing request/response shape changed. `/status` gains a `local_node: {last_seen_at, online}` field, `online` computed as "seen within the last 150 seconds" (2.5x the 60-second heartbeat interval, tolerating one missed beat without flapping).

  **`desktop/Sources/PCorpOS/ActivityTracker.swift`**: `start()` gains a periodic loop, independent of the existing `NSWorkspace` app-switch observer, POSTing to `/local-node/heartbeat` every 60 seconds for as long as the app runs — reusing the exact same `URLSession.shared`/`BackendHost.url(path:)` pattern the file's own activity-post already uses, no new networking pattern introduced.

  **A real bug found live during verification, not shipped blind**: the first `/status` test showed `online: false` immediately after a fresh heartbeat — backwards. Root cause: SQLite's `datetime('now')` (used to write the timestamp) returns UTC, while the comparison used Python's local-time `datetime.now()` — on any machine not already at UTC+0, freshly-written timestamps looked hours old. Fixed by computing naive-UTC "now" (`datetime.now(timezone.utc).replace(tzinfo=None)`) to compare against the naive-UTC string SQLite actually stores, re-verified correct in both directions (fresh heartbeat → online, backdated timestamp → offline).

  Verified end to end against the real, live-running app — not just standalone scripts, since there was no nonexistent-cloud-endpoint reason to stop short this time: rebuilt via `build_app.sh`, restarted the real `launchd`-managed backend (picked up automatically via `KeepAlive`) and relaunched the real frontend, then confirmed via the real backend log that `/local-node/heartbeat` requests actually arrived repeatedly on schedule with zero app-switching in between, and confirmed the live `/status` endpoint reported `local_node.online: true` with a fresh timestamp. Checked the backend log for any new errors from the restart — found none; a handful of old `address already in use` tracebacks were confirmed to be from a much earlier restart cycle in this same session's log history, unrelated to today's changes. Cleaned up the one test row this verification wrote to the real `activity_log` table. `/activity/log`'s pre-existing behavior confirmed unchanged aside from the new side effect. iOS untouched — Shadow Mode is desktop-only by design.

- **Stage 8, second Local Node capability: AppleScript Calendar now identifies itself as unavailable rather than silently failing an approval nobody could act on.** Continuing the same "one capability at a time" discipline as Shadow Mode's liveness signal — Calendar is the most consequential of the three remaining, since `system_calendar.py`'s own docstring already names the real risk: it runs AppleScript directly from the backend process itself, "no bridge needed, since the backend already runs locally on Josh's own Mac" — true today, false the instant the backend runs anywhere else, and one of the two backend-side `osascript` calls the audit flagged as having zero isolation boundary.

  **Deliberately did not build a network bridge** — there's no remote cloud backend yet to call back into, so a callback mechanism would be speculative, untestable design against nothing real, exactly what the migration spec's own engineering standard warns against. What's real today, independent of any migration: Calendar.app can genuinely be unreachable (closed, a revoked Automation permission, a wedged state), and that failure is invisible until a write is actually attempted and fails with a raw AppleScript error — a live gap against the spec's own Section 26 requirement that anything Mac-required "must clearly identify itself... rather than silently failing."

  **New `check_calendar_available()`** (`backend/app/system_calendar.py`) reuses the exact same `_run_osascript()` helper every real calendar operation in this file already depends on, with a minimal read-only probe (`get name of calendars`) — proves the same path `create_event`/`update_event`/`delete_event` actually need, not a weaker proxy for it. `/status`'s `local_node` gains a `capabilities` object (`calendar: {available}` for now, structured to let Trading Division/HF Markets land the same way later without restructuring) — a live check each call, not cached, since it's a ~1s local subprocess call and `/status` isn't on any hot polling path today.

  **`calendar_tools.py`'s three write tools** (`propose_create_calendar_event`/`propose_update_calendar_event`/`propose_delete_calendar_event`) now check availability **before** requesting approval, not after — no point sending Josh a real approval card for something already known to be impossible to execute. Unavailable returns a clear, honestly-labeled message instead of proceeding to the approval flow at all; `list_calendar_events` (reads `calendar_db.py`'s cache, never touches AppleScript directly) is untouched.

  Verified directly against the real Mac, not simulated: `check_calendar_available()` correctly returns `True` (Calendar.app genuinely reachable here); a deliberately invalid AppleScript string correctly returns `False` with a real captured syntax error, proving the underlying plumbing actually distinguishes success from failure rather than always returning `True`. Confirmed live via the real running backend that `/status` reports `local_node.capabilities.calendar.available: true`. Proved the pre-approval gate is real, not just correctly-worded, with a websocket stand-in that raises if `send_text`/`receive_text` is ever called: monkeypatched unavailable, called all three write tools, got the clear message back with zero websocket interaction attempted for any of them — then restored the real check and confirmed a genuinely-available Calendar correctly proceeds past the gate to the real approval-request payload, intercepted deliberately before completing a live round-trip (no real approval was sent to Josh's actual running app during this test).

- **The real cutover: Joshua created a Render account, provisioned a real Postgres instance, and `personal.db` became the first domain to genuinely run on cloud infrastructure instead of the Mac — not a test, not a rehearsal.** Real research (not assumed) found Render's own Starter tier (~$7/mo Postgres + ~$7/mo web service, $14/mo total) fits P Corp's actual data size fine despite generic pricing guides calling that tier "inadequate for production" — that guidance assumes a typical multi-user app; the entire real dataset across all 12 domains is a few MB, smallest table aside, `activity_log` tops out around 2,500 rows. Also researched and ruled out Supabase Edge Functions as a compute host entirely: a hard 150-400 second execution ceiling is fundamentally incompatible with a persistent WebSocket chat connection and an always-on 15-minute scheduler loop — real information that shaped the recommendation, not guessed.

  **The real Stage 3 migration ran for the first time against actual cloud infrastructure**, not the local Postgres stand-in: all 12 domains, all 38 tables, backed up and replicated into the real Render database, independently cross-checked directly against it (`pg_tables` count matched exactly) — the exact same tooling built and proven locally earlier this session, now doing the real thing it was always meant for. The per-domain `.bak-<timestamp>` files from this real run were deliberately kept, not pruned like every earlier local-test-replica run — this is a genuine backup of a real cutover, not disposable test output.

  **`DATA_BACKEND=postgres` was then actually flipped live** on the real running backend — the first genuinely-live piece of Mac independence in this entire project. Proved it wasn't just configured but actually active by writing a distinguishing test row directly into the cloud database (never touching local SQLite) and confirming the real running `/personal/dashboard` route served it back — then reverted the switch once, per Joshua's own explicit request, to keep tonight's result at "safely copied" rather than "live" while he decided whether to actually go ahead — then flipped it again for real once he did.

  **A real bug the live cutover itself exposed, not caught by any of this session's earlier isolated testing**: `personal_tools.py`'s `build_personal_block()` — injected into Frank's own system prompt on every single turn — called `summarize()` with no database connection at all, meaning Frank's own live awareness of his goals and habits stayed pinned to SQLite even after real writes started landing in Postgres instead. Every earlier verification pass tested the dashboard route and the write tools in isolation; none of them exercised "write a goal, then check what Frank himself sees" end to end, which is exactly the gap a real cutover surfaces and isolated testing can't. Fixed by computing `postgres_conn` once per WebSocket connection in `main.py` (not per-turn — `app.state.postgres_conn` never changes for the life of the process) and threading it through both the system-prompt build and the tool-dispatch branch that already had it, removing a since-redundant duplicate computation. Also corrected a stale docstring on `summarize()` that had claimed it wasn't wired into the system prompt at all — it clearly was, `main.py`'s own turn-building code proves it, the comment just hadn't been updated when that wiring was originally added.

  Verified directly: inserted a distinguishing goal into the real cloud database only, confirmed `build_personal_block(postgres_conn)` includes it while `build_personal_block()` with no connection correctly does not (proving the two really do read different sources, not just returning the same cached thing twice) — cleaned up immediately after. Restarted the real backend to load the fix, confirmed clean startup and a correctly-empty live dashboard afterward, matching the real (all soft-deleted) state of the actual data. All other 11 domains remain completely unaffected — still reading and writing local SQLite exactly as before; this cutover is scoped to `personal.db` alone.

- **First real Render deployment code: the backend can now run as a cloud web service, not just read/write cloud data.** A health check earlier tonight gave an honest "no" to whether the iPhone works independently of the Mac — the real cutover moved `personal.db`'s data to Postgres, but the backend *process* itself has only ever run on this Mac, reachable over Tailscale/loopback either way. Making that genuinely false needs the backend deployed somewhere else, which needs real code first: `backend/app/main.py`'s `run()` only ever bound `127.0.0.1` (optionally plus a specific Tailscale IP) — `0.0.0.0` was deliberately ruled out in `SECURITY.md` for the Mac-hosted threat model, since it would expose the backend to anyone else on the same Wi-Fi. That reasoning doesn't hold for an isolated cloud container, where `0.0.0.0` is exactly correct — Render's own router controls real external exposure, and Stage 7's real auth already gates every route regardless. Confirmed via real research, not assumed: Render requires binding `0.0.0.0` and the port it assigns dynamically via a `PORT` env var, or every request 502s.

  **A second real gap, found by tracing what would actually happen, not caught by any existing test**: the hang-watchdog (`_start_hang_watchdog`, added earlier this session after a real incident) hardcodes its health-check URL to `127.0.0.1:{BACKEND_PORT}` — in cloud mode the real bound port is whatever Render assigned via `$PORT`, not the constant `8731`, so the watchdog would check the wrong port, always see it as unreachable, and force-kill the process in a crash loop. Its own docstring already says it exists because `launchd`'s restart-on-crash doesn't catch a hang that never crashes — Render supervises its own processes the same way, so the fix is not running this watchdog in cloud mode at all, not teaching it to guess the right port.

  **A third real gap, needed just to test any of this**: `auth.py`'s token is generated once and persisted to a local file — on Render's ephemeral filesystem (wiped every restart/redeploy), a fresh, unknowable token would generate on every cold start, with no way to learn it in advance to actually verify a deployment works.

  **`run()` gains a cloud-mode branch**, checked first via `os.environ.get("PORT")` before any Mac-hosted branching, so the two are mutually exclusive by construction: if set, binds `0.0.0.0:$PORT` and returns immediately, skipping the hang-watchdog entirely; if unset (true for every real deployment today), execution falls through to the exact same Tailscale/loopback logic as before, completely untouched. **`auth.py`'s `get_or_create_token()`** gains an optional `AUTH_TOKEN` env-var check ahead of the existing file-based read/generate path — genuinely correct for any real cloud deployment regardless of testing needs, not just a testing convenience, and a pure no-op on the Mac today since that env var is unset there.

  Scoped deliberately as an **isolated test**: nothing real points at this deployment yet, and nothing needs to — `BackendHost` on both Swift apps stays pointed at the Mac. That matters because Render's ephemeral filesystem would silently lose all data on every restart for the 11 domains still on local SQLite (only `personal.db` has moved to Postgres) — a real risk if this were a real client's actual backend, harmless only because nothing real is at stake here yet.
