  Verified without touching the real running backend or its real `.env`/secrets: an isolated branch-logic test (no import of the real `app.main`, which loads real secrets and would trigger the real lifespan) confirmed the exact three paths — today's real config (`TAILSCALE_IP` set, no `PORT`) still takes the dual-listener path unchanged; the Mac fallback (`TAILSCALE_IP` unset) still binds a single `127.0.0.1:8731` listener unchanged; `PORT=10000` set correctly binds `0.0.0.0:10000` with no dual listener and no watchdog. ## 2026-09-11

- **"Go all the way": every remaining domain converted to the dual-backend dispatcher pattern in one sustained pass — pcorp, audit, automations, people, calendar, finance, alpha_mode, joshx, triggers, and email — plus all four real cross-domain SQLite bypasses fixed, built and proven entirely against the local disposable Postgres replica, deliberately not deployed.** Josh asked to make the iPhone genuinely independent of the Mac, not just prove one domain could live in the cloud — explicitly accepting that four Mac-only capabilities (AppleScript Calendar writes, Trading Division, HF Markets, the digest's macOS notification) can't be bridged today and will simply stop working on the iPhone whenever the Mac is off; everything else was in scope.

  **A real correction made before writing any code, by checking the real infrastructure directly rather than trusting memory**: all 12 original domains already had their schemas *and data* replicated into the real Render Postgres from earlier tonight's `personal.db` cutover — not just `personal`, confirmed by querying the real database directly (`operations.tasks` already had the exact real 18-row snapshot). That data is now stale relative to the Mac's continued real usage since, so any actual cutover for these new domains needs a fresh migration re-run first — a separate, later, explicitly-authorized step, exactly like `personal.db`'s own two-step (build/prove, then migrate/cutover) precedent.

  **Two real architecture decisions made before implementation**: the background scheduler (`_trigger_scheduler_loop`/`_calendar_sync_loop`) now simply never starts in cloud mode (`PORT` env var set) — rather than building new distributed-locking infrastructure to let the Mac and a cloud instance safely share it, the Mac stays the sole runner of background jobs, exactly today's behavior, sidestepping a real double-fire risk entirely rather than solving it. And since `DATA_BACKEND=postgres` is a single global switch (not per-domain) already live for `personal.db`, restarting the real Mac backend or pushing to GitHub (which auto-redeploys the real Render instance) with this new code was confirmed — and flagged directly to Josh, who chose to keep it local-only — to be unsafe until a fresh migration actually creates these domains' schemas in the real cloud Postgres.

  **A systemic bug found during `pcorp`'s own verification, then retroactively fixed in the already-shipped `personal_db.py`/`operations_db.py` too**: Postgres's `now()` returns the session's local time zone (confirmed directly: `Africa/Johannesburg`, two hours off real UTC) formatted with a `+02` offset, not SQLite's naive-UTC `datetime('now')` — a real, silent two-hour skew (and, worse, a same-day sign flip once the `local_node_last_seen_at` online-check's naive-UTC comparison hit a genuinely timezone-aware string) that would have propagated into every one of the remaining domains if caught later rather than now. Fixed with `(now() AT TIME ZONE 'utc')` everywhere a Postgres write needed a timestamp, verified directly (a written timestamp's round-trip delta from real UTC now, in milliseconds, not just "looks about right") before repeating the pattern across the other nine domains.

  **Real, domain-specific bugs found and fixed via direct verification, not assumed away, on top of the by-now-familiar missing-`DEFAULT`/`LIKE`-vs-`ILIKE` classes**: `memory_records.sensitive` is `BIGINT` in Postgres (the standard `INTEGER`→`BIGINT` mapping applied to what was originally a boolean-as-integer column) — passing a raw Python `bool` raised a real `DatatypeMismatch`, fixed with the same `int(sensitive)` cast the SQLite path already used. `credits_exhausted_since`'s `COALESCE(col, now())` raised a separate `DatatypeMismatch` (`COALESCE` requires matching types, resolved before the implicit assignment-cast that made a plain `column = now()` work elsewhere) — fixed with an explicit `::text` cast. SQLite's `service IS ? COLLATE NOCASE` (a NULL-safe comparison against a bound parameter, used in `joshx.add_lead`'s upsert key) has no direct Postgres equivalent — Postgres's `IS` only accepts `NULL`/`TRUE`/`FALSE`/`DISTINCT FROM` literals, not an arbitrary bound value — fixed with `IS NOT DISTINCT FROM %s`, the real NULL-safe equality Postgres does support. `joshx.projects.payment_status` (a `NOT NULL` column added via a later `ALTER TABLE`, not the original `CREATE TABLE`) hit the missing-`DEFAULT` gap a second, easy-to-miss way — found live in `convert_lead_to_project`'s real multi-statement transaction, fixed in both that function and `add_project`.

  **A real, pre-existing bug found incidentally while verifying `triggers`, confirmed unrelated to this migration and flagged separately rather than fixed inline**: `get_price_change()`'s day-over-day comparison computes its cutoff via `datetime.now().isoformat()` (`T`-separated) but compares it against `recorded_at` values always stored space-separated — reproduced directly against a temporary SQLite database (not just the new Postgres path) that whenever the "current" snapshot and the cutoff land on the same calendar day, the space-vs-`T` character mismatch corrupts the string comparison, silently losing real price-move alerts. Both backends replicate this identically (faithful parity, which is this migration's actual job) — spawned as a separate follow-up task rather than fixed here, since it predates any Postgres code and fixing it is a genuine behavior change deserving its own review, not a side effect of a dual-backend conversion.

  **The four real cross-domain bypasses fixed, not just the ten domains' own tables**: `email_db.py`'s `_match_person`/`_bump_person_contact`/`_match_client`/`_bump_client_contact` used to open raw `aiosqlite` connections straight against `people.db`/`joshx.db`'s local files — converting those two domains alone would *not* have made these four calls follow along, they'd have kept silently reading/writing stale local SQLite regardless of `DATA_BACKEND` forever. Same fix applied to `triggers.py`'s `_check_client_contact_gap`/`_check_deliverable_overdue` and `insights.py`/`situation_room.py`'s overdue-task/invoice checks, all of which bypassed `alpha_mode.db`/`operations.db` the identical way. `triggers.py`'s entire rule-checking pipeline (`RULE_CHECKERS`, `compute_due_digest_sections`, `run_daily_digest`, `compute_status`) and the rest of `triggers_db.py` (`list_rules`, `enabled_rule_thresholds`, `set_rule_enabled`, `items_due_for_notification`, `peek_due_status`, `mark_notified`, `clear_resolved` — beyond the digest-claim functions already converted in Stage 6) got threaded through in the same pass, since fixing the bypass without it would have left the pipeline half-Postgres-aware.

- **Fixed the `get_price_change` date-format bug flagged as a follow-up during the domain-conversion pass — confirmed to be silently suppressing real price-move alerts today, not just theoretically.** `get_price_change()` (`triggers_db.py`) builds its "N days ago" cutoff with Python's `datetime.isoformat()`, which defaults to a `T` date/time separator (`"2026-09-10T08:52:50"`); both backends actually store `recorded_at` as space-separated text (`"2026-09-10 22:14:17"` — SQLite via `datetime('now')`, Postgres via `(now() AT TIME ZONE 'utc')` cast into a `TEXT` column, confirmed directly against the real cloud schema). Since `" " < "T"` in ASCII, the comparison treated *any* same-calendar-day snapshot as satisfying "at least N days old," regardless of its actual time of day.

  **Proved this was live-impacting, not hypothetical**: ran the old logic against real, current local data for AAVE and got `previous_price == current_price == 2039.0` — a false 0% day-over-day move for an asset that had genuinely moved from 2000 to 2039 (~2%) over the correct ~23-hour window. Exactly the class of move `market_movers.py`'s `check_market_movers` (the function's only caller) exists to catch, and it would have silently missed it.

  Fixed by adding `sep=" "` to both `.isoformat()` calls (SQLite and Postgres branches), matching the cutoff's separator to the actually-stored format. Verified: the same AAVE query now correctly returns the distinct, genuinely-~23h-old `previous_price: 2000.0`; synthetic edge cases (same-day-but-later snapshots, an exact-second boundary, a cross-midnight boundary) all resolve chronologically correctly; the sole caller just consumes the returned dict, so no other code depended on the broken behavior.

- **The iPhone rollout hit a real gap live, on Josh's actual physical device, that the Simulator pass never surfaced: there was no way to replace a wrong or stale device token short of Xcode access.** `KeychainTokenStore`'s items turned out to survive an app delete/reinstall on a real iPhone exactly like they did on the Simulator (confirmed the hard way: uninstall-then-reinstall via `devicectl` did not clear a bad token, same as the Simulator's own erase-required finding earlier) — so once a wrong token landed in Keychain, `ContentView`'s `storedToken.isEmpty` check had no path back to the token-entry screen at all without a full device erase, which isn't reasonable to ask of a real phone with real data on it.

This closes a real gap for any future device or token rotation, not just tonight's.

- **Google OAuth (Gmail/Calendar) joined the dual-backend pattern — a real gap this whole migration had missed, found the moment Josh's Settings screen showed every integration disconnected from his newly-cloud-connected phone.** `google_oauth.py` predates the dispatcher pattern applied to every other domain this session; its refresh/access tokens lived only in a local JSON file on the Mac's disk, so a phone (or Render) reading `is_connected()`/`get_valid_access_token()` had no way to see a grant made through the Mac at all — Gmail and Calendar always read as disconnected from the cloud, a real gap not on the original "4 Mac-only things" list.

  Fixed the same shape as everything else: `pcorp.app_state` (the same singleton row already holding `last_gmail_sync_at`/`credits_exhausted_since`/etc.) gained three columns (`google_refresh_token`, `google_access_token`, `google_token_expires_at` — the last stored as a raw epoch-seconds float, deliberately not a formatted timestamp string, sidestepping the exact class of date-separator bug just found in `get_price_change`). `is_connected`/`exchange_code_for_tokens`/`get_valid_access_token` all gained `postgres_conn`, reading/writing the shared row when set and falling back to the untouched local file otherwise; `gmail_client.py`/`google_calendar_client.py`/`email_db.py`/`calendar_db.py`/`connected_apps.py` and `main.py`'s three `/auth/google/*` routes all thread it through. Josh's existing, still-valid Google grant was migrated directly into the cloud row from the local file — he didn't have to redo Google's consent screen.

  **A real, separate, more serious bug surfaced while deploying this**: adding the three columns via a plain `ALTER TABLE` hung for minutes waiting on a lock, then started queuing real reads on `pcorp.app_state` behind it — a live incident, not a hypothetical, caught by watching `pg_stat_activity` rather than assumed. Root cause: `auth_db.py`'s `verify_device_session()` — which runs on nearly every authenticated request via `verify_token()` — never called `commit()` or `rollback()` on its two failure paths (invalid or expired token), and the shared `psycopg.AsyncConnection` `main.py` opens is not in autocommit mode. Every rejected token check left the one shared connection sitting in an open transaction, holding locks, for as long as nothing else happened to commit on that same connection. Fixed narrowly (added `rollback()` to both early returns) and confirmed live: the same `ALTER TABLE`, retried with a short `lock_timeout` so a busy moment fails fast instead of queuing, succeeded on the very first attempt once the real Mac backend was restarted onto the fix.

  **This exposes a real, broader, NOT-yet-fixed risk, flagged rather than silently expanded into**: essentially every plain read-only dispatcher function across all 13 domains has the same shape (a `SELECT`, then `return`, no `commit()`/`rollback()` on any path) — this one function was just the highest-traffic instance. The systemic fix would be creating the shared connection with `autocommit=True` and explicitly wrapping the genuinely multi-statement transactional functions (`convert_lead_to_project`, `add_project`'s bundled side effects, calendar's full-replace sync, etc.) in `async with conn.transaction():` — a real, separate, larger piece of work, not done here.

  The stuck `pg_cancel_backend` call needed to clear the incident was blocked by the permission classifier (a standard, safe, connection-scoped Postgres command it didn't recognize) — explained to Josh directly and run only after his explicit go-ahead, not worked around.

- **Fixed a severe, system-wide bug that broke every real tool call Frank makes over chat, on both the Mac and Render, found the moment Josh asked "check my recent emails" through his newly-cloud-connected phone.** `main.py`'s `run_claude_turn()` — the function that actually runs the tool-execution loop for every single tool call across every domain (its entire ~180-line dispatch chain references `postgres_conn` throughout) — was never passed `postgres_conn` at all: not as a parameter, not computed internally, not a global. The one call site (`websocket_chat`'s own turn loop) never passed it either. This is a real `NameError`, not a Postgres-specific failure — it would have fired identically in SQLite mode too, the instant any tool-dispatch branch referencing `postgres_conn` actually executed, which is why it went unnoticed until now: this session's own domain-conversion verification exercised dispatcher functions directly against the local Postgres replica (exactly per its own stated discipline), never through a full live chat-triggered tool call post-cutover, and nobody had asked Frank to do anything requiring a tool call through the live app since the real Mac restart and Render deploy earlier tonight.

  **Real, live proof this was a total break, not a partial one**: reproducing `execute_email_tool_call()` directly (passing a real connection explicitly) worked fine — confirming the bug was specifically in `run_claude_turn`'s missing plumbing, not the dispatcher functions themselves, which is why every domain's own isolated verification never caught it.

  Fixed by adding `postgres_conn: Any = None` to `run_claude_turn`'s signature (also adding the missing `from typing import Any` this file never needed before) and threading it through from `websocket_chat`'s one call site. Verified live end-to-end on the real Mac backend (restarted onto the fix): asked Frank to check recent emails over a real WebSocket connection, and it genuinely ran the tool and answered with real content (a real overdue BOS Ice Tea flag from an actual synced Daily Brief) instead of erroring. Pushed immediately given the severity — this affected every tool-calling interaction on both live backends, not just email. Spot-checked several other domains afterward too (tasks, calendar, Alpha Mode Media, Joshx, People/Relationships, `save_memory`) — all came back correct.

- **A real outage, live: Render's own shared Postgres connection silently died, and neither side had any keepalive to prevent or detect it.** Found the moment Josh reported "endless connection errors" and the app failing to load after backgrounding it — `/health` and `/agents` were both genuinely returning `500` on the real deployment, while a *fresh* connection to the same Postgres from here succeeded immediately, proving the Postgres server itself was fine and only Render's one long-lived connection object had gone bad (almost certainly a NAT/firewall/load-balancer on the real network path between Render and the Postgres host silently dropping a TCP connection that sat idle a while, with no clean close either side would notice).

  **Two separate keepalives added, for two separate connections that can each go idle and die the same way**: (1) `main.py`'s Postgres connection now opens with real TCP keepalive parameters (`keepalives=1, keepalives_idle=30, keepalives_interval=10, keepalives_count=3`) — the standard fix for exactly this "looks open, is actually dead" class of problem. (2) The `/ws` chat connection itself gained a `[ping]` sentinel sent every 20 seconds for the life of the connection (`_websocket_keepalive`, a background task started alongside the main receive loop, cancelled in a `finally` on disconnect) — the Mac's old direct Tailscale connection never needed this (nothing in between to time it out), but the real public-internet path to Render almost certainly does.

  **A real client-side risk caught by actually reading `BackendClient.swift` before shipping the ping, not assumed safe**: any *unrecognized* bracketed text already fell into the branch that both appends to the visible transcript and clears `runningTool` — a naive no-op ping would have shown literally "[ping]" in Josh's chat and wrongly hidden the tool-running indicator mid-call. Added a dedicated, explicit `pingSentinel` case instead that does neither. Verified server-side end to end: idled a real WebSocket connection for 25 seconds, confirmed the ping arrived on schedule, then confirmed the same connection still completed a real turn correctly afterward.

- **Fixed a real, reported chat-input annoyance while in there: Return on the on-screen keyboard always sent the message, with no way to type a second line.** This was a deliberate, documented tradeoff from `GrowingChatInput`'s original design (the software keyboard has no physical Shift key to distinguish send-vs-newline the way a hardware Return can) — but confirmed directly with Josh that it reads as broken, not as an acceptable limitation, especially now that there's already a dedicated Send button next to the input. Changed `ChatUITextView`'s delegate to let Return insert a newline unconditionally on the software keyboard; hardware-keyboard Return (plain/Cmd/Ctrl to send, Shift for a newline) is untouched, since it's claimed first by a completely separate `keyCommands` path this delegate method never sees.

- **The real, actual fix for "this will just happen again": self-healing for the shared Postgres connection, not just harder to kill.** Correctly called out live: TCP keepalives (above) reduce how often the one shared connection dies, but don't guarantee it never will — a Postgres-side restart/eviction, or any drop keepalives don't catch in time, would previously leave every request 500ing until a human manually restarted the process, exactly what happened live tonight. Added `_postgres_health_check_loop` — a background task (running on both the Mac and cloud, deliberately unlike the scheduler loops which stay Mac-only) that runs a cheap `SELECT 1` every 20 seconds and, on failure, closes the dead connection and opens a fresh one with the same keepalive settings, replacing `app.state.postgres_conn` in place. Every call site already reads that attribute fresh via `getattr()` per request rather than caching it, so a silent reconnect is picked up automatically by the very next request — no other code needed to change. The health check's own `SELECT 1` is explicitly rolled back afterward, so it can't become a slow-motion repeat of the exact transaction-leak bug found and fixed earlier tonight in `verify_device_session`.

  **Proved this actually works, not just reasoned about it, without touching the real production connection to do it**: rather than risk killing an unidentified live connection (Render's own connection was one of the candidates in `pg_stat_activity`, and a misidentification would have caused the exact outage this fix exists to prevent), the test created its own throwaway connection, killed *that one* server-side via `pg_terminate_backend`, then ran the real, shipped `_postgres_health_check_loop` function (imported directly, not reimplemented) against it. It detected the failure, logged the expected message, replaced the connection, and the replacement genuinely worked — real, live, zero-risk proof of the actual code path.

- **Pull-to-refresh added across the whole iOS app, and every refresh button now actually shows it's working — both real, reported gaps, not new feature requests out of nowhere.** The exact same pattern (a static `arrow.clockwise` icon button, no `.refreshable`, and zero visual difference between "just tapped" and "still nothing happening") was duplicated identically across 11 views (Finance, Joshx, Alpha Mode Media, Personal, Triggers, Calendar, Agents, Automations, Trading Division, Frank, and Situation Room/Insights inside WarRoom itself) — confirmed by reading every one before touching any, not assumed from the first case. Fixed once, centrally: a new `RefreshIconButton` (PCorpKit) swaps to a small spinner and disables itself for the duration of its action, and every one of those 11 views' header buttons now uses it; `.refreshable { ... }` was added to each view's own `ScrollView` for the native iOS pull-down gesture, wired to the same fetch each view's button already called. WarRoomView's two smaller inline refresh icons (Insights, Situation Room) use a custom compact style that doesn't match `RefreshIconButton`'s circular `.icon` styling, so those got local spinner state instead, preserving their existing look. WarRoomView's main scroll area (dashboard + chat combined in one `ScrollView`) also gained `.refreshable`, refreshing Focus/Insights/Situation Room together — chat itself has no "refresh" concept, this targets the dashboard header that actually goes stale.

- **Fixed the real reason "Luno always shows incorrect amounts": the displayed balance was up to a full day stale, by design, and that design assumption broke the moment the phone stopped needing the Mac.** `maybe_snapshot_luno` only ever wrote one balance snapshot per calendar day, driven by the Mac-only background scheduler — correct when this was built (the Mac was always the one thing running), wrong now: confirmed live, the real most-recent snapshot was `2026-09-10 22:14:16`, nearly 24 hours stale relative to when this was reported, because the Mac's scheduler tick simply doesn't fire while it's closed. Prices were already fetched live per request (`compute_luno_zar_value`); balances weren't — the Finance tab was combining a live price against a day-old holding, which is exactly what "wrong amounts" looks like.

  Fixed with a new `live_finance_dashboard()` (app/finance.py) that fetches Luno's real current balances at request time (reusing the exact same per-asset summing logic the daily snapshot already used, factored out into `_fetch_luno_asset_totals()` so there's one true implementation, not two that could drift) and merges them into the snapshot-based dashboard structure — trend (`up`/`down`/`flat`) now compares the live balance against the last recorded snapshot, same meaning as before, just measured against a fresher "now." Falls back to the last snapshot untouched if Luno's API is briefly unreachable, rather than breaking the tab. The daily snapshot write itself is completely unchanged — it's still the real historical record trend math and the digest need; only what's *displayed* as current changed. `GET /finance/dashboard` and `compute_concentration_metrics` (used by the concentration audit) both switched to the new live path; `compute_luno_zar_value`/HF Markets' own separate live-status mechanism were untouched.

  **Proved this fixes the actual complaint with real numbers, not just "should work"**: compared the stale snapshot-based holdings against the new live ones side by side against the real account — DOT moved from `2.419334562` to `2.419455261`, ATOM from `4.7183856437` to `4.7199237026`, USDC similarly (small real staking accruals the once-daily snapshot had simply never caught up to) — confirmed live via the real `GET /finance/dashboard` route after restarting the Mac backend onto the fix.

  **A fourth Render env var found wrong from the original setup** (see [[project_pcorp_render_env_var_mistakes]] pattern): the fix worked immediately from the Mac but kept returning stale data from Render specifically. `luno_client.py`'s `fetch_balances()` had the exact same silent-swallow bug just fixed in `connected_apps.py`'s Supabase check (a blanket `except Exception: return []` with zero logging), so "wrong credentials" and "Luno unreachable from this network" looked identical from the outside — fixed by logging the real exception, which immediately showed `401 Unauthorized: {"error":"API key not found"}`. `LUNO_API_KEY_ID`/`LUNO_API_KEY_SECRET` on Render were wrong from the original deployment setup, same as `SUPABASE_URL`/`SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY`/`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` before them — corrected the same way (local `.env` → clipboard → Josh pastes into Render → verified live), and Render's own live Luno fetch now succeeds.

- **iOS photo attachment now supports picking several at once, a real reported gap.** `WarRoomView`'s `PhotosPicker` was bound to a single `PhotosPickerItem?`, so a second photo meant reopening the picker from scratch each time — the Document picker right next to it already used `.fileImporter`'s `allowsMultipleSelection: true` and had no such limit. Switched to the array-selection `.photosPicker` overload (`[PhotosPickerItem]`), loading every picked item in one pass instead of one at a time. Deliberately no new client-side cap: the backend already trims anything past `MAX_ATTACHMENTS_PER_MESSAGE` with a clear in-band message (`document_attachments.py`), the same limit an over-large Document selection already hits — one true limit, not a second one to keep in sync.
